A converse to Cartan’s Theorem B: The extension property for real analytic and Nash sets
José F. Fernando, Riccardo Ghiloni

Abstract
. In 1957 Cartan proved his celebrated Theorem B and deduced that if Ω ⊂ ℝ n \Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n} is an open set and X X is a coherent real analytic subset of Ω \Omega , then X X has the analytic extension property, that is, each real analytic function on X X extends to a real analytic function on Ω \Omega . So far, the converse implication in its full generality remains unproven. As a matter of fact, in the literature only special cases of non-coherent real analytic sets X ⊂ Ω X\subset\Omega without the extension property appear, some of them due to Cartan himself: mainly real analytic sets X ⊂ Ω X\subset\Omega that have a visible ‘tail’, that is, X X has a non-pure dimensional irreducible analytic component Y Y such that the set of points of lower dimension of Y Y is visible inside X X (this means that there exists a point x ∈ Y x\in Y such that dim ( X x ) < dim ( Y ) \dim(X_{x})<\dim(Y) ). This kind of examples generated a general feeling that non-coherent real analytic sets without the analytic extension property may have visible ‘tails’. In this article we prove the converse implication: If a subset X X of Ω \Omega has the analytic extension property, then it is a coherent real analytic subset of Ω \Omega . Thus, the class of sets with the analytic extension property coincides with that of coherent real analytic sets. To that end we analyze the first cohomology group of the sheaf of zero ideals of X X . We extend the previous characterization to the Nash case, which is somehow more demanding, because of its finiteness properties and its disappointing behavior with respect to cohomology of sheaves of Nash function germs. Let Ω ⊂ ℝ n \Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n} be an open semialgebraic set and let X ⊂ Ω X\subset\Omega be a set. We prove that each Nash function on X X extends to a Nash function on Ω \Omega if and only if X ⊂ Ω X\subset\Omega is a coherent Nash set. The ‘if’ implication goes back to some celebrated results of Coste, Ruiz and Shiota, whereas as it happens in the analytic case the ‘only if’ implication has been treated only for Nash sets X X that have visible ‘tails’. If M ⊂ ℝ n M\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n} is a Nash manifold, 𝒞 ∞ {\mathcal{C}}^{\infty} semialgebraic functions on M M coincide with Nash functions on M M . As an application of our previous strategies, we confront the coherence of a Nash set X ⊂ Ω X\subset\Omega with the fact that each 𝒞 ∞ {\mathcal{C}}^{\infty} semialgebraic function on X X is a Nash function on X X . Namely, we prove that these two properties are equivalent for Nash sets. More generally, we provide a full characterization of the semialgebraic sets S ⊂ Ω S\subset\Omega for which 𝒞 ∞ {\mathcal{C}}^{\infty} semialgebraic functions on S S coincide with Nash functions on S S .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 14P15, 14P20, 58A07; Secondary 26B05, 26E10, 32C05, 32C07, 32E10

1.
Introduction

Let
\Omega
be an open subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and let
X
be a subset of
\Omega
. We denote
{\mathbb{N}}:=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
the set of natural numbers including
0
. A function
f:X\to{\mathbb{R}}
is called
(real) analytic
if for each point
a\in X
there exists an open neighborhood
U
of
a
in
\Omega
such that
f|_{X\cap U}
extends to an analytic function defined on
U
or, equivalently, if there exist an open neighborhood
U
of
a
in
\Omega
and a (real) power series
\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}}a_{\alpha}(x-a)^{\alpha}
that converges at the points of
U
and such that the value
f(x)
equals the sum of the convergent series
\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}}a_{\alpha}(x-a)^{\alpha}
in
{\mathbb{R}}
for each
x\in X\cap U
. We say that the set
X\subset\Omega
has the
analytic extension property
if each analytic function
f:X\to{\mathbb{R}}
extends to an analytic function defined on
\Omega
. A main initial difficulty here is the absence of analytic partitions of unity.

In this article we solve the following extension problem.

Problem 1.1
.

How can one decide whether a set
X\subset\Omega
has the analytic extension property?

This problem goes back to the 1957 article
[
C2
]
where Cartan proved several fundamental results of the theory of coherent sheaves, including the celebrated Theorem B. Recall that a set
X\subset\Omega
is
analytic
if it is closed in
\Omega
and for each
a\in X
there exist an open neighborhood
U
of
a
in
\Omega
and finitely many analytic functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}
defined on
U
whose common zero set is
X\cap U
. The set
X\subset\Omega
is
global analytic
if there exist finitely many analytic functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}
defined on the whole
\Omega
whose common zero set is
X
. There exist analytic sets which are not global analytic
[
C2
, §11]
. Global analytic sets were called
C
-analytic sets
by Whitney and Bruhat in
[
WB
]
, probably as an abbreviation for ‘Cartan real analytic sets’.

The global analyticity is an immediate necessary condition for the set
X\subset\Omega
to have the analytic extension property. We borrow the suitable strategy from the proof of
[
NT
, Thm.1]
. Namely, suppose the set
X\subset\Omega
has the analytic extension property, but it is not
C
-analytic. By
[
WB
, §6.Prop.7]
, the family of
C
-analytic subsets of
\Omega
is stable under arbitrary intersections. Thus, there exists the smallest
C
-analytic subset
Y
of
\Omega
that contains
X
. Suppose there exists a point
y\in Y\setminus X
. Consider the analytic function
f:X\to{\mathbb{R}},\ x\mapsto\frac{1}{\|x-y\|^{2}}
and an analytic function
F:\Omega\to{\mathbb{R}}
that extends
f
. Define the analytic function
G:\Omega\to{\mathbb{R}}
,
x\mapsto 1-F(x)\|x-y\|^{2}
. As
G
is identically zero on
X
, it vanishes identically on
\{y\}
, but this is a contradiction, because
y\in Y
and
G(y)=1
.

Global analyticity is still not enough to assure the analytic extension property. Consider Whitney’s umbrella
W:=\{(x,y,z)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}:y^{2}-zx^{2}=0\}
and the function

f:W\to{\mathbb{R}},\ (x,y,z)\mapsto\begin{cases}\frac{x}{z+1}&\text{if $(x,y,z)\neq(0,0,-1)$},\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Observe that
W\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
is a
C
-analytic set (even an algebraic set) and
f
is an analytic function on
W
. The latter assertion can be proven as follows. Whitney’s umbrella
W
decomposes as the union of the singular ruled surface
C:=\{x\neq 0,z=\frac{y^{2}}{x^{2}}\}\sqcup\{x=y=0,z\geq 0\}
(the cloth of the umbrella) and the
z
-semiaxis
H:=\{x=y=0,z\leq 0\}
(the handle of the umbrella). Observe that
C
meets
H
only at the origin
O
. As
p\in H\setminus\{O\}
and
f
vanishes identically around
p
, the function
f
is analytic on
W
, because
\{z+1=0\}\cap W=\{p\}
. Suppose now that
f
has an analytic extension
F
to
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. Observe that
(z+1)F(x,y,z)=x
on
W
. We can complexify the present geometric configuration using
[
WB
, §7]
and obtain an open neighborhood
V
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{3}
and a holomorphic function
F_{\mathbb{C}}:V\to{\mathbb{C}}
such that
F_{\mathbb{C}}
extends
F
and
(z+1)F_{\mathbb{C}}(x,y,z)=x
on
W_{\mathbb{C}}:=\{(x,y,z)\in V:y^{2}-zx^{2}=0\}
. Thus, if
\epsilon>0
is small enough, the point
q:=(\epsilon,\epsilon\sqrt{-1},-1)
belongs to
W_{\mathbb{C}}
and
0=0F_{\mathbb{C}}(q)=\epsilon
, which is a contradiction. Consequently, the analytic extension
F
of
f
does not exist.

The reader observes that this proof of the non-existence of
F
is based on the following fact: if we move on
W
from the cloth to the handle through the origin, the local (real) dimension of
W
decreases from
2
to
1
, but the local (complex) dimension of the complexification
W_{\mathbb{C}}
of
W
does not: it is constantly equal to
2
. This phenomenon of dimensional decrease allows to ‘hide in the handle of
W
’ the poles of the meromorphic function
(x,y,z)\mapsto\frac{1}{z+1}
after multiplying it by the analytic function
(x,y,z)\to x
, which vanishes identically on the handle but not on
W
. Thus, we obtain ‘by restriction to the real part
W
of
W_{\mathbb{C}}
an analytic function
f
on
W
with a unique ‘
hidden pole
’ at
p
. On the contrary, the meromorphic function
(x,y,z)\mapsto\frac{x}{z+1}
has poles on
W_{\mathbb{C}}
exactly at the points
\{(x,y,z)\in V:\ (x+\sqrt{-1}y)(x-\sqrt{-1}y)=0,z=-1\}
, which is the intersection with
W
of the union of two (complex) lines.

Cartan’s Theorem B provides a sufficient condition for a
C
-analytic set to have the analytic extension property. Such a sufficient condition is a local condition, the so-called
coherence
. Let
X\subset\Omega
be an analytic set and let
a\in X
. Consider an open neighborhood
U
of
a
in
\Omega
and analytic functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}
defined on
U
such that their common zero set is
X\cap U
. The functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}
are
good equations for
X
locally at
a
if each local analytic equation of
X
at
a
is a local analytic combination of
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}
. More precisely, if
f
is an analytic function defined on an open neighborhood
U_{1}
of
a
in
\Omega
that vanishes on
X\cap U_{1}
, there exist an open neighborhood
U_{2}
of
a
in
U\cap U_{1}
and analytic functions
g_{1},\ldots,g_{r}
defined on
U_{2}
such that
f|_{U_{2}}=g_{1}f_{1}|_{U_{2}}+\ldots+g_{r}f_{r}|_{U_{2}}
on
U_{2}
. The analytic set
X\subset\Omega
is said to be
coherent at
a\in X
if there exist an open neighborhood
U
of
a
in
\Omega
and analytic functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}
defined on
U
such that
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}
are good equations for
X
locally at each point
b\in X\cap U
. The analytic set
X\subset\Omega
is
coherent
if it is coherent at each of its points. Non-singular analytic subsets of
\Omega
and singular analytic curves of
\Omega
are examples of coherent analytic set. Cartan proved that each coherent analytic set is
C
-analytic and that the above phenomenon of dimensional decrease cannot occur locally at any coherent point, see
[
C2
, §9&10]
for further details. In particular, Whitney’s umbrella is not coherent at the origin. Actually, Cartan’s Theorem B
[
C2
, §6, Thm.3(B)]
implies the following.

Theorem 1.2

(Cartan,
[
C2
, §7(2)]
)
.

Each coherent analytic set
X\subset\Omega
has the analytic extension property.

Coherence of analytic sets is thus a sufficient local condition that guarantees the analytic extension property. In general, the problem of finding local obstructions to have the analytic extension property has been treated in the literature mainly for
C
-analytic sets for which the previously described phenomenon of dimensional decrease appears. As a matter of fact, in the mind of many researchers non-coherence of
C
-analytic sets concerns the existence of ‘
tails
’, as in the case of Whitney’s umbrella. However, this is not the general situation. In Section
3
we present several examples of pure dimensional
C
-analytic sets (algebraic sets indeed) that are not coherent. The following theorem is our first main result: it asserts that coherence is also a necessary condition to have the analytic extension property.

Theorem 1.3
.

A set
X\subset\Omega
has the analytic extension property if and only if it is a coherent analytic set.

To that end we prove (in view of Theorem
1.2
) the following result. Recall that the ring
{\mathcal{M}}(\Omega)
of meromorphic functions on an open subset
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is the total ring of fractions of the ring
{\mathcal{O}}(\Omega)
of analytic functions on
\Omega
.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
X\subset\Omega
be a non-coherent
C
-analytic set. Then there exist many meromorphic functions
\xi:\Omega\dashrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
whose restrictions
f:=\xi|_{X}
are analytic on
X
, but have no analytic extensions to
\Omega
.

Problem
1.1
has a natural counterpart in Nash geometry. Recall that a subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is
semialgebraic
if it is a Boolean combination of sets defined by polynomial equalities and inequalities. Let
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be an open semialgebraic set. A function
f:\Omega\to{\mathbb{R}}
is said to be
Nash
if it is smooth and its graph is semi-algebraic, or equivalently, by
[
BCR
, Prop.8.1.8]
if it is analytic and its graph is semialgebraic. Let
X
be a subset of
\Omega
. A function
f:X\to{\mathbb{R}}
is called
local Nash
if, for each point
a\in X
, there exists an open semialgebraic neighborhood
U
of
a
in
\Omega
such that
f|_{X\cap U}
extends to a Nash function defined on
U
. We say that the set
X\subset\Omega
has the
Nash extension property
if each local Nash function
f:X\to{\mathbb{R}}
extends to a Nash function defined on
\Omega
. The set
X\subset\Omega
is a
Nash set
if it is the common zero set of finitely many Nash functions defined on
\Omega
. An adapted argument to the one exposed above
[
NT
, Thm.1]
for the analytic setting shows that the Nash extension property is reserved for Nash sets. Let
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be an open semialgebraic set and let
X\subset\Omega
. Suppose that
X
has the Nash extension property, but it is not a Nash set. As the ring
{\mathcal{N}}(\Omega)
is noetherian
[
BCR
, Thm.8.7.18]
, there exists the smallest Nash subset
Y
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
that contains
X
. Observe that
Y
is a
C
-analytic set. Suppose there exists a point
y\in Y\setminus X
and consider the local Nash function
f:X\to{\mathbb{R}},\ x\mapsto\frac{1}{\|x-y\|^{2}}
. As
f
has no analytic extension to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, it has no Nash extension to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, as well.

The notion of coherent Nash set can be defined as in the analytic case: it is enough to replace ‘open neighborhood’ with ‘open semialgebraic neighborhood’ and ‘analytic’ with ‘Nash’, respectively. As we recall in Lemma
2.11
, a Nash set
X\subset\Omega
is coherent (as a Nash set) if and only if it is coherent as an analytic set (se also
[
BFR
, §2.B]
).

Similarly to what we have formulated in the analytic case, we arrive to the following Nash extension problem.

Problem 1.5
.

Given an open semialgebraic set
\Omega
and a Nash set
X\subset\Omega
, is it true that
X\subset\Omega
has the Nash extension property if and only if
X\subset\Omega
is coherent?

The ‘if’ implication was proven in the remarkable papers
[
CRS1
,
CS
]
by Coste, Ruiz and Shiota. So far, as in the analytical case, the ‘only if’ implication has been treated mainly for Nash sets
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
with visible ‘tails’.

Problem
1.5
is solved affirmatively by our second main result.

Theorem 1.6
.

A subset
X
of an open semialgebraic set
\Omega
has the Nash extension property if and only if
X\subset\Omega
is a coherent Nash set.

To that end we prove the following result. Recall that the ring
{\mathcal{M}}^{\bullet}(\Omega)
of meromorphic Nash functions on an open semialgebraic subset
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is the total ring of fractions of the ring
{\mathcal{N}}(\Omega)
of Nash functions on
\Omega
.

Theorem 1.7
.

Let
X
be a non-coherent Nash subset of an open semialgebraic set
\Omega
. Then there exist many meromorphic Nash functions
\xi:\Omega\dashrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
on
\Omega
whose restrictions
f:=\xi|_{X}
to
X
are local Nash functions, but have no Nash extensions to
\Omega
.

The proof of Theorem
1.7
is similar to the one of Theorem
1.4
, but requires to take into account the finiteness conditions imposed by the semialgebricity of Nash functions. In the Nash setting there exist difficulties to pass from local to global results, due to the unexpected cohomological behavior of Nash functions even in the simplest cases. Namely, Hubbard proved that
H^{1}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathbb{R}})\neq 0
, see
[
H
]
. We explain the precise technicalities in §
2.B
.

The theory of sheaves is the appropriate setting where one can contextualize the concept of coherence and consequently the theorems stated above in the analytic case. Let
\Omega
be an open subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and let
X\subset\Omega
be an analytic set. The idea is to associate to each point
x\in\Omega
the ring
{\mathcal{O}}_{\Omega,x}
of germs
f_{x}
at
x
of the analytic functions
f
on a neighborhood of
x
in
\Omega
and the ideal
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\Omega,x}
constituted by the germs
h_{x}
that vanish identically on the germ
X_{x}
of
X
at
x
. Define the set
{\mathcal{O}}_{\Omega}:=\bigcup_{x\in\Omega}{\mathcal{O}}_{\Omega,x}
, the projection map
\pi:{\mathcal{O}}_{\Omega}\to\Omega,\ f_{x}\mapsto x
and the subset
{\mathcal{J}}_{X}:=\bigcup_{x\in\Omega}{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\Omega}
. The set
{\mathcal{O}}_{\Omega}
admits a natural topology such that each analytic functions
f:\Omega\to{\mathbb{R}}
can be interpreted as a continuous section
\sigma:\Omega\to{\mathcal{O}}_{\Omega}
of the projection map
\pi
(that is,
\pi\circ\sigma=\mathrm{id}_{\Omega}
). Namely, if
f:\Omega\to{\mathbb{R}}
is an analytic function, define the section
\sigma_{f}:\Omega\to{\mathcal{O}}_{\Omega},\ x\mapsto f_{x}
of
\pi
induced by
f
, whereas if
\sigma:\Omega\to{\mathcal{O}}_{\Omega}
is a continuous section of
\pi
, define the analytic function
f_{\sigma}:\Omega\to{\mathbb{R}},\ x\mapsto(\sigma(x))(x)
associated to
\sigma
. It holds that
f_{\sigma_{f}}=f
and
\sigma_{f_{\sigma}}=\sigma
. If we endow
{\mathcal{O}}_{\Omega}
with such a topology, we obtain the so-called
sheaf
{\mathcal{O}}_{\Omega}
of analytic functions on
\Omega
and
{\mathcal{J}}_{X}
endowed with the induced topology is the
subsheaf of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\Omega}
of ideals of germs of analytic functions vanishing on
X
. The subsheaf
{\mathcal{J}}_{X}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\Omega}
is coherent at a point
a\in\Omega
if there exist an open neighborhood
U
of
a
in
\Omega
and analytic functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}
defined on
U
such that the germs
f_{1,b},\ldots,f_{r,b}
generate the ideal
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,b}
of the ring
{\mathcal{O}}_{\Omega,b}
for each
b\in U
. The analytic set
X\subset\Omega
is
coherent
if
{\mathcal{J}}_{X}
is coherent at each point of
\Omega
. This ‘sheaf version’ of coherence of
X\subset\Omega
coincides with the one given above.

1.A.
Whitney’s extension problem and related results

Close to Problems
1.1
and
1.5
appears another classic extension problem often called
Whitney’s extension problem
. Although the ‘differentiable nature’ of the latter problem is essentially different from the analytical nature of Problems
1.1
and
1.5
, there are some subtle and deep connections between them that reveals the central role of sheaves and other similar tools.

In his pioneering 1934 articles
[
W1
,
W2
,
W3
]
Whitney raised the question (known as
Whitney’s extension problem
) of determining whether a function
f
defined on a closed subset
X
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
admits an extension to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
of class
{\mathcal{C}}^{p}
for some
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
. In
[
W1
]
it is proved what is known as classical
Whitney’s extension theorem
. Namely, Whitney characterized the families
\{f_{\alpha}\}_{|\alpha|\leq p}
of continuous functions on
X
such that
f_{0}=f
admits an extension
F:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
of class
{\mathcal{C}}^{p}
whose Taylor polynomial at each point
a\in X
is
\sum_{|\alpha|\leq p}f_{\alpha}(a)(x-a)^{\alpha}
. In addition, he proved that
F
can be chosen analytic on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus X
.

The deeper question of finding an extension of
f
to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
of class
{\mathcal{C}}^{p}
using only the values of
f
was treated in
[
W2
]
: here Whitney gave a solution in the case
n=1
using limits of
p^{\mathrm{th}}
divided differences of
f
. In
[
G
]
Glaeser introduced a ‘paratangent bundle’ of
X
using limits of secants and solved the question in the case
p=1
. Bierstone, Milman and Pawłucki
[
BMP2
]
generalized Glaeser’s construction and defined the
paratangent bundle
\tau^{p}(X)
of
X
of any order
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
. The idea is similar (dual in some sense) to the one used to construct the subsheaf
{\mathcal{J}}_{X}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\Omega}
. Namely, repeating a limit procedure (Glaeser operation) a finite number of times, one associates to each point
x\in X
a linear subspace
\tau^{p}_{x}(X)
of the dual space
\mathcal{P}_{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})^{*}
of the real vector space
\mathcal{P}_{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
of polynomial functions on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
of degree
\leq p
. Then, one defines
\tau^{p}(X):=\bigcup_{x\in X}\tau^{p}_{x}(X)
. Applying this construction to the graph of any function
f:X\to{\mathbb{R}}
, one obtains a bundle
\nabla^{p}f
contained in
\tau^{p}(X)\times{\mathbb{R}}
. In the same article
[
BMP2
]
the authors conjectured that
f
has an extension to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
of class
{\mathcal{C}}^{p}
if and only if
\nabla^{p}f
is a ‘function’
(more precisely, the graph of a function
\tau^{p}(X)\to{\mathbb{R}}
), and they proved a version of their conjecture:
if
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a compact subanalytic set and
\nabla^{q}f
is a ‘function’ for some
q\geq p
sufficiently large, then
f
has an extension to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
of class
{\mathcal{C}}^{p}
. The proof of this remarkable result is based on a
{\mathcal{C}}^{p}
composite function property of (the uniformizations of) compact subanalytic sets established in
[
BMP1
]
.

The
{\mathcal{C}}^{p}
composite function property is a version of the
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
composite function property of closed subanalytic sets previously introduced in
[
BM1
]
. Suppose
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a closed subanalytic set. Let
{\mathcal{C}}^{k}(X)
be the set of restrictions to
X
of functions defined on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
of class
{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
for each
k\in{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{\infty\}
and define
{\mathcal{C}}^{(\infty)}(X):=\bigcap_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{k}(X)
. Evidently,
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(X)\subset{\mathcal{C}}^{(\infty)}(X)
. An important result asserts that the
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
composite function property for
X
is equivalent both to the equality
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(X)={\mathcal{C}}^{(\infty)}(X)
and to a ‘stratified coherence’ property of
X
, the so-called
semicoherence
of
X
, widely studied and developed in
[
BM3
,
BM4
]
. Thus,
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(X)={\mathcal{C}}^{(\infty)}(X)
if and only if
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a semicoherent subanalytic set

[
BMP1
]
. Our Theorems
1.3
and
1.6
are the analytic and Nash counterparts of the latter differential subanalytic result. Examples of semicoherent subanalytic sets are the closed Nash subanalytic sets, which include closed semianalytic sets
[
BM1
]
. In
[
P1
]
Pawłucki provided an example of
3
-dimensional compact subanalytic set
P\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{5}
that is not semicoherent, so
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(P)\subsetneq{\mathcal{C}}^{(\infty)}(P)
(see also
[
P2
]
). In his 2006 article
[
F3
]
Fefferman provided a complete solution to Whitney’s extension problem by proving the above Bierstone-Milman-Pawłucki conjecture via the use of a natural variant of the paratangent bundle of order
p
, see also
[
F1
,
F2
,
BMP3
]
. We refer the reader to Fefferman’s expository paper
[
F4
]
for further details and references.

Whitney’s extension theorem has been generalized to the o-minimal setting by Kurdyka, Pawłucki and Thamrongthanyalak in
[
KP1
,
KP2
,
Th
]
. The statements are natural o-minimal reformulations of the classical theorem. The proofs however are quite different, because o-minimality involves always finiteness restrictions that prevent the use of certain strong techniques from the differential case.

Let
S\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a closed semialgebraic set and let
f:S\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a semialgebraic function. Suppose
f
extends to a function
F:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
of class
{\mathcal{C}}^{p}
for some
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
. A question proposed by Bierstone and Milman
[
Z
]
asks whether it is possible to take
F
semialgebraic. In the continuous case, the affirmative solution is contained in the article
[
DK
]
by Delfs and Knebusch. In
[
ATh
]
, Aschenbrenner and Thamrongthanyalak solved affirmatively this question in the case
p=1
. Recently, Fefferman and Luli
[
FL
]
settled the case
n=2
for an arbitrary
p\geq 1
. In
[
BCM
]
Bierstone-Campesato-Milman provided a weak solution to the previous problem with a
loss of differentiability
via a function
t:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{N}}
such that
t(p)\geq p
for each
p\geq 1
. Namely, if the semialgebraic function
f:S\to{\mathbb{R}}
extends to a function
F:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
of class
{\mathcal{C}}^{t(p)}
for some
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
, then
f
extend to a
{\mathcal{C}}^{p}
differentiable semialgebraic function
F^{\prime}:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
.

In Section
7
and Appendix
A
we relate smooth semialgebraic functions on a semialgebraic set
S\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
(see §
7.B
) with semialgebraic functions on
S
that are local Nash at the points of
S
. We characterize in Theorem
7.7
the semialgebraic sets
S
such that the smooth semialgebraic functions on
S
coincide with the Nash functions on
S
. In case
S=X
is a Nash subset of an open semialgebraic subset
U
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, the previous coincidence is provided exactly when
X
is coherent (see Theorem
1.7
and Lemma
7.9
).

Structure of the article

The article is organized as follows. In Section
2
we recall and analyze some results concerning coherence in both the real analytic and the Nash cases. In Section
3
we study the set
T(X)
of ‘tails’ of a
C
-analytic set
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, we recall a description of its set
N(X)
of points of non-coherence provided in
[
ABF3
]
and provide a good understanding of both sets. We show as well that both
T(X)
and
N(X)
are semialgebraic subsets of
X
in case
X
is a Nash set. We also discuss in Section
3
some enlightening examples that the reader can have in mind to illustrate the strategy followed to prove in Section
6
(stronger versions of) the main results of this article: Theorems
1.4
and
1.7
. In Sections
4
and
5
we present the key Theorems
4.1
and
5.1
, which (have interest by their own and): (1)
N(X)
is adherent to the set
T(X)
of ‘tails’ of
X
(even if they are hidden) and (2) there exists special analytic equations (resp. Nash equations) of
X\setminus N(X)
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X)
that encode the ‘tails’ of
X
outside
N(X)
. Finally, in Section
7
we prove that the ring of
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
semialgebraic functions on a Nash set
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
coincide with the ring of Nash functions on
X
if and only if
X
is coherent. More generally, we characterize the semialgebraic sets
S\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
for which smooth semialgebraic functions on
S
coincide with Nash functions on
S
. The article finishes with an Appendix
A
where we prove the equivalence between the smooth semialgebraic functions on
S
and the semialgebraic functions on
S
that are local Nash on
S
.

2.
Coherence of analytic and Nash sets

The content of this section is scattered in the literature. We include it (with suitable references to the literature when available) in order to have a full vision of the tools we have at our disposal to prove the main results of this article. Given a function
f
or a set
{\mathfrak{F}}
of functions on a set
X
, we denote
\mathcal{Z}(f)=\{x\in X:\ f(x)=0\}
and
\mathcal{Z}({\mathfrak{F}})=\bigcap_{f\in{\mathfrak{F}}}\mathcal{Z}(f)
their respective zero sets. If
X
is a topological space and
f_{1,x},\ldots,f_{r,x}
are function germs at a point
x\in X
represented by functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}
defined on a common open neighborhood
V^{x}\subset X
of
x
, we define
\mathcal{Z}(f_{1,x},\ldots,f_{r,x})
as the set germ
\{y\in V^{x}:\ f_{1}(y)=0,\ldots,f_{r}(y)=0\}_{x}
at
x
. Let
{\mathbb{K}}:={\mathbb{R}}
or
{\mathbb{C}}
and let us endow
{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
with the Euclidean topology.

2.A.
Coherence in the analytic setting

We begin this section comparing the concept of coherence for complex analytic sets and real analytic sets and analyzing its main consequences. Let
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
be an open set and let
X\subset\Omega
be a closed set. Recall that
X
is an
analytic subset of
\Omega
if for each
x\in X
there exists analytic function germs
f_{1,x},\ldots,f_{r,x}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{K}}^{n},x}
such that
X_{x}=\mathcal{Z}(f_{1,x},\ldots,f_{r,x})
.

2.A.1.
Coherence of complex analytic sets.

Suppose first that we are in the complex case
{\mathbb{K}}={\mathbb{C}}
and let
X\subset\Omega
be a complex analytic subset of and open set
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
. Pick a point
x\in\Omega
and denote
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}:=\{f_{x}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},x}:\ X_{x}\subset\mathcal{Z}(f_{x})\}\}
the zero ideal of the complex analytic germ
X_{x}
. Let
f_{1,x},\ldots,f_{r,x}\in{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}
be a system of generators of the ideal
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},x}
and let
U^{x}\subset\Omega
be an open neighborhood of
x
such that there exist analytic functions
f_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}(U^{x})
that are representatives of the analytic germs
f_{i,x}
. Oka’s coherence theorems
[
N1
, Ch.IV]
imply that if we shrink
U^{x}
, then
\{f_{1,y},\ldots,f_{r,y}\}
is a system of generators of
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,y}
for each
y\in U^{x}
. This means that every complex analytic set
X\subset\Omega
is
coherent
. If in addition the open set
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
is Stein, then the coherence of
X
(that we have by Oka’s coherence theorems) implies:

(i)

X
is the zero set of finitely many analytic functions on
\Omega
(see
[
C2
, Proof of Prop.15]
).

(ii)

The ideal of analytic functions on
\Omega
that are identically zero on
X
generates the ideal of analytic germs on
\Omega_{x}
that vanishes identically on
X_{x}
for each
x\in X
(as a consequence of Cartan’s Theorem A).

(iii)

The analytic functions on
X
(that is, the global sections of the sheaf
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{J}}_{X}
) are restrictions to
X
of analytic functions on
\Omega
(as a consequence of Cartan’s Theorem B).

Observe that the limitation to have the previous properties is of global nature, that is,
\Omega
is a Stein space, or equivalently,
\Omega
has a suitable shape to have ‘enough’ analytic functions.

2.A.2.
Coherence of real analytic sets

We provide next a more systematic approach to the concept of
coherence of real analytic sets
than the one provided in the Introduction. Assume first
\Omega={\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and let
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a
C
-analytic subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, that is, the common zero set of finitely many analytic functions on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. We will comment below in §
2.A.4
(via Whitney’s analytic immersion theorem) that this assumption is not restrictive. For each open subset
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
denote the ring of analytic functions on
U
with
{\mathcal{O}}(U)
. Let
{\mathcal{I}}(X):=\{f\in{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}):\ X\subset\mathcal{Z}(f)\}
be the ideal of
{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
of analytic functions vanishing identically on
X
. Consider the sheaves
{\mathcal{J}}_{X}
and
{\mathcal{I}}_{X}
of ideals on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
given by the following formulas:

\begin{cases}{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}:=\{f_{x}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}:\ X_{x}\subset\mathcal{Z}(f_{x})\},\\
{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}:={\mathcal{I}}(X){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}.\end{cases}

If
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is an open subset, then
H^{0}(U,{\mathcal{J}}_{X})={\mathcal{I}}(X\cap U)=\{f\in{\mathcal{O}}(U):\ X\cap U\subset\mathcal{Z}(f)\}
, whereas
H^{0}(U,{\mathcal{I}}_{X})={\mathcal{I}}(X){\mathcal{O}}(U)
. In general,
{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}\subset{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}
for each
x\in X
and
{\mathcal{I}}(X){\mathcal{O}}(U)\subset{\mathcal{I}}(X\cap U)
for each open subset
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

A real analytic set
X
is
coherent if the sheaf of ideals
{\mathcal{J}}_{X}
is coherent. Recall that a sheaf
{\mathcal{F}}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-modules is coherent if:

(i)

{\mathcal{F}}
is of finite type, that is, for each
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
there exist an open neighborhood
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
of
x
,
m\in{\mathbb{N}}^{*}
and a surjective morphism
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}^{m}|_{U}\rightarrow{\mathcal{F}}|_{U}
, and

(ii)

the kernel of each homomorphism
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}^{p}|_{V}\rightarrow{\mathcal{F}}|_{V}
is of finite type for each
p\geq 1
and each open subset
V
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

As the sheaf of rings
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
(see
[
S
, §1.2.15.Def.3]
for the definition of sheaf of rings) is by
[
C2
, Prop.4]
coherent, a sheaf of ideals
{\mathcal{F}}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
is coherent if and only if
{\mathcal{F}}
is of finite type
[
GR
, IV.B.Prop.8]
. The famous Cartan’s Theorems A and B (
[
C2
, Thm.3]
) describe the local-global behavior of coherent sheaves
{\mathcal{F}}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-modules:

(A)

The stalks of a coherent sheaf
{\mathcal{F}}
are spanned by the global sections.

(B)

Each
p
-cohomology group of a coherent sheaf
{\mathcal{F}}
is trivial for each
p>0
.

Remark 2.1
.

The previous results for real analytic sets are derived from Cartan’s Theorems A and B for Stein spaces and the following fact:
each open set
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
has by
[
C2
, §1]
a basis of ‘very nice’ open neighborhoods in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
, which: are Stein open sets, are invariant under conjugation in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
and have
U
as a deformation retract. Thus, in the real case a sufficient condition to have the properties (i), (ii), (iii) described above in §
2.A.1
for complex analytic sets is
coherence. The substantial difference between complex and real analytic sets concerns coherence: complex analytic sets are always coherent (Oka’s coherence theorems), whereas real analytic sets behave as complex analytic sets (in a Stein open subset of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
) when they are coherent.

\hfill\sqbullet

Recall that a real analytic set
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is pure dimensional if the analytic germs
X_{x}
have the same dimension as
X
for each
x\in X
. For each real analytic set
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
we have the following property concerning coherence:

Lemma 2.2

(
[
C2
, Prop.14&15]
)
.

Each coherent analytic set
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a
C
-analytic set. In addition, if
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a
C
-irreducible coherent analytic set, then it is pure dimensional.

A non-empty
C
-analytic set
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is
C
-irreducible if it is not the union of two proper
C
-analytic subsets. We refer the reader to
[
Fe1
,
WB
]
for a detailed study about
C
-irreducibility. As a consequence of the previous lemma and Cartan’s Theorem A, we have the following crucial characterization of coherent real analytic sets, which will be useful along the sequel.

Corollary 2.3
.

A real analytic set
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is coherent if and only if it is a
C
-analytic set and
{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}={\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}
for each
x\in X
.

Remark 2.4
.

Let
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a coherent
C
-analytic set and
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
an open subset. Then
{\mathcal{I}}(X\cap U)={\mathcal{I}}(X){\mathcal{O}}(U)
.

As
X\cap U
is a coherent
C
-analytic subset of
U
, we have
{\mathcal{I}}(X\cap U){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|_{U}={\mathcal{J}}_{X\cap U}={\mathcal{J}}_{X}|_{U}={\mathcal{I}}_{X}|_{U}={\mathcal{I}}(X){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|_{U}
. Consequently,
{\mathcal{I}}(X\cap U)=H^{0}(U,{\mathcal{I}}(X\cap U){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|_{U})=H^{0}(U,{\mathcal{I}}(X){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|_{U})={\mathcal{I}}(X){\mathcal{O}}(U)
.
\sqbullet

2.A.3.
Analytic extension property

Let
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a
C
-analytic subset. The sheaf of analytic functions germs on
X
is
{\mathcal{C}}^{\omega}_{X}:={\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{J}}_{X}
, whereas
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}:={\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{I}}_{X}
is the sheaf of global analytic functions germs on
X
. The name of the latter follows from Cartan’s Theorem B. The sheaf of ideals
{\mathcal{I}}_{X}
is generated by global sections, so it is a coherent
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-sheaf of ideals on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
whose support is
X
and in fact:
{\mathcal{I}}_{X}
is the biggest coherent
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-sheaf of ideals whose support is
X
.

Proof.

Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a coherent
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-sheaf of ideals whose support is
X
. By Cartan’s Theorem A the stalks
{\mathcal{F}}_{x}
are generated by global sections of
{\mathcal{F}}
and
H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{F}})\subset{\mathcal{I}}(X)
. Thus,
{\mathcal{F}}=H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{F}}){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\subset{\mathcal{I}}(X){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}={\mathcal{I}}_{X}
.
∎

In particular, the
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-sheaf of quotient rings
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{I}}_{X}
is coherent. By Cartan’s Theorem B the cohomology group
H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{I}}_{X})=0
, so the exact sequence

0\to H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{I}}_{X})\to H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})\to H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{I}}_{X})\to 0

is short and consequently each global section of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{I}}_{X}
is the image of a global analytic function on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. We denote
{\mathcal{O}}(X):=H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{I}}_{X})\equiv H^{0}(X,({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{I}}_{X})|_{X})
the ring of global analytic functions on
X
, whereas
{\mathcal{C}}^{\omega}(X):=H^{0}(X,({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{J}}_{X})|_{X})
is the ring of analytic functions on
X
. At this point we reformulate what means the analytic extension property for
C
-analytic sets.

Definition 2.5

(Analytic extension property)
.

A
C
-analytic set
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
has the
analytic extension property if the homomorphism of rings
{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\omega}(X)
is surjective, that is, each analytic function on
X
is the restriction to
X
of a analytic function on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

2.A.4.
Reduction to the case of affine spaces

We comment briefly why our choice of considering
C
-analytic subsets of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
instead of
C
-analytic subsets of an open subset
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is not restrictive.

Proof.

Assume that
M
is a real analytic manifold of dimension
m
(this includes the case of an open subset of an affine space) and let
X\subset M
be a
C
-analytic subset, that is, the common zero set of finitely many analytic functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}
on
M
. By Whitney’s analytic immersion theorem there exists an analytic immersion
\varphi:M\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{2m+1}
such that
\varphi(M)
is closed in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2m+1}
, that is, we may assume that
M
is a closed real analytic submanifold in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
for some positive integer
n
. Let
(U,\rho)
be an analytic tubular neighborhood of
M
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and define
F_{i}:=f_{i}\circ\rho
for
i=1,\ldots,r
. As
M
is a coherent analytic subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r}
define global sections of the coherent sheaf
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{J}}_{M}
, there exist analytic functions
G_{1},\ldots,G_{r}\in{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
such that
G_{i}|_{M}=f_{i}
. Let
G_{r+1}
be an analytic equation of
M
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. We have
X=\mathcal{Z}(G_{1},\ldots,G_{r},G_{r+1})
is a
C
-analytic subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Thus, in the following we only treat the case of
C
-analytic subsets of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
∎

2.B.
Coherence in the Nash setting

Next, we turn out to the Nash case. We have already defined in the Introduction semialgebraic sets, Nash functions and Nash sets. A semialgebraic set
M\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is called an (affine) Nash manifold if it is in addition a smooth submanifold of (an open semialgebraic subset of)
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. By
[
BCR
, Prop.8.1.8 & Cor.9.3.10]
Nash manifolds coincide with analytic submanifolds of (an open semialgebraic subset of)
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
that are theirselves semialgebraic sets. Let
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be an open semialgebraic set and let us denote the ring of Nash functions on
U
with
{\mathcal{N}}(U)
. Let
S\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a semialgebraic set. We say that a function
f:S\to{\mathbb{R}}
is a Nash function if there exist an open semialgebraic neighborhood
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
of
S
and a Nash extension
F:U\to{\mathbb{R}}
of
f
. We denote
{\mathcal{N}}(S)
the ring of Nash functions on
S
(see also Definition
7.6
, Remark
7.7
, §
7.D.2
and (
7.6
)). At this point we prove (see also
[
FGR
, Prop.2.8(ii)]
) that Nash subsets of an open semialgebraic subset
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
coincide with those
C
-analytic subset of
U
that are semialgebraic subsets of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. This means that the Nash category corresponds to the amalgamation of the (global) analytic and semialgebraic categories.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be an open semialgebraic set and let
X\subset U
. The following conditions are equivalent:

(i)

X
is a Nash subset of
U
.

(ii)

X
is a
C
-analytic subset of
U
that is a semialgebraic subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

Proof.

The implication (i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii) is clear, because Nash functions are analytic functions
[
BCR
, Prop.8.1.8]
, so let us prove the converse implication. We claim:
We may assume that
X
is a
C
-irreducible
C
-analytic subset of
U
.

By
[
BCR
, Prop.2.9.10]

X
is a finite pairwise disjoint union of connected Nash manifolds
M_{i}
, each one Nash diffeomorphic to
(0,1)^{\dim(M_{i})}
. By
[
WB
, §6.Prop.8 & Prop.9]
there exist the locally finite family
\{X_{j}\}_{j\in J}
of
C
-irreducible
C
-analytic components of
X
. By the identity principle each Nash manifold
M_{i}
is contained in some
X_{j}
. As the
M_{i}
are finitely many, the
X_{j}
are finitely many and each one is a finite union of some of the Nash manifolds
M_{i}
. Consequently, each
X_{j}
is a semialgebraic set. Thus, we may assume that
X
is a
C
-irreducible
C
-analytic subset of
U
.

Let
Y
be the smallest Nash set that contains
X
, which exists, because
{\mathcal{N}}(U)
is noetherian
[
BCR
, Thm.8.7.18]
. As
X
is
C
-irreducible,
Y
is an irreducible Nash set, so the zero ideal
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(Y):=\{f\in{\mathcal{N}}(U):X\subset\mathcal{Z}(f)\}
of
Y
in
{\mathcal{N}}(U)
is prime. By
[
FGR
, Prop.2.8(i)]
the ideal
{\mathcal{I}}(Y)
is generated by
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(Y)
in
{\mathcal{O}}(U)
, that is,
{\mathcal{I}}(Y)={\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(Y){\mathcal{O}}(U)
. By
[
CS
, Cor.2]
the ideal
{\mathcal{I}}(Y)={\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(Y){\mathcal{O}}(U))
is a prime ideal of
{\mathcal{O}}(U)
, so
Y
is a
C
-irreducible
C
-analytic subset of
U
. As
X
is semialgebraic, we have
\dim(X)=\dim(Y)
, so
Y=X
, because both are irreducible Nash sets. Consequently,
X
is a Nash subset of
U
, as required.
∎

2.B.1.
Coherence of Nash sets

Assume first we work in the open semialgebraic set
\Omega={\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. We will comment below in §
2.B.4
(via Mostowski’s Lemma
[
Mo
, Lem.6]
) that this assumption is not restrictive. Let us recover the definition of Nash function on a Nash subset
X
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
via global sections of a certain sheaf
[
BFR
, §2.B]
. The first choice should be the following. Let
{\mathcal{J}}_{X}^{\bullet}
be the sheaf of
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-ideals given by

{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}^{\bullet}:=\{f_{x}\in{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}:\ X_{x}\subset\mathcal{Z}(f_{x})\},

which is the ideal of germs of Nash functions in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{x}
vanishing identically on
X_{x}
for each
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and whose support is
X
. As in the analytic case, the sheaf
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{J}}_{X}^{\bullet}
has in general a bad local-global behavior and the ring of its global sections does not coincide necessarily with the ring of the restrictions to
X
of Nash functions on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

As
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
is a coherent sheaf of rings (see
[
S
, §1.2.15.Def.3]
and
[
Sh
, (I.6.6)]
), a sheaf
{\mathcal{F}}
of
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-ideals is coherent if and only if
{\mathcal{F}}
is of finite type
[
GR
, IV.B.Prop.8]
. When dealing with sheaves of
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-ideals, coherence is not enough in general to have results in the vein of Cartan’s Theorems (A) and (B) (see the Introduction of
[
CRS2
]
), because semialgebraicity involves a finiteness phenomenon that coherence alone does not capture. We say that a sheaf
{\mathcal{F}}
of
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-ideals is finite if there exists a finite open semialgebraic covering
\{U_{i}\}_{i=1}^{r}
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
such that each restriction
{\mathcal{F}}|_{U_{i}}
is generated by finitely many Nash functions over
U_{i}
. As a finite sheaf of
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-ideals is of finite type, we deduce that it is coherent. In
[
CS
,
CRS1
]
the authors prove that this is the correct notion for sheaves of
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-ideals to obtain results in the vein of Cartan’s Theorems (A) and (B) (in general for
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-modules the right notion is strong coherence
[
CRS2
]
). Recall that a coherent sheaf
{\mathcal{M}}
of
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-modules is strongly coherent if there exists an exact sequence
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}^{q}\to{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}^{p}\to{\mathcal{M}}\to 0
. We suggest the survey
[
CRS3
]
as a general reference.

Theorem 2.7

(
[
CS
, Thm.1, Cor.2]
,
[
CRS1
, Prop.0.6, Prop.0.7]
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a finite sheaf of
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-ideals and
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Then:

(A)

The stalk
{\mathcal{F}}_{x}
is generated by global sections of
{\mathcal{F}}
.

(B)

Every global section of
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{F}}
is represented by a Nash function on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

Let
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a Nash set and denote the (finitely generated) ideal
\{f\in{\mathcal{N}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}):X\subset\mathcal{Z}(f)\}
of Nash functions vanishing identically on
X
with
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X)
. In the following the sheaf of
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-ideals
{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}^{\bullet}:={\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X){\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
will play a central role, because it is the both the biggest finite and the biggest coherent
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-sheaf of ideals whose support is
X
(Corollary
2.10
). The proof of this fact takes advantage of the faithfully flatness of the inclusion homomorphism
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
for each
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Let us recall how one prove this latter fact, which will be useful several times.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
(A,{\mathfrak{m}}_{A})
and
(B,{\mathfrak{m}}_{B})
be local excellent rings and let
\varphi:A\hookrightarrow B
be an inclusion homomorphism such that
\varphi({\mathfrak{m}}_{A})\subset{\mathfrak{m}}_{B}
. Assume that the completions
\widehat{A}
and
\widehat{B}
coincide. Then
\varphi
is faithfully flat.

Proof.

As
A
and
B
are excellent local rings, the inclusions homomorphisms
\phi:A\hookrightarrow\widehat{A}
and
\psi:B\hookrightarrow\widehat{B}
are by
[
Mt
, (33.A), (34.A)]
regular. As
\widehat{A}=\widehat{B}
, we may assume
\phi=\psi\circ\varphi
. As both
\phi
and
\psi
are regular homomorphisms, we deduce by
[
Mt
, (33.B) Lem.1]
that
\varphi
is regular, so it is flat. As
A
and
B
are local rings and
\varphi({\mathfrak{m}}_{A})\subset{\mathfrak{m}}_{B}
, we deduce by
[
Mt
, (4.D) Thm.3]
that
\varphi
is faithfully flat, as required.
∎

Corollary 2.9
.

The inclusion homomorphism
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
is faithfully flat.

Proof.

As
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}\cong{\mathbb{R}}\{{\tt x}_{1},\ldots,{\tt x}_{n}\}
and
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
is by
[
BCR
, Cor.8.1.6]

{\mathbb{R}}
-isomorphic to the ring of algebraic formal series
{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}_{1},\ldots,{\tt x}_{n}]]_{\rm alg}
, we deduce by
[
ABR
, Ch.VII.Ex.2.3]
that both rings are local excellent rings. Their common completion is
{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}_{1},\ldots,{\tt x}_{n}]]
and their maximal ideals are generated by the variables
{\tt x}_{1},\ldots,{\tt x}_{n}
. By Lemma
2.8
we conclude that the inclusion homomorphism
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
is faithfully flat, as required.
∎

Corollary 2.10
.

The sheaf of
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-ideals given by
{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}^{\bullet}:={\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X){\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
for each
x\in X
is both:

(i)

The biggest finite sheaf of
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-ideals whose support is
X
.

(ii)

The biggest coherent sheaf of
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-ideals whose support is
X
.

In addition,
{\mathcal{I}}_{X}={\mathcal{I}}_{X}^{\bullet}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
.

Proof.

(i) Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a finite sheaf of
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-ideals whose support is
X
. By Theorem
2.7
(A) the stalks
{\mathcal{F}}_{x}
are generated by global sections of
{\mathcal{F}}
. As the global sections of
{\mathcal{F}}
vanish identically on
X
, we deduce
H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{F}})\subset{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X)
, so
{\mathcal{F}}_{x}=H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{F}}){\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}\subset{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X){\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}={\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}^{\bullet}
for each
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

(ii) Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a coherent sheaf of
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-ideals whose support is
X
. Then
{\mathcal{F}}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
is a coherent sheaf of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-ideals whose support is
X
as well, so
H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{F}}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})\subset{\mathcal{I}}(X)={\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X){\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
by
[
FGR
, Prop.2.8(i)]
. By Cartan’s Theorem A

{\mathcal{F}}_{x}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}=H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{F}}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}\subset({\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X){\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}={\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}_{X,x}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}

for each
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. As the monomorphism
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
is by Corollary
2.9
faithful flat, we conclude
{\mathcal{F}}_{x}\subset{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}_{X,x}
for each
x\in X
, as required.

The latter assertion holds, because by
[
FGR
, Prop.2.8(i)]

{\mathcal{I}}_{X}={\mathcal{I}}(X){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}={\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X){\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}={\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}={\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X){\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}={\mathcal{I}}_{X}^{\bullet}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}},

as required.
∎

2.B.2.
Nash coherence versus analytic coherence

In this framework it is natural to ask when
X
is coherent as a Nash set, that is, when
{\mathcal{J}}_{X}^{\bullet}
is a coherent sheaf of
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-ideals. The following result borrowed from
[
BFR
, §2.B]
establishes the equivalence of Nash coherence and analytic coherence for Nash sets.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a Nash set. The following conditions are equivalent:

(i)

X
is coherent as a Nash set.

(ii)

X
is coherent as a
C
-analytic set.

(iii)

{\mathcal{J}}_{X}^{\bullet}={\mathcal{I}}_{X}^{\bullet}
.

Proof.

(i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii) Suppose first
X
is a coherent Nash set. Recall that
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}={\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}^{\bullet}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
(see
[
BCR
, Proof of Prop.8.6.9]
) and the ideal
{\mathcal{I}}(X\cap U)
is generated by
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X\cap U)
for each open semialgebraic set
U
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
(see
[
FGR
, Prop.2.8(i)]
). As
{\mathcal{J}}_{X}^{\bullet}
is of finite type, for each
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
there exists an open semialgebraic neighborhood
U^{x}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
such that
{\mathcal{J}}_{X}^{\bullet}|_{U^{x}}={\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X\cap U^{x}){\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|_{U^{x}}
. The sheaf of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-ideals
{\mathcal{F}}
given by
{\mathcal{F}}|_{U^{x}}:={\mathcal{I}}(X\cap U^{x}){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|_{U^{x}}={\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X\cap U^{x}){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|_{U^{x}}
for each
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is of finite type (because the ideal
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X\cap U^{x})
is finitely generated, as
{\mathcal{N}}(U^{x})
is a Noetherian ring) and its support is
X
. Thus,
{\mathcal{F}}
is coherent and by Cartan’s Theorem A
{\mathcal{F}}_{x}={\mathcal{I}}(X){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}={\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}
. Putting all together:

{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}={\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}^{\bullet}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}=({\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X\cap U^{x}){\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}={\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X\cap U^{x}){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}={\mathcal{F}}_{x}={\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}

for each
x\in X
, so
X
is a coherent analytic set.

(ii)
\Longrightarrow
(iii) Suppose next
X
is a coherent
C
-analytic set. Consider the ideal
{\mathcal{I}}(X)
of all analytic functions in
{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
vanishing identically on
X
. By Cartan’s Theorem A the stalks
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}
are generated by the global sections
H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{J}}_{X})={\mathcal{I}}(X)
. As
{\mathcal{I}}(X)
is generated by
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X)
in
{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
(see
[
FGR
, Prop.2.8(i)]
), it follows

{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}^{\bullet}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}={\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}={\mathcal{I}}(X){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}={\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}\supset{\mathcal{J}}^{\bullet}_{X,x}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}\supset{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}^{\bullet}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x},

so
{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}^{\bullet}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}={\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}^{\bullet}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
. The monomorphism
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
is by Corollart
2.9
faithful flat, so
{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}^{\bullet}={\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}^{\bullet}
for each
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, that is,
{\mathcal{J}}_{X}^{\bullet}={\mathcal{I}}_{X}^{\bullet}
.

(iii)
\Longrightarrow
(i) As
{\mathcal{J}}_{X}^{\bullet}={\mathcal{I}}_{X}^{\bullet}
is a coherent sheaf of
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-ideals,
X
is coherent as a Nash set, as required.
∎

Remark 2.12
.

If
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a coherent Nash set, then
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X){\mathcal{N}}(U)={\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X\cap U)
for each semialgebraic open subset
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, where
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X\cap U)=\{f\in{\mathcal{N}}(U):\ X\cap U\subset\mathcal{Z}(f)\}
.

As
X\cap U
is a coherent Nash subset of
U
, we have
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X\cap U){\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|_{U}={\mathcal{J}}^{\bullet}_{X\cap U}={\mathcal{J}}^{\bullet}_{X}|_{U}={\mathcal{I}}_{X}^{\bullet}|_{U}={\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X){\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|_{U}
. By
[
Sh
, I.6.5]
we have

{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X\cap U)=H^{0}(U,{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X\cap U){\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|_{U})=H^{0}(U,{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X){\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|_{U})={\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X){\mathcal{N}}(U),

as required.
\sqbullet

2.B.3.
Nash extension property

Roughly speaking, the coherent sheaf
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{I}}_{X}^{\bullet}
captures the global notions concerning Nash functions, whereas
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{J}}_{X}^{\bullet}
the local ones. Define
{\mathcal{C}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{N}}}(X):=H^{0}(X,({\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{J}}^{\bullet}_{X})|_{X})
, which is the ring of local Nash functions on a Nash set
X
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. We will see in Lemma
7.9
that the elements of
{\mathcal{C}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{N}}}(X)
are semialgebraic functions on
X
. We reformulate what means the Nash extension property for Nash sets.

Definition 2.13
.

A Nash set
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
has the
Nash extension property if the homomorphism
{\mathcal{N}}(X)\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{N}}}(X)
is surjective, that is, each local Nash function on
X
is the restriction to
X
of a Nash function on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

2.B.4.
Reduction to the case of affine spaces

As in the real analytic case, we comment briefly why our choice of considering Nash subsets of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
instead of Nash subsets of an open semialgebraic subset
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is not restrictive.

Proof.

Let
M\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a Nash manifold and let
X\subset M
be a Nash subset, that is, the common zero set of finitely many Nash functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}
on
M
. As
M
is locally compact,
U:={\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus(\operatorname{Cl}(M)\setminus M)
is an open semialgebraic subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. By Mostowski’s Lemma
[
Mo
, Lem.6]
there exists a continuous semialgebraic function
h:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that
h|_{U}
is a strictly positive Nash function and
\mathcal{Z}(h)={\mathbb{R}}^{m}\setminus U=\operatorname{Cl}(M)\setminus M
. The image of the Nash map
\varphi:U\to{\mathbb{R}}^{m+1},\ x\mapsto\big(x,\frac{1}{h(x)}\big)
is the closed Nash manifold
T:=\varphi(U)=\{(x,y)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}:\ yh(x)=1\}
and the projection

\pi:{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\ (x,x_{n+1}):=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},x_{n+1})\to x

induces by restriction a Nash map
\rho:=\pi|_{T}:T\to U
. Observe that
\varphi:U\to T
and
\rho:T\to U
are mutually inverse diffeomorphisms. Thus, as
M
is closed in
U
, also
N:=\varphi(M)
is closed in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
, so we may assume from the beginning that
M
is a closed Nash submanifold in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
for some positive integer
n
. Let
(U,\rho)
be a Nash tubular neighborhood of
M
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and define
F_{i}:=f_{i}\circ\rho
for
i=1,\ldots,r
. As
M
is a coherent Nash subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r}
define global sections of the coherent sheaf
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{J}}_{M}^{\bullet}
, there exist by Theorem
2.7
(i), Corollary
2.10
and Lemma
2.11
Nash functions
G_{1},\ldots,G_{r}\in{\mathcal{N}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
such that
G_{i}|_{M}=f_{i}
. Let
G_{r+1}
be a Nash equation of
M
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. We have
X=\mathcal{Z}(G_{1},\ldots,G_{r},G_{r+1})
is a Nash subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
∎

2.B.5.
Cartan’s Theorems (A) and (B) in the Nash setting.

Let us summarize a part of the information above in the following Cartan’s style result:

Theorem 2.14
.

Let
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be an open semialgebraic set and let
X\subset\Omega
be a coherent Nash subset of
\Omega
. Then:

(A)

The ideal
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X)
of Nash functions on
\Omega
that are identically zero on
X
generates the ideal
{\mathcal{J}}^{\bullet}_{X,x}
of Nash germs on
\Omega_{x}
that vanishes identically on
X_{x}
for each
x\in X
.

(B)

The Nash functions on
X
, that is, the global sections of the sheaf
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{J}}^{\bullet}_{X}
are restrictions to
X
of Nash functions on
\Omega
.

We recall here (by the sake of completeness) the identity
{\mathcal{N}}(X)=H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{I}}_{X}^{\bullet})
.

Corollary 2.15
.

There exist a natural isomorphism between the ring
{\mathcal{N}}(X)
of Nash functions on
X
and the ring of global sections of the sheaf
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{I}}_{X}^{\bullet}
, that is,
{\mathcal{N}}(X)=H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{I}}_{X}^{\bullet})
.

Proof.

By Remark
2.12
we have
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X\cap V)={\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X){\mathcal{N}}(V)
for each open semialgebraic subset
V\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Then by Theorem
2.14
(B) we deduce
H^{0}(V,({\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{I}}_{X}^{\bullet})|_{V})={\mathcal{N}}(V)/{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X){\mathcal{N}}(V)
for each open semialgebraic subset
V\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. The support of
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{I}}_{X}^{\bullet}
is
X
, so for each open semialgebraic neighborhood
U
of
X
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
we have
H^{0}(X,({\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{I}}_{X}^{\bullet})|_{X})\equiv H^{0}(U,({\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{I}}_{X}^{\bullet})|_{U})\equiv H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{I}}_{X}^{\bullet})
. Fix an open semialgebraic neighborhood
U
of
X
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. As

{\mathcal{N}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})/{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X)\equiv H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{I}}_{X}^{\bullet})\equiv H^{0}(U,{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{I}}_{X}^{\bullet}|_{U})\equiv{\mathcal{N}}(U)/{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X){\mathcal{N}}(U),

we deduce that for each Nash function
g:U\to{\mathbb{R}}
there exists a Nash function
h:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that
g-h|_{U}\in{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X){\mathcal{N}}(U)
, so
g=h
on
X
. In particular,
each function
f\in{\mathcal{N}}(X)
has a Nash extension to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, so that the restriction homomorphism

{\mathcal{N}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\to{\mathcal{N}}(X),\ f\mapsto f|_{X}

(2.1)

is surjective. Consequently,
{\mathcal{N}}(X)={\mathcal{N}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})/{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X)\equiv H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{I}}_{X}^{\bullet})
, as required.
∎

3.
Set of tails and set of points of non-coherence. Examples

In this section we recall the description of the set
N(X)
of points of non-coherence of a
C
-analytic set
X
provided in
[
ABF3
, §5.1]
and we analyze the set
T(X)
of ‘tails’ of
X
. The characterization of
N(X)
is inspired by
[
T2
,
TT
]
and the description of both
N(X)
and
T(X)
involve an invariant complexification
\widetilde{X}
of
X
and an invariant normalization
(Y,\pi)
of
\widetilde{X}
. We also refer the reader to
[
Fn
,
Ga
]
for further information concerning
N(X)
. In addition, we analyze the corresponding concepts in the Nash setting.

Denote
\sigma:=\sigma_{n}:{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{n},\ z:=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\mapsto\overline{z}:=(\overline{z}_{1},\ldots,\overline{z}_{n})
the complex conjugation in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
. Recall that a subset
A\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
is invariant (under conjugation) if
\sigma_{n}(A)=A
and a function
F:\Omega\to{\mathbb{C}}
(defined on an open subset
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
) is invariant (under conjugation) if
\Omega
is invariant and
\overline{F\circ\sigma_{n}}=F
. We recall some concepts involved in the descriptions of the set
N(X)
of points of non-coherence and the set
T(X)
of ‘tails’ of a
C
-analytic set.

3.A.
Involved concepts for the analytic setting

We analyze first the concept of complexification of a
C
-analytic set
X
.

3.A.1.
Complexification of a
C
-analytic set

Let us recall briefly how the complexification of
X
is constructed. Consider the coherent sheaves of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n}}
-ideals
{\mathcal{I}}_{X}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}}
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. By
[
C2
, Prop.2]
there exists an open neighborhood
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and a coherent sheaf
{\mathcal{F}}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n}}
-ideals on
\Omega
such that
{\mathcal{F}}|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}={\mathcal{I}}_{X}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}}={\mathcal{I}}(X){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n}}
. Shrinking
\Omega
if necessary, we may assume that the support of
{\mathcal{F}}
is an invariant (under conjugation in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
) complex analytic set
\widetilde{X}\subset\Omega
such that its fixed part (with respect to conjugation in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
) is
X
. Recall that
\widetilde{X}
is called a complexification of
X
. By
[
C2
]
(see also
[
ABF5
, Thm.1.2.12]
) two complexifications of
X
coincide in an open neighborhood of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
and shrinking
\Omega
we may always assume that it is an invariant Stein open subset of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
and
\widetilde{X}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
is an invariant Stein complex analytic set.

Denote the sheaf of zero
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n}}
-ideals of
\widetilde{X}
with
{\mathcal{J}}_{\widetilde{X}}^{\mathbb{C}}
(we use the superindex
{\mathbb{C}}
to stress that we are considering a complex analytic set), that is,
{\mathcal{J}}_{\widetilde{X},x}^{\mathbb{C}}
is the ideal of complex analytic germs
f_{x}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},x}
such that
\widetilde{X}_{x}\subset\mathcal{Z}(f_{x})
. Denote
{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\widetilde{X}):=H^{0}(\Omega,{\mathcal{J}}_{\widetilde{X}}^{\mathbb{C}})
and as
\widetilde{X}
is a Stein complex analytic set, by Cartan’s Theorem A
{\mathcal{J}}_{\widetilde{X},x}^{\mathbb{C}}={\mathcal{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\widetilde{X}){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},x}
for each
x\in\widetilde{X}
. Observe that
\widetilde{X}\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}=X
, because
\widetilde{X}
is the support of
{\mathcal{F}}
and
{\mathcal{F}}|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}={\mathcal{I}}(X){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n}}|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
. As
\widetilde{X}
is invariant,
{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\widetilde{X})
is generated by invariant analytic functions that vanish identically on
X
. By Rückert’s Nullstellensatz
[
JP
, Thm.3.4.4]

{\mathcal{I}}(X){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},x}={\mathcal{F}}_{x}\subset\sqrt{{\mathcal{F}}_{x}}={\mathcal{J}}_{\widetilde{X},x}^{\mathbb{C}}={\mathcal{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\widetilde{X}){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},x}\subset{\mathcal{I}}(X){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},x}

for each
x\in X
. Consequently, all the previous inclusion are equalities

{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\widetilde{X}){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},x}={\mathcal{J}}_{\widetilde{X},x}^{\mathbb{C}}={\mathcal{I}}(X){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},x}={\mathcal{F}}_{x}

(3.1)

for each
x\in X
. This means in particular that
{\mathcal{F}}_{x}
is a radical ideal for each
x\in X
and that we may assume that the sheaf of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n}}
-ideals
{\mathcal{F}}
(chosen at the begining) is
{\mathcal{J}}_{\widetilde{X}}
.

Denote the structural sheaf of
\widetilde{X}
with
{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{X}}:={\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{J}}_{\widetilde{X}}^{\mathbb{C}}
and define
{\mathcal{O}}(\widetilde{X}):=H^{0}(\Omega,{\mathcal{O}}_{\Omega}/{\mathcal{J}}_{\widetilde{X}}^{\mathbb{C}})
and
{\mathcal{O}}(\Omega)=H^{0}(\Omega,{\mathcal{O}}_{\Omega})
. As
\widetilde{X}
is a Stein complex analytic set, Cartan’s Theorem B implies that
{\mathcal{O}}(\widetilde{X})={\mathcal{O}}(\Omega)/{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\widetilde{X})
.

We refer the reader to
[
ABF5
,
C2
,
T1
,
WB
]
for a deeper study of the complexification of a
C
-analytic set. A crucial property of the complexification of a
C
-analytic subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is the following.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a
C
-analytic set and let
\widetilde{X}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
be a complexification of
X
. Let
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be an analytic function such that
f|_{X}=0
. Let
F
be an invariant analytic extension of
f
to an invariant open neighborhood
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Then there exists an invariant open neighborhood
\Theta\subset\Omega
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
such that
F|_{\widetilde{X}\cap\Theta}=0
.

Proof.

Shrinking
\Omega
if necessary, we may assume
\widetilde{X}\cap\Omega
is a closed subset of
\Omega
. The complex analytic set
Z:=\{z\in\widetilde{X}\cap\Omega:\ F(z)=0\}
satisfies
{\mathcal{J}}_{Z,x}^{\mathbb{C}}={\mathcal{I}}(X){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}}={\mathcal{J}}_{\widetilde{X},x}^{\mathbb{C}}
for all
x\in X
, because
f=F|_{X}\in{\mathcal{I}}(X)
. Consequently, by Lemma
3.1
there exists an open neighborhood
\Theta\subset\Omega
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
such that
Z\cap\Theta=\widetilde{X}\cap\Theta
. Consequently,
F|_{\widetilde{X}\cap\Theta}=0
, as required.
∎

3.A.2.
Invariant normalization in the
C
-analytic setting

Let
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a
C
-analytic set. Denote the restriction to
\widetilde{X}
of the complex conjugation on
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
with
\sigma:\widetilde{X}\to\widetilde{X}
. It holds
d:=\dim(X)=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(\widetilde{X})
and
X=\widetilde{X}^{\sigma}:=\{x\in\widetilde{X}:\ \sigma(x)=x\}
. Let
\pi:Y\to\widetilde{X}
be the normalization of
\widetilde{X}
. As
\widetilde{X}
is Stein, also
Y
is Stein
[
N2
]
. Thus, the complex conjugation of
\widetilde{X}
extends to an anti-involution
\widehat{\sigma}
on
Y
that makes the following diagram commutative
[
GMT
, IV.3.10]

\textstyle{Y^{\widehat{\sigma}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi|_{Y^{\widehat{\sigma}}}}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{X}^{\sigma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{\widetilde{X}}

where
Y^{\widehat{\sigma}}:=\{y\in Y:\ \widehat{\sigma}(y)=y\}
is the set of fixed points of
\widehat{\sigma}
.

3.A.3.
Regular and singular points in the analytic setting

We say that
x
is a regular point of a
C
-analytic set
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
if
{\mathcal{O}}(X)_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{x}}
is a regular local ring (where
{\mathfrak{m}}_{x}
is the maximal ideal of
{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
associated to
x
). Pick a point
x
of
X
and assume for simplicity
x=0
. Thus,
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
coincides with the ring
{\mathbb{R}}\{{\tt x}\}
of real convergent power series in
n
variables. Its completion is the ring
{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]]
of real formal power series in
n
variables, which is also the completion of the local ring
{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{x}}
. The completions of the local rings
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}/{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}={\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}/({\mathcal{I}}(X){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x})
and
{\mathcal{O}}(X)_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{x}}=({\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})/{\mathcal{I}}(X))_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{x}}
are by
[
Mt
, (23.I) Thm.54, (23.L) Thm.55, Cor.3]
both isomorphic to
{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]]/({\mathcal{I}}(X){\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]])
. Let
\widetilde{X}
be a complexification of
X
. The completion of the local ring
{\mathcal{O}}(\widetilde{X})_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{x}}=({\mathcal{O}}(\Omega)/{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\widetilde{X}))_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{x}}
(where
{\mathfrak{n}}_{x}
is the maximal ideal of
{\mathcal{O}}(\Omega)
associated to
x
) is by
[
Mt
, (23.I) Thm.54, (23.L) Thm.55, Cor.3]
and (
3.1
)

{\mathbb{C}}[[{\tt x}]]/({\mathcal{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\widetilde{X}){\mathbb{C}}[[{\tt x}]])={\mathbb{C}}[[{\tt x}]]/({\mathcal{I}}(X){\mathbb{C}}[[{\tt x}]])=({\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]]/({\mathcal{I}}(X){\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]]))\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}},

whereas the completion of the local ring
{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{X},x}={\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},x}/{\mathcal{J}}_{\widetilde{X},x}^{\mathbb{C}}
is by
[
Mt
, (23.I) Thm.54, (23.L) Thm.55, Cor.3]
and (
3.1
)

{\mathbb{C}}[[{\tt x}]]/({\mathcal{J}}_{\widetilde{X},x}^{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{C}}[[{\tt x}]])={\mathbb{C}}[[{\tt x}]]/({\mathcal{I}}(X){\mathbb{C}}[[{\tt x}]])=({\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]]/({\mathcal{I}}(X){\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]]))\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}}.

Thus, the completions of the local rings
{\mathcal{O}}(\widetilde{X})_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{x}}=({\mathcal{O}}(\Omega)/{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\widetilde{X}))_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{x}}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{X},x}
coincide. In addition, they are obtained tensoring by
{\mathbb{C}}
the common completions of
{\mathcal{O}}(X)_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{x}}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}/{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}
.

All the local rings above are by
[
ABR
, Ch.VII.Ex.2.3]
excellent, so the inclusions of such local rings in their respective completions are faithfully flat, regular morphisms
[
Mt
, (33.A), (34.A)]
. In addition, the homomorphism
{\mathbb{R}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
is faithfully flat and regular. This means that if one of the excellent local rings in the following list

{\mathcal{O}}(X)_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{x}},\quad{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}/{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x},\quad{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{X},x},\quad{\mathcal{O}}(\widetilde{X})_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{x}}

is regular, then all the other are regular too, see
[
Mt
, (33.B), Lem.2, (33.E), Lem.4]
. Consequently, the set of regular points of
X
is
\operatorname{Reg}(X)=\operatorname{Reg}(\widetilde{X})\cap X
and the singular locus of
X
is
\operatorname{Sing}(X):=X\setminus\operatorname{Reg}(X)=\operatorname{Sing}(\widetilde{X})\cap X
. The connected components of the open subset
\operatorname{Reg}(X)
of
X
is a finite union of real analytic manifolds (possibly of different dimensions if they are more than one). As
\operatorname{Sing}(\widetilde{X})
is a complex analytic set of strictly smaller dimension than
\widetilde{X}
, we deduce
\operatorname{Sing}(X)
is a
C
-analytic set of strictly smaller dimension than
X
.

3.B.
Involved concepts for the Nash setting

In the Nash setting the constructions above are slightly more intrincate, because we need to take care of the semialgebraicity of the involved objects. We use Lemma
3.2
to guarantee the semialgebraicity of such constructions. Let
V\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be an algebraic set and let
{\mathcal{I}}^{*}(V)
be the ideal of all polynomials of
{\mathbb{R}}[{\tt x}]:={\mathbb{R}}[{\tt x}_{1},\ldots,{\tt x}_{n}]
that vanish identically on
V
. For each
x\in V
denote
{\mathfrak{m}}_{x}
the maximal ideal of
{\mathbb{R}}[{\tt x}]
constituted by all polynomials that vanish at
x
. We say that
V
is non-singular at
x\in V
if the local ring
({\mathbb{R}}[{\tt x}]/{\mathcal{I}}^{*}(V))_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{x}}
is regular (compare with §
3.A.3
and §
3.B.3
) and
V
is non-singular if
V
is non-singular at all its points. In the vein of
[
FGR
, Prop.2.8(i)]
we prove the following result concerning Nash zero ideals generated by polynomials in the non-singular algebraic case.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
V\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a non-singular algebraic set and let
M
be an open semialgebraic subset of
V
. Consider the open semialgebraic set
\Omega:={\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus(V\setminus M)
and let
W\subset\Omega
be an open semialgebraic neighborhood of
M
. Then
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(M)={\mathcal{I}}^{*}(V){\mathcal{N}}(W)
in
{\mathcal{N}}(W)
and
{\mathcal{I}}(M)={\mathcal{I}}^{*}(V){\mathcal{O}}(W)
in
{\mathcal{O}}(W)
. In addition,
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}_{M}={\mathcal{I}}^{*}(V){\mathcal{N}}_{W}
and
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}_{M}={\mathcal{I}}^{*}(V){\mathcal{O}}_{W}
.

Proof.

Observe that
M=\Omega\cap\operatorname{Cl}(M)
is a Nash submanifold of
\Omega
and a closed subset of
\Omega
, so it is a closed subset of
W
. We claim:
There exists a finite open semialgebraic covering
\{W_{k}\}_{k}
of
W
such that
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}_{M}|_{W_{k}}={\mathcal{I}}^{*}(V){\mathcal{N}}_{W}|_{W_{k}}
for each
k
. Thus,
{\mathcal{I}}^{*}(V)
generates
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}_{M}
as a finite sheaf of
{\mathcal{N}}_{W}
-ideals.

Let
P_{1},\ldots,P_{m}
be a system of generators of
{\mathcal{I}}^{*}(V)
in
{\mathbb{R}}[{\tt x}]
. Observe that
P_{1}^{2}+\cdots+P_{m}^{2}\in{\mathcal{I}}^{*}(V)
generates
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}_{M,x}
at the points
x\in W\setminus M
, so
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}_{M,x}={\mathcal{I}}^{*}(V){\mathcal{N}}_{W}
for each
x\in W\setminus M
. Thus, we only have to cover each connected component of
M
by finitely many open semialgebraic subsets of
W
. We assume, to lighten notations, that
M
is connected to prove the claim.

Denote
d:=\dim(M)
and for each set
I\subset\{1\leq i\leq m\}
of cardinality
n-d
define
W_{I}:=\{x\in W:\ {\rm rk}(\frac{\partial P_{i}}{\partial{\tt x}_{j}}(x))_{i\in I,1\leq j\leq n}=n-d\}
, which is an open semialgebraic subset of
W
, and
M_{I}=W_{I}\cap M
, which is an open semialgebraic subset of
M
(and consequently a Nash manifold of dimension
d
if it is non-empty). Observe that
M_{I}
is a closed subset of
W_{I}
. For each
I
such that
M_{I}\neq\varnothing
there exists by
[
BFR
, Thm.1.4]
a finite open semialgebraic covering
\{W_{Ik}\}_{k=1}^{p_{I}}
of
W_{I}
equipped with Nash diffeomorphisms
\phi_{Ik}:W_{Ik}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
that maps
M_{I}\cap W_{Ik}
onto the linear variety
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times\{0\}
for each
k=1,\ldots,p_{I}
. By the real Nullstellensatz for Nash functions
[
BCR
, Thm.8.6.5]
the ideal
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times\{0\})=\{x_{d+1},\ldots,x_{n}\}{\mathcal{N}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
, because the ideal in the righthand side is a real prime ideal whose zero set is
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times\{0\}
. Fix
I,k
and to lighten rotations write
N:=M_{Ik}
,
\psi:=(\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{n})=\phi_{Ik}
and
U:=W_{Ik}
. The ideal
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(N)
of
{\mathcal{N}}(U)
is generated by
\{\psi_{d+1},\ldots,\psi_{n}\}
. As
N
is a
d
-dimensional Nash manifold, the matrix
(\frac{\partial\psi_{\ell}}{\partial{\tt x}_{j}})_{d+1\leq\ell\leq n,1\leq j\leq n}
has rank
n-d
at each point of
N
. As
P_{i}|_{N}=0
for each
i\in I
, there exist
b_{i\ell}\in{\mathcal{N}}(U)
such that
P_{i}=\sum_{\ell=d+1}^{n}b_{i\ell}\psi_{\ell}
. Write
P:=(P_{i})_{i\in I}
,
B:=(b_{i\ell})_{i\in I,d+1\leq\ell\leq n}
,
\psi^{\prime}:=(\psi_{d+1},\ldots,\psi_{n})
and observe that
P^{t}=B\psi^{\prime t}
. We compute the Jacobian matrix of
P
using the latter formula and we obtain

\Big(\frac{\partial P_{i}}{\partial{\tt x}_{j}}(x)\Big)_{i\in I,1\leq j\leq n}=B(x)\Big(\frac{\partial\psi_{\ell}}{\partial{\tt x}_{j}}(x)\Big)_{d+1\leq\ell\leq n,1\leq j\leq n}

for each
x\in N
. As both matrices
(\frac{\partial P_{i}}{\partial{\tt x}_{j}}(x))_{i\in I,1\leq j\leq n}
and
(\frac{\partial\psi_{\ell}}{\partial{\tt x}_{j}}(x))_{d+1\leq\ell\leq n,1\leq j\leq n}
have rank
n-d
for each
x\in N
, we deduce
\det(B(x))\neq 0
for each
x\in N
. Define
U^{\prime}:=\{x\in U:\ \det(B(x))\neq 0\}
, which is an open semialgebraic neighborhood of
N
in
U
. Using Cramer solution to the system of linear equations
P_{i}=\sum_{\ell=d+1}^{n}b_{i\ell}\psi_{\ell}
where
i\in I
we find Nash functions
c_{\ell i}\in{\mathcal{N}}(U^{\prime})
such that
\psi_{\ell}=\sum_{i\in I}c_{\ell i}P_{i}
for
\ell=d+1,\ldots,n
. Thus, the ideal
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(N)
of
{\mathcal{N}}(U^{\prime})
is generated by
{\mathcal{I}}^{*}(V)
and we substitute
U
by
U^{\prime}
. As
M
is a coherent Nash subset of
W
, we have
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(N){\mathcal{N}}(U^{\prime})
generates by Remark
2.12

{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}_{M}
on
U^{\prime}
as a finite sheaf of
{\mathcal{N}}_{W}
-ideals, so
{\mathcal{I}}^{*}(V)
generates
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}_{M}
on
U^{\prime}
as a finite sheaf of
{\mathcal{N}}_{W}
-ideals.

Let us check
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(M)={\mathcal{I}}^{*}(V){\mathcal{N}}(W)
. Pick
f\in{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(M)
and consider the transporter sheaf of
{\mathcal{N}}_{W}
-ideals
({\mathcal{I}}^{*}(Z){\mathcal{N}}_{W}:f{\mathcal{N}}_{W})
, which is the sheaf of
{\mathcal{N}}_{W}
-ideals associated to the presheaf of
{\mathcal{N}}_{W}
-ideals
({\mathcal{I}}^{*}(Z){\mathcal{N}}_{W}(A):f{\mathcal{N}}_{W}(A))=\{g\in{\mathcal{N}}(A):\ gf\in{\mathcal{I}}^{*}(Z){\mathcal{N}}_{W}(A)\}
for each open set
A\subset W
. As
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}_{M}={\mathcal{I}}^{*}(V){\mathcal{N}}_{W}
as finite sheafs of
{\mathcal{N}}_{W}
-ideals, we deduce that
({\mathcal{I}}^{*}(Z){\mathcal{N}}_{W}:f{\mathcal{N}}_{W})={\mathcal{N}}_{W}
as finite sheaves of
{\mathcal{N}}_{W}
-ideals. By Theorem
2.7
(A)
({\mathcal{I}}^{*}(Z){\mathcal{N}}_{W}:f{\mathcal{N}}_{W})={\mathcal{N}}_{W}
is generated by its global sections
H^{0}(W,({\mathcal{I}}^{*}(Z){\mathcal{N}}_{W}:f{\mathcal{N}}_{W}))=H^{0}(W,{\mathcal{N}}_{W})
, so
f\in{\mathcal{I}}^{*}(Z)
. Thus,
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(M)={\mathcal{I}}^{*}(V){\mathcal{N}}(W)
. The remaining equalities in the statement follow straightforwardly from the previous one.
∎
