We analyze next the concept of (semialgebraic) complexification of a Nash set
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

3.B.1.
Semialgebraic complexification of a Nash set

Let
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a Nash set. Let
f_{1},\ldots,f_{p}
be a system of generators of
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X)
and consider the Nash map
f:=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{p}):{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
. Denote
{\tt x}:=({\tt x}_{1},\ldots,{\tt x}_{n})
and
{\tt y}:=({\tt y}_{1},\ldots,{\tt y}_{p})
. By Artin-Mazur description of Nash manifolds and Nash maps
[
BCR
, Thm.8.4.4]
there exists a non-singular algebraic set
V\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n+q}
of dimension
n
, a connected component
M
of
V
, a Nash diffeomorphism
\psi:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to M
and a polynomial map
g:=(g_{1},\ldots,g_{p}):V\to{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
such that
f=g\circ\psi
. In addition, if
\pi:{\mathbb{R}}^{n+q}={\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{q}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is the projection onto the first factor, then
\pi|_{V}=\psi^{-1}
.

Let
h_{1},\ldots,h_{k}
be a system of generators of the zero ideal of
V
in
{\mathbb{R}}[{\tt x},{\tt y}]
and let
\widetilde{V}
be the zero set in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n+q}
of the invariant polynomial extensions
H_{1},\ldots,H_{k}
of
h_{1},\ldots,h_{k}
to
{\mathbb{C}}^{n+q}
. Observe that
\widetilde{V}
is the Zariski closure of
V
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n+q}
. We consider the real underlying algebraic structures of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\equiv{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}
and
{\mathbb{C}}^{n+q}\equiv{\mathbb{R}}^{2(n+q)}
when referring to its semialgebraic subsets. The Nash diffeomorphism
\pi|_{V}:V\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
extends to an invariant complex polynomial map
\Pi:\widetilde{V}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
, which is a local diffeomorphism at the points of
V
. By
[
BFR
, Lem.9.2]
there exist an invariant open semialgebraic neighborhood
W_{1}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n+q}
of
V
and an invariant open semialgebraic neighborhood
W_{2}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
such that
\Pi|_{\widetilde{V}\cap W_{1}}:\widetilde{V}\cap W_{1}\to W_{2}
is a Nash diffeomorphism. Denote
\Psi:=(\Pi|_{\widetilde{V}\cap W_{1}})^{-1}:W_{2}\to\widetilde{V}\cap W_{1}
, which is a Nash extension of
\psi:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to M
to
W_{2}
.

Consider the invariant polynomial functions
G_{1},\ldots,G_{p}
that extend
g_{1},\ldots,g_{p}
to
{\mathbb{C}}^{n+q}
. Consider the Nash functions on
W_{2}
given by
F_{i}:=G_{i}\circ\Psi
and observe that
F_{i}|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}=f_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,p
. Denote
\widetilde{X}:=\mathcal{Z}(F_{1},\ldots,F_{p})\subset W_{2}
, which satisfies
\widetilde{X}\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}=X
. We have

{\mathcal{I}}_{X}={\mathcal{I}}(X){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}={\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{p}){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}.

In addition,
{\mathcal{I}}_{X}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}}=(F_{1},\ldots,F_{p}){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n}}
, so
\widetilde{X}
is the support of
{\mathcal{I}}_{X}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}}
around
X
. We conclude that
\widetilde{X}
is an invariant complexification of
X
and it is a semialgebraic subset of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
.

3.B.2.
Invariant normalization in the Nash setting: semialgebraic structure

For each
k\geq 1
we identify
{\mathbb{C}}^{k}
with its underlying real structure
{\mathbb{R}}^{2k}
when referring to its semialgebraic subsets and denote
\sigma_{k}:{\mathbb{C}}^{k}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{k}
the complex conjugation of
{\mathbb{C}}^{k}
. Let
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a Nash set. Then there exist
m\geq 0
, open semialgebraic subsets
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
,
\Theta\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n+m}
, complex analytic sets
\widetilde{X}\subset\Omega
and
Y\subset\Theta
such that:

(i)

\widetilde{X}
is an invariant complexification of
X
and it is a semialgebraic subset of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
.

(ii)

Y
is a semialgebraic subset of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n+m}
.

(iii)

\rho:Y\to\widetilde{X},\ (x,w)\mapsto x
is an invariant normalization of
\widetilde{X}
(with respect to the usual conjugation of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n+m}
), where
\rho
is the restriction to
Y
of the projection
P:{\mathbb{C}}^{n+m}={\mathbb{C}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{C}}^{m}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
onto the first factor. Observe that
\rho\circ\sigma_{n+m}=\sigma_{n}\circ\rho
.

Proof.

We keep all the notations of the previous paragraph §
3.B.1
. Define
Z
as the zero set in
V
of
g_{1},\ldots,g_{p}
, that is,
Z=\mathcal{Z}(g_{1},\ldots,g_{p},h_{1},\ldots,h_{k})
, and
X^{\prime}:=\psi(X)=M\cap Z
. As
\operatorname{Sing}(V)=\varnothing
, the complex algebraic set
\operatorname{Sing}(\widetilde{V})
does not meet
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+q}
and it is invariant under conjugation. Define
U:={\mathbb{R}}^{n+q}\setminus(V\setminus M)
, which is an open semialgebraic subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+q}
, because
M
is open in
V
and
V
is closed in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+q}
. Observe that
X^{\prime}\subset M\subset U
and both are closed in
U
. We claim:
g_{1},\ldots,g_{p},h_{1},\ldots,h_{k}
is a system of generators of
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X^{\prime})
in
{\mathcal{N}}(U)
.

Let
b\in{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X^{\prime})
and consider the restriction
b|_{M}
. As
f_{1},\ldots,f_{p}
is a system of generators of
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X)
in
{\mathcal{N}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
, we deduce that
g_{1},\ldots,g_{p}
is a system of generators of
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X^{\prime})
in
{\mathcal{N}}(M)
, so there exists Nash functions
a_{1},\ldots,a_{p}\in{\mathcal{N}}(M)
such that
b|_{M}=g_{1}a_{1}+\cdots+g_{p}a_{p}
. As
M
is a Nash manifold closed in
U
, there exists Nash functions
A_{1},\ldots,A_{p}\in{\mathcal{N}}(U)
such that
A_{i}|_{U}=a_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,r
. Thus,
b-A_{1}g_{1}-\cdots-A_{p}g_{p}=0
on
M
. As
V
is non-singular, there exist by Lemma
3.2
Nash functions
B_{1},\ldots,B_{k}\in{\mathcal{N}}(U)
such that
b=A_{1}g_{1}+\cdots+A_{p}g_{p}+B_{1}h_{1}+\cdots+B_{k}h_{k}
on
U
, so
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X^{\prime})=\{g_{1},\ldots,g_{p},h_{1},\ldots,h_{k}\}{\mathcal{N}}(U)
, as claimed.

Consequently,
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X^{\prime})={\mathcal{I}}^{*}(Z){\mathcal{N}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n+q})
, where
{\mathcal{I}}^{*}(Z)
is the zero ideal of
Z
in
{\mathbb{R}}[{\tt x},{\tt y}]
. The sheaf of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n}}
-ideals we use to construct the analytic complexification of
X^{\prime}
is
{\mathcal{I}}_{X^{\prime}}\otimes_{R}{\mathbb{C}}
, where
{\mathcal{I}}_{X^{\prime}}|_{U}={\mathcal{I}}(X^{\prime}){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n+q}}|_{U}={\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X^{\prime}){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n+q}}|_{U}={\mathcal{I}}^{*}(Z){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n+q}}|_{U}
.

Define
\widetilde{Z}:=\mathcal{Z}(G_{1},\ldots,G_{p},H_{1},\ldots,H_{k})
, which is the Zariski closure of
Z
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n+q}
. As
\widetilde{Z}
is the zero set of
{\mathcal{I}}^{*}(Z){\mathbb{C}}[{\tt x},{\tt y}]
, its zero ideal in
{\mathbb{C}}[{\tt x},{\tt y}]
is by Hilbert’s Nullstellensatz and
[
B
, Ch.V,​ §15,​ Prop.5]

{\mathcal{I}}^{*}(\widetilde{Z})={\mathcal{I}}^{*}(Z){\mathbb{C}}[{\tt x},{\tt y}]
. Thus,
({\mathcal{I}}_{X^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}})|_{U}={\mathcal{I}}^{*}(Z){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n+q}}|_{U}={\mathcal{I}}^{*}(\widetilde{Z}){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n+q}}|_{U}
.

The algebraic normalization
(Y_{1},\eta)
of
\widetilde{Z}
is a pair constituted by an invariant complex algebraic subset
Y_{1}
of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n+q+m}
for some
m\geq 1
and the restriction
\eta
to
Y_{1}
of the projection
H:{\mathbb{C}}^{n+q+m}={\mathbb{C}}^{n+q}\times{\mathbb{C}}^{m}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{n+q}
onto the first factor
[
FG
, Prop.3.11]
, which satisfies
\sigma_{n+q}\circ\eta=\eta\circ\sigma_{n+q+m}
. By Zariski Main Theorem
[
MO
, §V.6]

(Y_{1},\eta)
is the analytic normalization of
\widetilde{Z}
(see also
[
ABF4
, Thm.1.1 & §3.4]
).

We consider the real underlying algebraic structures of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n+q+m}\equiv{\mathbb{R}}^{2(n+q+m)}
,
{\mathbb{C}}^{n+q}\equiv{\mathbb{R}}^{2(n+q)}
,
Y_{1}
,
\widetilde{Z}
and
\eta
. As
\eta^{-1}(X^{\prime})
and
\eta^{-1}(V\setminus M)
are disjoint invariant closed semialgebraic subsets of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n+q+m}
, we deduce that
\Theta:={\mathbb{C}}^{n+q+m}\setminus\eta^{-1}(V\setminus M)
is an invariant open semialgebraic neighborhood of
\eta^{-1}(X^{\prime})
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n+q+m}
. In addition,
\Omega:={\mathbb{C}}^{n+q}\setminus(V\setminus M)
is an invariant open semialgebraic neighborhood of
X^{\prime}
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n+q}
. Let
\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}:=\widetilde{Z}\cap\Omega
, which is an open semialgebraic neighborhood of
X^{\prime}
in
\widetilde{Z}
and satisfies
\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n+q}=X^{\prime}
.

Let
\widetilde{X}^{\prime}\subset\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}
be an invariant analytic complexification of
X^{\prime}
. For each
x\in X^{\prime}
we have

({\mathcal{I}}_{X^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}})_{x}={\mathcal{I}}^{*}(\widetilde{Z}){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n+q},x}\subset{\mathcal{I}}(\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n+q},x}\subset{\mathcal{I}}(\widetilde{X}^{\prime}){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n+q},x}=({\mathcal{I}}_{X^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}})_{x}.

Thus,
({\mathcal{I}}_{X^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}})_{x}={\mathcal{I}}(\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n+q},x}
for each
x\in X^{\prime}
. Consequently,
\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}
is a semialgebraic invariant analytic complexification of
X^{\prime}
and
(Y_{1}^{\prime}:=\eta^{-1}(\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}),\eta^{\prime}:=\eta|_{Y_{1}^{\prime}})
is a semialgebraic invariant (analytic) normalization of
\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}
. Observe that
Y_{1}^{\prime}=Y_{1}\cap\Theta
.

Consider the composition
P:=\Pi\circ H:{\mathbb{C}}^{n+q+m}={\mathbb{C}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{C}}^{q+m}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
, which is the projection onto the first factor. Let
\widetilde{X}:=\mathcal{Z}(F_{1},\ldots,F_{p})\subset W_{2}
and recall that
\Psi|_{\widetilde{X}}:\widetilde{X}\to\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}\cap W_{1}
is a Nash diffeomorphism. Define
Y:=\eta^{-1}(\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}\cap W_{1})
and
\rho:=P|_{Y}
. It holds that
(Y,\rho)
is an invariant normalization of
\widetilde{X}
. In addition,
\widetilde{X}
is a semialgebraic subset of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
and
Y
is a semialgebraic subset of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n+q+m}
.
∎

3.B.3.
Regular and singular points in the Nash setting

We say that
x
is a regular point of a Nash set
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
if
{\mathcal{N}}(X)_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{x}}
is a regular local ring (where
{\mathfrak{n}}_{x}
is the maximal ideal of
{\mathcal{N}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
associated to
x
). Let us check that regular points in this case coincide with the regular points of
X
endowed with its analytic structure. Pick a point
x
of the Nash set
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and assume for simplicity
x=0
. Thus,
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
coincides with the ring
{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]]_{\rm alg}
of real algebraic power series in
n
variables
[
BCR
, Cor.8.1.6]
. Its completion is the ring
{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]]
of real formal power series in
n
variables, which is also the completion of the local ring
{\mathcal{N}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{x}}
. The completions of the local rings
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}/{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}_{X,x}={\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}/({\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X){\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x})
and
{\mathcal{N}}(X)_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{x}}=({\mathcal{N}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})/{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X))_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{x}}
are by
[
Mt
, (23.I) Thm.54, (23.L) Thm.55, Cor.3]
both isomorphic to
{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]]/({\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X){\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]])
.

If we consider
X
as a
C
-analytic set, the completions of the local excellent rings

\begin{cases}{\mathcal{O}}(X)_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{x}}=({\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})/{\mathcal{I}}(X))_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{x}}=({\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})/{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X){\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}))_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{x}},\\
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}/{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}={\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}/({\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}_{X,x}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x})={\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}/({\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x})\end{cases}

are by
[
Mt
, (23.I) Thm.54, (23.L) Thm.55, Cor.3]
both isomorphic to
{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]]/({\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X){\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]])
. This means that the completions of the rings
{\mathcal{N}}(X)_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{x}},{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}/{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}_{X,x},{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}
and
{\mathcal{O}}(X)_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{x}}
coincide.

All the local rings above are by
[
ABR
, Ch.VII.Ex.2.3]
excellent, so the inclusions of such local rings in their respective completions are faithfully flat, regular morphisms
[
Mt
, (33.A), (34.A)]
. This means that if one of the excellent local rings in the following list

{\mathcal{N}}(X)_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{x}},\quad{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}/{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}_{X,x},\quad{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x},\quad{\mathcal{O}}(X)_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{x}}

is regular, then all the others are regular as well, see
[
Mt
, (33.B), Lem.2]
and
[
Mt
, (33.E), Lem.4]
. Consequently, the regular (resp. singular) points of
X
are the same if we consider either the Nash structure or the analytic structure of the Nash set
X
. Using Jacobian criterion for Nash manifolds, we deduce that if
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a Nash set, then
\operatorname{Sing}(X)
is a Nash subset of
X
of strictly smaller dimension and
\operatorname{Reg}(X)=X\setminus\operatorname{Sing}(X)
is a semialgebraic subset of
X
.

3.C.
Set of ‘tails’ of a
C
-analytic set

Let us characterize, using the normalization of the complexification of a
C
-analytic set
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, the set
T(X):=\{x\in X:\ {\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}\neq{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}\}
of ‘tails’ of
X
, that is, the set of points of
X
where the sheaves
{\mathcal{J}}_{X}
and
{\mathcal{I}}_{X}
differ. We call
T(X)
in this way, because it generalizes the visible ‘tails’ of celebrated
C
-analytic sets like Cartan’s or Whitney’s umbrellas (even if in the general case the ‘tails’ of
X
could be hidden).

Remark 3.3
.

Let
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a Nash set. As the inclusion homomorphism
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
is by Corollary
2.9
faithfully flat,
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}={\mathcal{J}}^{\bullet}_{X,x}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
and
{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}={\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}_{X,x}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
for each
x\in X
, we deduce that
{\mathcal{J}}^{\bullet}_{X,x}\neq{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}_{X,x}
if and only if
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}\neq{\mathcal{I}}_{X}
. This means that
T(X)=\{x\in X:\ {\mathcal{J}}^{\bullet}_{X,x}\neq{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}_{X,x}\}
.
\sqbullet

We provide next a geometric property of the ‘tails’ of a
C
-analytic set.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a
C
-analytic set and let
\widetilde{X}
be an invariant complexification of
X
. Then for each
x\in T(X)
there exists an irreducible component
T_{x}
of
\widetilde{X}
(depending on
x
) such that
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(T_{x}\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n})<\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(T_{x})
.

Proof.

Pick a point
x\in X
. As
{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}\subsetneq{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}
, we also have
{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}_{\widetilde{X},x}={\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}}\subsetneq{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}}
(because the homomorphism
{\mathbb{R}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
is faithfully flat). The germ
Z_{x}:=\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}})
is the complexification of the germ
X_{x}=\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{J}}_{X,x})
and it holds
Z_{x}\neq\widetilde{X}_{x}
. Let
\widetilde{X}_{1,x},\ldots,\widetilde{X}_{s,x}
be the irreducible components of
\widetilde{X}_{x}
. The irreducible components of
X_{x}
are however by
[
N1
, Ch.V.Prop.2]
the intersections with
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{x}
of the irreducible components of
Z_{x}
. We have
Z_{x}\subsetneq\widetilde{X}_{x}
and
Z_{x}\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{x}=X_{x}=\widetilde{X}_{x}\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{x}
. Suppose that for each
j=1,\ldots,s
there exists an irreducible component
S_{j}
of
\widetilde{X}_{j,x}\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{x}
such that
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(S_{j})=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(\widetilde{X}_{j,x})
. Thus, as
\widetilde{X}_{j,x}
is irreducible,
\widetilde{X}_{j,x}
is the complexification of
S_{j}\subset X
, so
\widetilde{X}_{x}
is the complexification of
\bigcup_{j=1}^{s}S_{j}\subset X
. Consequently,
\widetilde{X}_{x}\subset Z_{x}
, which is a contradiction. Consequently, the statement holds, as required.
∎

Remark 3.5
.

We prove in Corollary
4.9
that this property characterizes the points of
T(X)
.
\sqbullet

Recall that a
C
-semianalytic subset
S
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a locally finite union in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
of basic
C
-semianalytic sets, that is, subsets of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
of the type
\{f=0,g_{1}>0,\ldots,g_{r}>0\}
where
r\geq 1
and
f,g_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
(see
[
ABF3
, §3]
). We will use the following remark freely along this article.

Remarks 3.6
.

(i) If
S\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a
C
-semianalytic set, there exists a countable subset
D\subset S
that is dense in
S
.

As
S
is a locally finite union of set of the type
T:=\{f=0,g_{1}>0,\ldots,g_{r}>0\}
, we deduce using an exhaustion of
R^{n}
by compact sets that the previous locally finite union is countable. Thus, it is enough to consider the case of basic
C
-semianalytic sets
T:=\{f=0,g_{1}>0,\ldots,g_{r}>0\}
. Denote
A:=\{g_{1}>0,\ldots,g_{r}>0\}
and
X:=\{f=0\}
, so
T=X\cap A
. As
T
is an open subset of
X
, if
D
is a dense subset of
X
, then
D\cap A
is a dense subset of
T
, because
\operatorname{Cl}(D\cap A)\cap A=\operatorname{Cl}(D)\cap A=T\cap A
. Consider the stratification of
X
(as a finite union of analytic manifolds) given by
X=\bigcap_{k\geq 0}\operatorname{Sing}_{k}(X)\setminus\operatorname{Sing}_{k+1}(X)
where
\operatorname{Sing}_{k}(X):=\operatorname{Sing}_{k-1}(\operatorname{Sing}(X))
and
\operatorname{Sing}_{0}(X):=X
. Thus, it is enough to prove that analytic manifolds admit dense countable subsets, which follows from their local structure diffeomorphic to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and their paracompactness.

(ii) In the Nash setting
C
-semianalytic sets correspond to semialgebraic sets. As semialgebraic sets admit finite stratification with strata Nash manifolds
[
BCR
, §9]
and Nash manifolds can be covered by finitely many open subsets that are Nash diffeomorphic to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, each semialgebraic set admit a dense countable subset.

\sqbullet

Lemma 3.7

(Description of tails)
.

Let
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a
C
-analytic set (resp. a Nash set) and
T(X):=\{x\in X:\ {\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}\neq{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}\}
. Let
\widetilde{X}
be an invariant Stein complexification of
X
that is a complex analytic subset of an invariant Stein open neighborhood
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and let
(Y,\pi)
be an invariant Stein normalization of
\widetilde{X}
endowed with an involution
\widehat{\sigma}
compatible with the complex conjugation
\sigma
of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
. Then

T(X)=\pi(\pi^{-1}(X)\setminus Y^{\widehat{\sigma}})\cup\pi(\operatorname{Sing}(Y^{\widehat{\sigma}})\setminus\operatorname{Cl}(Y^{\widehat{\sigma}}\setminus\operatorname{Sing}(Y^{\widehat{\sigma}})))

and it is a
C
-semianalytic set (resp. a semialgebraic set) of dimension
\dim(T(X))<\dim(X)
contained in
\operatorname{Sing}(X)
.

Proof.

Pick a point
x\in T(X)
. Write
\pi^{-1}(x)=\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}\}
and recall that
\pi(Y_{y_{1}}),\ldots,\pi(Y_{y_{s}})
are the irreducible components of
\widetilde{X}_{x}
. By Lemma
3.4
there exists an irreducible component
\pi(Y_{y_{j}})
of
\widetilde{X}_{x}
such that
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\pi(Y_{y_{j}})\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{x})<\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(\pi(Y_{y_{j}}))
. As
\pi
is a proper analytic map with finite fibers, we have
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\pi^{-1}(X)_{y_{j}})<\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(Y_{y_{j}})
. As
\pi\circ\widehat{\sigma}=\sigma\circ\pi
, we deduce
Y^{\widehat{\sigma}}_{y_{j}}\subset\pi^{-1}(X)_{y_{j}}
, so
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(Y^{\widehat{\sigma}}_{y_{j}})<\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(Y_{y_{j}})
. Thus, either
Y^{\widehat{\sigma}}_{y_{j}}=\varnothing
and
y_{j}\in\pi^{-1}(X)\setminus Y^{\widehat{\sigma}}
or
Y^{\widehat{\sigma}}_{y_{j}}\subset\operatorname{Sing}(Y^{\widehat{\sigma}})_{y_{j}}
, so
y_{j}\in\operatorname{Sing}(Y^{\widehat{\sigma}})\setminus\operatorname{Cl}(Y^{\widehat{\sigma}}\setminus\operatorname{Sing}(Y^{\widehat{\sigma}}))
. We conclude

x\in\pi(\pi^{-1}(X)\setminus Y^{\widehat{\sigma}})\cup\pi(\operatorname{Sing}(Y^{\widehat{\sigma}})\setminus\operatorname{Cl}(Y^{\widehat{\sigma}}\setminus\operatorname{Sing}(Y^{\widehat{\sigma}}))).

We prove next the converse. Let
x\in X
be such that there exists
y\in\pi^{-1}(X)\setminus Y^{\widehat{\sigma}}
with
\pi(y)=x
. We have
\pi(\widehat{\sigma}(y))=\sigma(\pi(y))=\sigma(x)=x
and
y\neq\widehat{\sigma}(y)
. Thus,
\pi(Y_{y})
and
\pi(Y_{\widehat{\sigma}(y)})
are two (different) irreducible components of
\widetilde{X}_{x}
that are mutually conjugated, because

\sigma(\pi(Y_{y}))=\pi(\widehat{\sigma}(Y_{y}))=\pi(Y_{\widehat{\sigma}(y)}).

The germ
Z_{x}:=\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}})
is the complexification of the germ
X_{x}=\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{J}}_{X,x})
. If no irreducible component of
Z_{x}
is contained in
\pi(Y_{y})
, then
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}}\neq{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}}
, so
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}\neq{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}
. Thus, suppose
Z_{1,x}
is an irreducible component of
Z_{x}
contained in
\pi(Y_{y})
and denote
X_{1,x}:=Z_{1,x}\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{x}
, which is by
[
N1
, Ch.V.Prop.2]
an irreducible component of
X_{x}
. As
X_{1,x}\subset\pi(Y_{y})\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{x}
,

X_{1,x}\subset\pi(Y_{y})\cap\pi(Y_{\widehat{\sigma}(y)})\subset\operatorname{Sing}(\widetilde{X})_{x},

so the complexification
Z_{1,x}
of
X_{1,x}
is contained in
\pi(Y_{y})\cap\pi(Y_{\widehat{\sigma}(y)})\subset\operatorname{Sing}(\widetilde{X})_{x}
. Thus,
Z_{x}\neq\widetilde{X}_{x}
and
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}\neq{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}
, so
x\in T(X)
.

Let
x\in X
be such that there exists
y\in\operatorname{Sing}(Y^{\widehat{\sigma}})\setminus\operatorname{Cl}(Y^{\widehat{\sigma}}\setminus\operatorname{Sing}(Y^{\widehat{\sigma}}))
with
\pi(x)=y
. Then
Y^{\widehat{\sigma}}_{y}=\operatorname{Sing}(Y^{\widehat{\sigma}})_{y}
and
\pi(Y_{y})
is an irreducible component of
\widetilde{X}_{x}
. As the restriction
\pi|_{Y\setminus\pi^{-1}(\operatorname{Sing}(\widetilde{X}))}:Y\setminus\pi^{-1}(\operatorname{Sing}(\widetilde{X}))\to\widetilde{X}\setminus\operatorname{Sing}(\widetilde{X})
is an analytic diffeomorphism and
\sigma\circ\pi=\pi\circ\widehat{\sigma}
, we deduce

\pi(Y_{y})\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{x}=\pi(Y_{y})\cap X_{x}\subset\operatorname{Sing}(\widetilde{X})_{x}\cup\pi(Y^{\widehat{\sigma}}_{y})=\operatorname{Sing}(\widetilde{X})_{x}\cup\pi(\operatorname{Sing}(Y^{\widehat{\sigma}})_{y})\subset\operatorname{Sing}(\widetilde{X})_{x}.

Thus, the complexification of
\pi(Y_{y})\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{x}
is contained in
\operatorname{Sing}(\widetilde{X})_{x}
, so
\pi(Y_{y})
is not the complexification of
\pi(Y_{y})\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{x}
. Consequently,
{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}\neq{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}
and
x\in T(X)
.

Observe that in both cases
x\in\operatorname{Sing}(\widetilde{X})
. Thus,
T(X)\subset\operatorname{Sing}(\widetilde{X})\cap X=\operatorname{Sing}(X)
and
\dim(T(X))\leq\dim(\operatorname{Sing}(X))<\dim(X)
.

The
C
-semianalyticity of
T(X)
follows from
[
ABF3
, Thm.1.1, Prop.3.5]
. We follow the description of
T(X)
, when
X
is a Nash set, and we observe that the involved objects are of semialgebraic nature: complexification (semialgebraic, see §
3.B.1
), normalization of the complexificacion (semialgebraic, see §
3.B.2
), sets of regular and singular points (semialgebraic, see §
3.B.3
). The remaining operations that appear in the construction of
T(X)
are: boolean operations, images under semialgebraic maps, inverse images under semialgebraic maps, topological closures, which are of semialgebraic nature. Thus,
T(X)
is a semialgebraic set, as required.
∎

3.D.
Description of the set of points of non-coherence.

Let
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a
C
-analytic set and define
N(X)
as the set of points
x\in X
such that
{\mathcal{J}}_{X}
is not of finite type at
x
(or equivalently, the analytic germ
X_{x}
is not coherent), that is, for each open neighborhood
U\subset X
of
x
the restriction sheaf
{\mathcal{J}}_{X}|_{U}
is not of finite type. We describe next the set
N(X)
of points of non-coherence of a
C
-analytic set
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
(following
[
ABF3
, §5]
) and then we show that it
X
is in addition a semialgebraic set, then
N(X)
is semialgebraic as well. It can be enlightening for the reader to have the following examples in mind when reading §
3.D.1
. The description of
N(X)
recalled bellow is crucial to prove Theorem
4.1
.

Examples 3.8
.

(i) The normalization of the complexification
\widetilde{W}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{3}
of Whitney’s umbrella
W:=\{y^{2}-zx^{2}=0\}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
is
\pi:{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\to\widetilde{W},\ (u,v)\mapsto(u,uv,v^{2})
and the inverse image
\pi^{-1}(W)={\mathbb{R}}^{2}\cup\{(0,\pm\sqrt{-1}w):\ w\in{\mathbb{R}}\}
.

(ii) The set of singular points of the complexification
\widetilde{X}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{4}
of the
3
-dimensional
C
-irreducible
C
-analytic hypersuface
X:=\{w^{2}-z(x^{2}+y^{2})=0\}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
is

\operatorname{Sing}(\widetilde{X})=\{x=0,y=0,w=0\}\cup\{x^{2}+y^{2}=0,z=0,w=0\}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{4},

which has codimension
2
in
\widetilde{X}
. The irreducible complex analytic hypersurface
\widetilde{X}
is by
[
O
]
normal (so it is locally irreducible), whereas
X
is non-coherent, because it has a visible ‘tail’ (the germs
X_{p}
at the points
p:=(0,0,z,0)\in X
with
z<0
have dimension
1
). We refer the reader to
[
T1
, Esempio, p. 211]
and
[
ABF3
, Ex.2.4]
for further details.

(iii) Let
X:=X_{1}\cup X_{2}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
where
X_{1}=\{(x^{2}-(z+1)y^{2})^{2}z-u^{2}=0\}
is a modified Whitney’s umbrella and
X_{2}:=\{u=0\}
. Then
N(X_{1})=\{x^{2}-y^{2}=0,z=0,u=0\}\cup\{(0,0,-1,0)\}
, whereas
N(X)=\{x^{2}-y^{2}=0,z=0,u=0\}
(see the concrete details in
[
ABF3
, Ex.5.7(ii)]
).

3.D.1.
Analytic setting

Let
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a
C
-analytic set. For each
\ell\geq 0
define
\operatorname{Sing}_{\ell}(X)
recursively as

\operatorname{Sing}_{\ell}(X):=\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{Sing}_{\ell-1}(X)),

where
\operatorname{Sing}_{0}(X):=X
. For each
k\in\{0,\ldots,d\}
let
{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}
be the family of all the
C
-analytic sets
Z\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
of dimension
k
that are a
C
-irreducible component of
\operatorname{Sing}_{\ell}(X)
for some
\ell\geq 0
. Define
Z_{k}:=\bigcup_{Z\in{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}}Z
and

\textstyle R_{k}:=\bigcup_{j=k+1}^{d}Z_{j,(j)}\quad\text{where}\quad Z_{j,(j)}:=\{z\in Z_{j}:\dim(Z_{j,z})=j\}=\operatorname{Cl}(Z_{j}\setminus\operatorname{Sing}(Z_{j})).

Let
\widetilde{Z}_{k}
be a complexification of
Z_{k}
and let
(Y_{k},\pi_{k})
be the normalization of
\widetilde{Z}_{k}
endowed with the anti-involution
\sigma_{k}:Y_{k}\to Y_{k}
that is induced by the usual conjugation on
\widetilde{Z}_{k}
. Let

Y_{k}^{\sigma_{k}}:=\{y\in Y_{k}:\ \sigma_{k}(y)=y\}

be the fixed part space of
Y_{k}
, which is a
C
-analytic space. Define

\displaystyle Y^{\sigma_{k}}_{k,(k)}:=\{y\in Y_{k}^{\sigma_{k}}:\dim_{{\mathbb{R}}}(Y_{k,y}^{\sigma_{k}})=k\}=\operatorname{Cl}(Y^{\sigma_{k}}_{k}\setminus\operatorname{Sing}(Y^{\sigma_{k}}_{k})),

\displaystyle C_{k,1}:=\pi_{k}^{-1}(Z_{k})\setminus Y_{k}^{\sigma_{k}},\quad C_{k,2}:=Y_{k}^{\sigma_{k}}\setminus Y^{\sigma_{k}}_{k,(k)},

\displaystyle A_{k,i}:=\operatorname{Cl}(C_{k,i})\cap\operatorname{Cl}(Y^{\sigma_{k}}_{k,(k)}\setminus\pi_{k}^{-1}(R_{k}))\quad\text{for $i=1,2$}.

We have:

Theorem 3.9

(
[
ABF3
, §5.1]
)
.

Let
N(X)
be the set of points of non-coherence of the
C
-analytic set
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
of dimension
d\geq 2
and let
N_{k}(Z_{k},R_{k}):=\pi_{k}(A_{k,1})\cup\pi_{k}(A_{k,2})
for
k\in\{2,\ldots,d\}
. Then,

(i)

N_{k}(Z_{k},R_{k})
is a
C
-semianalytic set of dimension
\leq k-2
.

(ii)

A point
x\in X
belongs to
N_{k}(Z_{k},R_{k})
if and only if the germ
Z_{k,x}
has a non-coherent irreducible component
T_{x}
of dimension
k
such that
\dim(T_{x}\setminus R_{k,x})=k
.

(iii)

\bigcup_{k=j}^{d}N_{k}(Z_{k},R_{k})
is the set of points of
X
such that the germ
X_{x}
has a non-coherent irreducible component of dimension
\geq j
.

(iv)

N(X)=\bigcup_{k=2}^{d}N_{k}(Z_{k},R_{k})
is a
C
-semianalytic subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

Remarks 3.10
.

(i) Each set
N_{k}(Z_{k},R_{k}):=\pi_{k}(A_{k,1})\cup\pi_{k}(A_{k,2})
is a closed subset of
X
(for
k=2,\ldots,d
), because each
A_{k,i}
is a closed subset of
Y_{k}
,
\pi_{k}:Y_{k}\to\widetilde{Z}_{k}
is a proper map and
Z_{k}
is a closed subset of both
X
and
\widetilde{Z}_{k}
.

(ii) For each open set
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
we have
N(X)\cap U=N(X\cap U)
, because coherence and non-coherence are local concepts. By Theorem
3.9
(i),(iv) we deduce
X\setminus N(X)
is dense in
X
.

(iii) Let
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a
C
-analytic subset and let
N(X)
be its set of points of non-coherence. Then
N(X)
is a closed subset of
X
and
X\setminus N(X)
is a coherent
C
-analytic subset of the open set
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X)
.
\hfill\sqbullet

3.D.2.
Nash setting

Let use show: The set
N(X)
of points of non-coherence of a Nash set
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a semialgebraic subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

Proof.

We follow the description of
N(X)
, when
X
is a Nash set, and we observe that the involved objects are of semialgebraic nature: complexification (semialgebraic, see §
3.B.1
), normalization of the complexificacion (semialgebraic, see §
3.B.2
), sets of regular and singular points (semialgebraic, see §
3.B.3
),
C
-analytic irreducible components of
X
are finitely many and coincide with the Nash irreducible components of
X
(see
[
FGR
, Prop.2.8(ii)]
).

The remaining operations that appear in the construction of
N(X)
are: boolean operations, images under semialgebraic maps, topological closures, considering sets of points of a certain dimension, and all of them are of semialgebraic nature. Thus,
N(X)
is a semialgebraic set.
∎

Remark 3.11
.

By Lemma
2.11
a Nash subset
X
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is coherent as an analytic set if and only if it is coherent as a Nash set. This means that the semialgebraic set
X\setminus N(X)
is a coherent Nash subset of the open semialgebraic set
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X)
.
\hfill\sqbullet

3.E.
Pure dimensional non-coherent examples

A general idea in Real Geometry is that non-coherence arises when the irreducible components of the objects are not pure dimensional. However, non-coherence appears also due to the presence of hidden ‘tails’ inside the irreducible components of the object. A first example of this phenomenon concerns the
C
-analytic set
X:=\{z(x+y)(x^{2}+y^{2})-x^{4}=0\}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
(see Figure
2
), which appears in
[
Ga
, Ex.1.9]
(see also
[
ABF5
, §5.3.1, p.197]
). It holds
\operatorname{Sing}(X)=\{x=0,y=0\}
,
N(X)=\{(0,0,0)\}
and
T(X)=\operatorname{Sing}(X)\setminus N(X)
. We leave the concrete details to the reader.

The previous situation forces us to distinguish in addition in the real analytic case between regular points and smooth points, something that in the complex analytic case never happens, because in such setting both concepts are equivalent. Let
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a
C
-analytic set (resp. Nash set) and let
x\in X
. We say that
x
is a smooth point of
X
if the analytic germ
X_{x}
coincides with the germ at
x
of a real analytic manifold. Denote
{\tt Smooth}(X)
the set of smooth points of
X
and
{\tt Non}\text{-}{\tt Smooth}(X):=X\setminus{\tt Smooth}(X)
. The set
{\tt Smooth}(X)
is always contained in
X\setminus N(X)
, because the germs at a point of a real analytic manifold are coherent. By the Jacobian criterion
\operatorname{Reg}(X)\subset{\tt Smooth}(X)
, but the inclusion can be strict (see Examples
3.13
and
3.14
). However, if
X
is coherent, the equality
\operatorname{Reg}(X)={\tt Smooth}(X)
holds.

Proof.

Suppose
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a coherent
C
-analytic set and pick a point
x\in{\tt Smooth}(X)
. Then, the analytic germ
X_{x}
is the germ at
x
of a real analytic manifold, so the quotient ring
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}/{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}
is a regular local ring. As
X
is coherent,
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}={\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}
, so
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}/{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}
is a regular local ring. Consequently,
x\in\operatorname{Reg}(X)
, as required.
∎

Example 3.12
.

If
X
is a
C
-analytic set (resp. a Nash set), the equality
\operatorname{Reg}(X)={\tt Smooth}(X)
does not guarantee that
X
is coherent. Let us modify the example that appeared in
[
Ga
, Ex.1.9]
(see also
[
ABF5
, §5.3.1, p.197]
). Consider
X:=\{z^{2}(x+y)^{2}(x^{2}+y^{2})-x^{6}=0\}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
(see Figure
2
). It holds
N(X)=\{(0,0,0)\}
,
\operatorname{Sing}(X)=\{x=0,yz=0\}
,
\operatorname{Reg}(X)={\tt Smooth}(X)
and
T(X)=\{x=0,y=0\}\setminus N(X)
. We leave the concrete details to the reader.
\sqbullet

Figure 1
.
X:z(x+y)(x^{2}+y^{2})-x^{4}=0

Figure 2
.
X:z^{2}(x+y)^{2}(x^{2}+y^{2})-x^{6}=0

We borrow the following enlightening pure dimensional example from
[
Fe2
, Ex.2.1]
.

Example 3.13

(Birdie non-coherent singularity)
.

Consider the algebraic set
X:=\{(x^{2}+zy^{2})x-y^{4}=0\}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. The set of regular points of
X
is
X\setminus\{x=0,y=0\}
, whereas:
The set of smooth points of
X
is
X\setminus\{x=0,y=0,z\leq 0\}
(see Figure
4
).

To prove that the points of the open half-line
\{x=0,y=0,z<0\}
are non-smooth we proceed by contradiction. Pick a point
p:=(0,0,-a^{2})\in\{x=0,y=0,z<0\}
and assume that it is smooth. As the line
\{x=0,y=0\}\subset X
, the vector
(0,0,1)
would be tangent to
X
at
p
, so the plane
z=-a^{2}
would be transversal to
X
at
p
. Thus, the intersection
X\cap\{z=-a^{2}\}
should be a curve that is smooth at
p
, but this is a contradiction, because such curve
\{(x^{2}-(ay)^{2})x-y^{4}=0,z=-a^{2}\}
has three tangent lines at
p
, which are those lines of equations
\{x-ay=0\}
,
\{x+ay=0\}
and
\{x=0\}
inside the plane
\{z=-a^{2}\}
. The origin cannot be a smooth point of
X
, because the set of smooth points of
X
is an open subset of
X
. Consequently, the set of non-smooth points of
X
contains the closed half-line
\{x=0,y=0,z\leq 0\}
.

Let us prove that the points of the open half-line
\{x=0,y=0,z>0\}
are smooth. To that end, observe that the map

\varphi:\{(s,u)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}:\ u>0\}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{3},\ (s,u)\mapsto((s^{2}+u)s^{2},(s^{2}+u)s,u)

is an analytic embedding whose image is
X\cap\{z>0\}
. Observe (computing the Jacobian matrix of
\varphi
) that
\varphi
is a local analytic diffeomorphism onto its image. The analytic map
\varphi
extends to a holomorphic map

\Phi:{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{3},\ (v,w)\to((v^{2}+w)v^{2},(v^{2}+w)v,w),

which provides the normalization of the complexification
\widetilde{X}:=\{(x^{2}+zy^{2})x-y^{4}=0\}
of
X
. To that end, observe
v=\frac{x}{y}
satisfies the integral equation
{\tt t}^{3}+z{\tt t}-y=0
(and in addition
z=w
,
y=v^{3}+zv
and
x=vy
).

We prove next that
N(X)=\{(0,0,0)\}
. As
\operatorname{Reg}(X)\subsetneq{\tt Smooth}(X)=X\setminus\{x=0,y=0,z\leq 0\}\subset X\setminus N(X)
, we have to check that the points of the open half-line
\{x=0,y=0,z<0\}
provide coherent analytic germs. Pick a point
(0,0,t)
with
t<0
. Then
\Phi^{-1}(\{(0,0,t)\})=\{(0,t),(\sqrt{-t},t),(-\sqrt{-t},t)\}
and

\displaystyle X_{(0,0,t)}

\displaystyle=\varphi({\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{(0,t)})\cup\varphi({\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{(\sqrt{-t},t)})\cup\varphi({\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{(-\sqrt{-t},t)}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}_{(0,0,t)}

\displaystyle=\Phi({\mathbb{C}}^{2}_{(0,t)})\cup\Phi({\mathbb{C}}^{2}_{(\sqrt{-t},t)})\cup\Phi({\mathbb{C}}^{2}_{(-\sqrt{-t},t)}).

Let
g\in{\mathcal{J}}_{X,(0,0,t)}
and let
G\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},(0,0,t)}
its natural holomorphic extension. As
g
vanishes identically on
\varphi({\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{(0,t)})
,
\varphi({\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{(\sqrt{-t},t)})
and
\varphi({\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{(-\sqrt{-t},t)})
, we deduce that
G
vanishes identically on
\Phi({\mathbb{C}}^{2}_{(0,t)})
,
\Phi({\mathbb{C}}^{2}_{(\sqrt{-t},t)})
and
\Phi({\mathbb{C}}^{2}_{(-\sqrt{-t},t)})
, because the latters are the complexifications of the analytic germs
\varphi({\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{(0,t)})
,
\varphi({\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{(\sqrt{-t},t)})
and
\varphi({\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{(-\sqrt{-t},t)})
. Thus,
G\in{\mathcal{J}}_{\widetilde{X},(0,0,t)}=((x^{2}+zy^{2})x-y^{4}){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},(0,0,t)}
, so
(x^{2}+zy^{2})x-y^{4}
divides
g
and
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,(0,0,t)}=((x^{2}+zy^{2})x-y^{4}){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},(0,0,t)}
. Consequently,
X_{(0,0,t)}
is a coherent analytic germ for each
t<0
.

We deduce also
T(X)=\operatorname{Sing}(X)\cap\{z<0\}
. The real parameter
s=\frac{x}{y}
is a solution of the integral equation
{\tt t}^{3}+z{\tt t}-y=0
. Using the solution general equation of degree
3
we find that

s=f(z,y):=\sqrt[3]{\frac{y}{2}+\sqrt{\frac{z^{3}}{27}+\frac{y^{2}}{4}}}+\sqrt[3]{\frac{y}{2}-\sqrt{\frac{z^{3}}{27}+\frac{y^{2}}{4}}}

As
x=ys
, we deduce that
F(x,y,z):=x-yf(y,z)
is an analytic equation of
X\setminus\{z\leq 0\}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\setminus\{z\leq 0\}
, which is the complement of a
C
-semianalytic set with no empty interior. The previous equation
F
generates the ideal
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,p}
for each
p\in X\setminus\{z\leq 0\}
. By Lemma
5.3
below there exists an analytic
h\in{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X))
such that
h_{x}
generates the ideal
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}
for each
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X)
. We have not found an explicit expression of such analytic equation defined on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X)
in terms of elementary analytic functions.

\sqbullet

Figure 3
.
X:(x^{2}+zy^{2})x-y^{4}=0

Figure 4
.
X:(x^{2}+z^{2}y^{2})x-y^{4}=0

Example 3.14

(Fake blanket)
.

Consider the algebraic set
X:=\{(x^{2}+z^{2}y^{2})x-y^{4}=0\}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. The set of regular points of
X
is
X\setminus\{x=0,y=0\}
, whereas:
The set of smooth points of
X
is
X\setminus\{(0,0,0)\}
(see Figure
4
).

To prove that the origin is a non smooth-point we proceed by contradiction. Assume it is a smooth point. As the line
\{x=0,y=0\}\subset X
, the vector
(0,0,1)
would be tangent to
X
at
p
, so the plane
z=0
would be transversal to
X
at
p
. Thus, the intersection
X\cap\{z=0\}
should be a curve that is smooth at
p
, but this is a contradiction, because such curve is
\{x^{3}-y^{4}=0,z=0\}
.

We prove next that the points of
X\setminus\{(0,0,0)\}
are smooth. To that end, observe that the map

\varphi:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{3},\ (s,u)\mapsto((s^{2}+u^{2})s^{2},(s^{2}+u^{2})s,u)

is an analytic embedding whose image is
X\setminus\{(0,0,0)\}
. In fact, the previous map extends to a homeomorphism between
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and
X
, whose inverse is the map

\psi:X\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2},\ (x,y,z)\mapsto\begin{cases}(\frac{x}{y},z)&\text{if $y\neq 0$},\\
(0,z)&\text{if $y=0$.}\end{cases}

As
X
is non-coherent, because
\operatorname{Reg}(X)\subsetneq{\tt Smooth}(X)
, and
X\setminus\{(0,0,0)\}\subset{\tt Smooth}(X)\subset X\setminus N(X)\subsetneq X
, we deduce
N(X)=\{(0,0,0)\}
. In addition,
T(X)=\operatorname{Sing}(X)\setminus N(X)
.

The analytic map
\varphi
extends to a holomorphic map

\Phi:{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{3},\ (v,w)\to((v^{2}+w^{2})v^{2},(v^{2}+w^{2})v,w),

which provides the normalization of the complexification
\widetilde{X}:=\{(x^{2}+zy^{2})x-y^{4}=0\}
of
X
. To that end, observe
v=\frac{x}{y}
satisfies the integral equation
{\tt t}^{3}+z^{2}{\tt t}-y=0
(an in addition
z=w
,
y=v^{3}+z^{2}v
and
x=vy
).

The real parameter
s=\frac{x}{y}
is a solution of the integral equation
{\tt t}^{3}+z^{2}{\tt t}-y=0
. Using the solution general equation of degree
3
we find that

s=f(z,y):=\sqrt[3]{\frac{y}{2}+\sqrt{\frac{z^{6}}{27}+\frac{y^{2}}{4}}}+\sqrt[3]{\frac{y}{2}-\sqrt{\frac{z^{6}}{27}+\frac{y^{2}}{4}}}

As
x=ys
, we deduce that
F(x,y,z):=x-yf(y,z)
is an analytic equation of
X\setminus\{z=0\}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\setminus\{z=0\}
, which is the complement in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
of a
C
-analytic set of dimension
2
. The previous equation
F
generates the ideal
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,p}
for each
p\in X\setminus\{z=0\}
. By Lemma
5.3
below there exists an analytic
h\in{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X))
such that
h_{x}
generates the ideal
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}
for each
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X)
. Again, we have not found an explicit expression of such analytic equation defined on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X)
in terms of elementary analytic functions.
\hfill\sqbullet

4.
Special properties of the set of points of non-coherence

We will keep the notations for the set of points of non coherence of a
C
-analytic set already introduced in Subsection
3.D
. The main result of this section is the following.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a
C
-analytic set (resp. a Nash set) such that
N(X)\neq\varnothing
. For each
x\in N(X)
there exists an analytic arc (resp. Nash arc)
\alpha:(-1,1)\to X
such that
\alpha(0)=x
and
\alpha((0,1))\subset T(X)\setminus N(X)
.

Before proving the previous result we present some consequences.

Corollary 4.2
.

Let
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a
C
-analytic set (resp. a Nash set). Then
\operatorname{Cl}(T(X))=\operatorname{Cl}(T(X)\setminus N(X))=T(X)\cup N(X)
.

Proof.

As
T(X)=\{x\in X:\ {\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}\neq{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}\}
and
N(X)
is the set of points
x\in X
such that for each open neighborhood
U\subset X
of
x
the restriction sheaf
{\mathcal{J}}_{X}|_{U}
is not of finite type, we deduce that
X\setminus(T(X)\cup N(X))
is the set of points
x\in X
such that there exists an open neighborhood
U^{x}\subset X
of
x
satisfying
{\mathcal{J}}_{X}|_{U^{x}}
is of finite type and
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}={\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}
. Thus, for each
x\in X
there exists a perhaps smaller open neighborhood
V^{x}\subset U^{x}
of
x
such that
{\mathcal{J}}_{X}|_{V^{x}}={\mathcal{I}}_{X}|_{V^{x}}
. Consequently,
X\setminus(T(X)\cup N(X))
is an open subset of
X
, so
T(X)\cup N(X)
is a closed subset of
X
. As
N(X)\subset\operatorname{Cl}(T(X)\setminus N(X))
(by Theorem
4.1
), we conclude
\operatorname{Cl}(T(X))\subset T(X)\cup N(X)=(T(X)\setminus N(X))\cup N(X)\subset\operatorname{Cl}(T(X)\setminus N(X))\subset\operatorname{Cl}(T(X))
. Thus, all the equalities in the statement hold, as required.
∎

Remarks 4.3
.

(i) The previous result implies
\dim(N(X)_{x})<\dim(T(X)_{x})
for each
x\in N(X)
.

(ii) If
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a
C
-analytic set, the set
N(X)
and
T(X)
may have non-empty intersection, even if
X
is
C
-irreducible. Consider for instance
X:=\{(x^{2}-y^{2}-x^{3})^{2}(y-x)-z^{2}=0\}
. We have
T(X)=\{x^{2}-y^{2}-x^{3}=0,x-y>0\}\cup\{(0,0,0)\}
, whereas
N(X)=\{(0,0,0)\}
.

Corollary 4.4
.

Let
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a
C
-analytic set (resp. a Nash set). The following conditions are equivalent:

(i)

X
is coherent.

(ii)

T(X)=\varnothing
.

(iii)

N(X)=\varnothing
.

Proof.

The implication (i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii) follows from Corollary
2.3
in the analytic case and from Corollary
2.11
in the Nash case. The implication (ii)
\Longrightarrow
(iii) follows from Corollary
4.2
, whereas the remaining (iii)
\Longrightarrow
(i) is a consequence of the definition of
N(X)
and of coherent sheaf of ideals.
∎

We prove next that each ‘tail’ of a
C
-analytic set is welded to
X
through
N(X)
.

Corollary 4.5
.

Let
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a
C
-analytic set and let
S
be a connected component of
\operatorname{Cl}(T(X))
. Then
S\cap N(X)\neq\varnothing
.

To lighten the proof of Corollary
4.5
we extract the following result.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{b}}
be ideals of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
. Then
({\mathfrak{a}}:{\mathfrak{b}})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}}=({\mathfrak{a}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}}:{\mathfrak{b}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}})
.

Proof.

We have to prove
({\mathfrak{a}}:{\mathfrak{b}}){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},x}=({\mathfrak{a}}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},x}:{\mathfrak{b}}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},x})
. If
f\in({\mathfrak{a}}:{\mathfrak{b}})
, then
f{\mathfrak{b}}\subset{\mathfrak{a}}
, so
f{\mathfrak{b}}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},x}\subset{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},x}
. Thus,
f\in({\mathfrak{a}}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},x}:{\mathfrak{b}}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},x})
and
({\mathfrak{a}}:{\mathfrak{b}}){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},x}\subset({\mathfrak{a}}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},x}:{\mathfrak{b}}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},x})
.

Let
f\in({\mathfrak{a}}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},x}:{\mathfrak{b}}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},x})
and write
f:=f_{1}+{\tt i}f_{2}
where
f_{1},f_{2}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
, see §
4.A
. If
h\in{\mathfrak{a}}
, then
(f_{1}+{\tt i}f_{2})h\in{\mathfrak{b}}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},x}
. As
{\mathfrak{b}}
is an ideal of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
, we have
(f_{1}-{\tt i}f_{2})h\in{\mathfrak{b}}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},x}
. Thus,
f_{1}h,f_{2}h\in{\mathfrak{b}}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},x}\cap{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}={\mathfrak{b}}
, so
f_{1},f_{2}\in({\mathfrak{a}}:{\mathfrak{b}})
and
f=f_{1}+{\tt i}f_{2}\in({\mathfrak{a}}:{\mathfrak{b}})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}}
, as required.
∎

We are ready to prove Corollary
4.5
.

Proof of Corollary
4.5
.

Suppose
S\cap N(X)=\varnothing
. Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be the sheaf of ideals on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
given by

{\mathcal{F}}_{x}:=\begin{cases}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}&\text{if $x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus S$}\\
({\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}:{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x})&\text{if $x\in S$}\end{cases}

As
T(X)
is a
C
-semianalytic set, also
\operatorname{Cl}(T(X))
is a
C
-semianalytic set. We deduce by
[
ABR
, Ch.VIII.Prop.3.1]

\operatorname{Cl}(T(X))
is locally connected, so its connected components are both open and closed subset of
T(X)
. As
({\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}:{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x})={\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
for each
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus\operatorname{Cl}(T(X))
and both
{\mathcal{J}}_{X}
and
{\mathcal{I}}_{X}
are coherent sheaves on
S
(because
S\cap N(X)=\varnothing
), we deduce by
[
G
, Ch.I.(6.12)]
that the transporter sheaf
({\mathcal{J}}_{X}:{\mathcal{I}}_{X})
is coherent on an open neighborhood of
S
. Consequently,
{\mathcal{F}}
is a coherent sheaf on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and its support
S\subset X
is by
[
C2
, Prop.15]
a
C
-analytic subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. As
S\subset\operatorname{Cl}(T(X))\subset\operatorname{Sing}(X)
, no
C
-irreducible component of
X
is contained in
S
. Let
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be the
C
-irreducible components of
X
. By
[
WB
, Prop.11]
there exist an open neighborhood
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and a complexification
\widetilde{X}
of
X
, which is an invariant complex analytic subset of
\Omega
, and its irreducible components
\{\widetilde{X}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
are the complexifications of the irreducible components of
X_{i}
. This property can be kept after shrinking suitably
\Omega
(if needed).

By
[
C2
, Prop.2 & 5]
the coherent sheaf of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n}}
-ideals
{\mathcal{F}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}}
extends to a coherent invariant sheaf of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n}}
-ideals
{\mathcal{G}}
on an invariant open Stein neighborhood of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
, which we may assume it is
\Omega
. By Lemma
4.6

{\mathcal{G}}_{x}=({\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}}:{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}})
for each
x\in S
. Thus,
{\mathcal{G}}_{x}\cdot({\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}})\subset{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}}={\mathcal{J}}_{\widetilde{X},x}^{\mathbb{C}}
(see §
3.A.1
), so
\widetilde{X}_{x}\subset\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{G}}_{x})\cup\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}})
for each
x\in S
.

The support of
{\mathcal{G}}
is a complex analytic subset
Z
of
\Omega
such that
Z\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}=S
. Pick a point
x_{0}\in S
. As
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x_{0}}\neq{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x_{0}}
, we also have
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x_{0}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\neq{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x_{0}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}={\mathcal{J}}_{\widetilde{X},x_{0}}^{\mathbb{C}}
, so there exists an irreducible component
X_{i}
of
X
such that
x_{0}\in X_{i}
and
\widetilde{X}_{i,x_{0}}\not\subset\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{J}}_{X,x_{0}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}})
. As
\widetilde{X}_{x_{0}}\subset\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{G}}_{x_{0}})\cup\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{J}}_{X,x_{0}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}})
, there exists an irreducible component
T_{x_{0}}
of
\widetilde{X}_{i,x_{0}}
such that
T_{x_{0}}\subset\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{G}}_{x_{0}})=Z_{x_{0}}
. As
\widetilde{X}_{i}
is irreducible and in particular pure dimensional, we deduce by the identity principle that
\widetilde{X}_{i}\subset Z
, so
X_{i}=\widetilde{X}_{i}\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\subset Z\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}=S
, which is a contradiction. Consequently,
S\cap N(X)\neq\varnothing
, as required.
∎

4.A.
Preliminary results

Before proving Theorem
4.1
we need some preliminary results. Let
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
be an invariant open set and let
F:\Omega\to{\mathbb{C}}
be a holomorphic function. Recall that
\overline{F\circ\sigma}:\Omega\to{\mathbb{C}}
is a holomorphic function on
\Omega
. Define

\Re(F):=\frac{F+\overline{F\circ\sigma}}{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\Im(F):=\frac{F-\overline{F\circ\sigma}}{2\sqrt{-1}}

the real and the imaginary part of
F
, which are invariant holomorphic functions on
\Omega
and satisfy
F=\Re(F)+\sqrt{-1}\Im(F)
. The same type of definitions hold for holomorphic germs at the points of
\Omega\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. If
Y_{x}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}_{x}
is a complex analytic germ, we denote
{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}_{Y,x}
the ideal of complex analytic germs
F_{x}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},x}
such that
Y_{x}\subset\mathcal{Z}(F_{x})
(see §
3.A.1
).

We say that
Y_{x}
is a complex Nash germ if there exists algebraic analytic function germs
F_{1,x},\ldots,F_{r,x}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},x}
such that
Y_{x}=\mathcal{Z}(F_{1,x},\ldots,F_{r,x})
. Recall that
F_{x}
is an algebraic analytic function germ if there exists a non-zero polynomial
P\in{\mathbb{C}}[{\tt x},{\tt y}]:={\mathbb{C}}[{\tt x}_{1},\ldots,{\tt x}_{n},{\tt y}]
such that
P({\tt x},f_{x})=0
. If
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, then
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}}
is the ring of complex algebraic analytic function germs at
x
. If
Y_{x}
is an invariant complex Nash germ,
Y_{x}\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{x}
is a Nash germ. In addition, if
{\mathfrak{a}}
is a radical ideal of
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
, then
\mathcal{Z}({\mathfrak{a}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}})
defines an invariant complex Nash germ at
x
. By means of Artin’s approximation theorem for the complex case one can imitate
[
BCR
, Cor.8.3.2 & Prop.8.6.9]
almost vervatim to show that the (analytic) irreducible components
Y_{1,x},\ldots,Y_{r,x}
of a complex Nash germs are again complex Nash germs. If
Y_{x}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}_{x}
is a complex Nash germ, we denote
{\mathcal{J}}^{{\mathbb{C}}\bullet}_{Y,x}
the ideal of complex Nash germs
F_{x}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},x}
such that
Y_{x}\subset\mathcal{Z}(F_{x})
(see §
3.A.1
).

4.A.1.
Local properties of the set of ‘tails’

We prove next the following result concerning the local behavior of the set of ‘tails’.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, let
{\mathfrak{a}}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
(resp.
{\mathfrak{a}}\subset{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
) be a radical ideal and define
X_{x}:=\mathcal{Z}({\mathfrak{a}})
. Suppose there exists a union of irreducible component
Z_{x}
of the invariant (complex) analytic set germ
Y_{x}:=\mathcal{Z}({\mathfrak{a}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}})
such that
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(Z_{x}\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{x})<\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(Z_{x})
. Then
{\mathfrak{a}}\subsetneq{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}
(resp.
{\mathfrak{a}}\subsetneq{\mathcal{J}}^{\bullet}_{X,x}
).

To lighten its proof we present before a preliminary result.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
A
be a commutative ring that is a
K
-algebra over a field
K
and let
{\mathfrak{a}}\subset A
be an ideal. Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in A
and assume that there exists a square matrix
M
of order
n
with coefficients in
K
such that
\det(M)\neq 0
and all the entries of
M(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})^{t}
belong to
{\mathfrak{a}}
. Then
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in{\mathfrak{a}}
.

Proof.

Write
M:=(m_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
and define
b_{i}:=m_{i1}a_{1}+\cdots+m_{in}a_{n}\in{\mathfrak{a}}
,
a:=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
and
b:=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n})
. Then
b^{t}=Ma^{t}
, so
a^{t}=M^{-1}b^{t}
and as
b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}\in{\mathfrak{a}}
, we conclude
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in{\mathfrak{a}}
, as required.
∎

We are ready to prove Lemma
4.7
.

Proof of Lemma
4.7
.

We prove first the
Analytic case
. The ideal
{\mathfrak{a}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}}
is by
[
B
, Ch.V,​ §15,​ Prop.5]
a radical ideal, so by Rückert’s Nullstellensatz
{\mathcal{J}}_{Y_{x},x}^{\mathbb{C}}={\mathfrak{a}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}}
. If
Y_{1,x},\ldots,Y_{r,x}
are the irreducible components of
Y_{x}
, we have
{\mathfrak{a}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}}=\bigcap_{i=1}^{r}{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}_{Y_{i},x}
. Denote
X_{i,x}:=Y_{i,x}\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. As
Z_{x}
is a union of irreducible components of
Y_{x}
and
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(Z_{x}\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{x})<\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(Z_{x})
, there exists
1\leq i_{0}\leq r
such that
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(X_{i_{0},x})<\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(Y_{i_{0},x})
. Let us prove:
There exists
h_{x}\in{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}
such that
\mathcal{Z}(h_{x})=X_{x}
and its invariant holomorphic extension
H_{x}
to
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}_{x}
does not belong to
{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}_{Y_{i_{0}},x}
.
Consequently,
h_{x}\in{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}\setminus{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}_{Y_{i_{0}},x}\subset{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}\setminus\bigcap_{i=1}^{r}{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}_{Y_{i},x}={\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}\setminus({\mathfrak{a}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}})={\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}\setminus{\mathfrak{a}}
.

Let
{\mathfrak{b}}:={\mathcal{J}}_{X_{i_{0}},x}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}}
, which is by
[
B
, Ch.V,​ §15,​ Prop.5]
a radical ideal. Define
V_{i_{0},x}:=\mathcal{Z}({\mathfrak{b}})
and as
{\mathfrak{b}}
is a radical ideal, we deduce by Rückert’s Nullstellensatz
{\mathcal{J}}_{V_{i_{0}},x}^{\mathbb{C}}={\mathfrak{b}}
. Observe that
V_{i_{0},x}
is invariant (because
{\mathcal{J}}_{V_{i_{0}},x}^{\mathbb{C}}={\mathfrak{b}}
admits a system of invariant generators). By
[
N1
, IV.1.Prop.3]

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(V_{i_{0},x})=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(X_{i_{0},x})<\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(Y_{i_{0},x})
, so
{\mathcal{J}}_{Y_{i_{0}},x}^{\mathbb{C}}\subsetneq{\mathcal{J}}_{V_{i_{0}},x}^{\mathbb{C}}={\mathfrak{b}}
. Let
H_{i_{0},x}^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{b}}\setminus{\mathcal{J}}_{Y_{i_{0}},x}^{\mathbb{C}}
. As
V_{i_{0},x}
is invariant, also
\overline{H_{i_{0},x}^{\prime}\circ\sigma}\in{\mathfrak{b}}
, so
\Re(H_{i_{0},x}^{\prime}),\Im(H_{i_{0},x}^{\prime})\in{\mathfrak{b}}
. As
H_{i_{0},x}^{\prime}=\Re(H_{i_{0},x}^{\prime})+\sqrt{-1}\Im(H_{i_{0},x}^{\prime})
, we conclude that either
\Re(H_{i_{0},x}^{\prime})\not\in{\mathcal{J}}_{Y_{i_{0}},x}^{\mathbb{C}}
or
\Im(H_{i_{0},x}^{\prime})\not\in{\mathcal{J}}_{Y_{i_{0}},x}^{\mathbb{C}}
, so after substituting
H_{i_{0},x}^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{b}}
by either
\Re(H_{i_{0},x}^{\prime})
or
\Im(H_{i_{0},x}^{\prime})
, we may assume
H_{i_{0},x}^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{b}}\setminus{\mathcal{J}}_{Y_{i_{0}},x}^{\mathbb{C}}
is in addition invariant. Let
H_{i_{0},x}^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathfrak{b}}
be invariant such that
\mathcal{Z}(H_{i_{0},x}^{\prime\prime})\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{x}=X_{i_{0},x}
(it is enough to consider the sum of squares of a finite system of invariant generators of
{\mathfrak{b}}
) and pick a positive
\rho\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
H_{i_{0},x}:=H_{i_{0},x}^{\prime 2}+\rho H_{i_{0},x}^{\prime\prime 2}\not\in{\mathcal{J}}_{Y_{i_{0}},x}^{\mathbb{C}}
(to prove the existence of
\rho
use Lemma
4.8
applied to a
2\times 2
invertible matrix whose rows are of the type
(1,\rho_{i})
where
\rho_{i}>0
is a positive real number for
i=1,2
). Thus,
h_{i_{0},x}:=H_{i_{0},x}|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{x}}\in{\mathfrak{b}}\cap{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}={\mathcal{J}}_{X_{i_{0}},x}
,
\mathcal{Z}(h_{i_{0},x})=X_{i_{0},x}
and
H_{i_{0},x}\not\in{\mathcal{J}}_{Y_{i_{0}},x}^{\mathbb{C}}
.

For each
i=1,\ldots,r
such that
X_{i,x}\subset X_{i_{0},x}
define
h_{i,x}:=h_{i_{0},x}
, whereas if
X_{i,x}\not\subset X_{i_{0},x}
we claim:
There exists
h_{i,x}\in{\mathcal{J}}_{X_{i},x}\setminus{\mathcal{J}}_{Y_{i_{0}},x}^{\mathbb{C}}
such that
\mathcal{Z}(h_{i,x})=X_{i,x}
.

Pick a system of generators
G_{1,x},\ldots,G_{s,x}
of
{\mathcal{J}}_{Y_{i},x}^{\mathbb{C}}
and observe that

X_{i,x}\subset\mathcal{Z}(\overline{G_{1,x}\circ\sigma},\ldots,\overline{G_{s,x}\circ\sigma})=\sigma(Y_{i,x}).

Consequently,

\begin{split}X_{i,x}&=Y_{i,x}\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{x}=Y_{i,x}\cap\sigma(Y_{i,x})\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{x}=\mathcal{Z}(G_{1,x},\ldots,G_{s,x},\overline{G_{1,x}\circ\sigma},\ldots,\overline{G_{s,x}\circ\sigma})\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{x}\\
&=\mathcal{Z}(\Re(G_{1,x}),\ldots,\Re(G_{s,x}),\Im(G_{1,x}),\ldots,\Im(G_{s,x}))\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{x}.\end{split}

(4.1)

Choose positive real numbers
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{s},\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{s}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
H_{i,x}:=\sum_{k=1}^{s}\lambda_{k}\Re(G_{k,x})^{2}+\mu_{k}\Im(G_{k,x})^{2}\not\in{\mathcal{J}}_{Y_{i_{0}},x}^{\mathbb{C}}
. Such positive real numbers exist, because otherwise by Lemma
4.8
(applied to a
(2s)\times(2s)
invertible matrix whose coefficients are all real and strictly positive)
\Re(G_{k,x}),\Im(G_{k,x})\in{\mathcal{J}}_{Y_{i_{0}},x}
for
k=1,\ldots,s
, so
{\mathcal{J}}_{Y_{i},x}^{\mathbb{C}}\subset{\mathcal{J}}_{Y_{i_{0}},x}^{\mathbb{C}}
, which is a contradiction, because
i\neq i_{0}
(recall that
X_{i,x}\not\subset X_{i_{0},x}
) and both
Y_{i,x}
and
Y_{i_{0},x}
are irreducible components of
Y_{x}
.

As
X_{i,x}=\mathcal{Z}(\Re(G_{1,x}),\ldots,\Re(G_{s,x}),\Im(G_{1,x}),\ldots,\Im(G_{s,x}))\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{x}
(see (
4.1
)), we deduce
X_{i,x}=\mathcal{Z}(h_{i,x})
, where
h_{i,x}:=H_{i,x}|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{x}}\in{\mathcal{J}}_{X_{i},x}\setminus{\mathcal{J}}_{Y_{i_{0}},x}^{\mathbb{C}}
, as claimed.

By construction
H_{i,x}\not\in{\mathcal{J}}_{Y_{i_{0}},x}^{\mathbb{C}}
for
i=1,\ldots,r
. As
{\mathcal{J}}_{Y_{i_{0}},x}^{\mathbb{C}}
is a prime ideal, we conclude
H_{x}:=\prod_{i=1}^{r}H_{i,x}\not\in{\mathcal{J}}_{Y_{i_{0}},x}^{\mathbb{C}}
, whereas
h_{x}:=H_{x}|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{x}}\in\bigcap_{i=1}^{r}{\mathcal{J}}_{X_{i},x}={\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}
and
\mathcal{Z}(h_{x})=\mathcal{Z}(h_{1,x}\cdots h_{r,x})=\bigcup_{i=1}^{r}X_{i,x}=X_{x}
, as required.

The proof in the
Nash case
follows
vervatim
after changing
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
by
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
,
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n},x}
by
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}}
,
{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}_{T,x}
by
{\mathcal{J}}^{{\mathbb{C}}\bullet}_{T,x}
(if
T
is a complex Nash set) and
{\mathcal{J}}_{S,x}
by
{\mathcal{J}}_{S,x}^{\bullet}
(if
S
is a Nash set).
∎

We provide an alternative geometric characterization of the set of ‘tails’ of a
C
-analytic set.

Corollary 4.9

(Geometric characterization of the set of ‘tails’)
.

Let
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a
C
-analytic set and let
\widetilde{X}
be a complexification of
X
. Then
x\in T(X)
if and only if there exists an irreducible component
T_{x}
of
\widetilde{X}_{x}
(depending on
x
) such that
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(T_{x}\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{x})<\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(T_{x})
.

Proof.

The only if implication follows from Lemma
3.4
, whereas the if implication follows from Lemma
4.7
applied to
{\mathfrak{a}}={\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}
.
∎

4.A.2.
Dimensional formula

A subset
S
of an open set
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a semianalytic set if for each point
x\in\Omega
there exists an open neighborhood
U^{x}
such that
S\cap U^{x}
is a finite union of sets of the type
\{f=0,g_{1}>0,\ldots,g_{r}>0\}\subset U^{x}
where
f,g_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}(U^{x})
are analytic functions on
U^{x}
. We recall the following result concerning dimensions of certain differences of semianalytic sets.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be an open set and let
A,B\subset\Omega
be semianalytic sets. Then
\dim(\operatorname{Cl}(A\setminus B)\cap B)<\dim(A\setminus B)
.

Proof.

By
[
Ł
, §17.Prop.5, p.60]
it holds
\dim(\operatorname{Cl}(A\setminus B)\setminus(A\setminus B))<\dim(A\setminus B)
. As

\operatorname{Cl}(A\setminus B)\setminus(A\setminus B)=(\operatorname{Cl}(A\setminus B)\cap B)\cup(\operatorname{Cl}(A\setminus B)\setminus A),

we deduce
\dim(\operatorname{Cl}(A\setminus B)\cap B)\leq\dim(\operatorname{Cl}(A\setminus B)\setminus(A\setminus B))<\dim(A\setminus B)
, as required.
∎

We are ready to prove Theorem
4.1
.

4.B.
Proof of Theorem
4.1

We prove both the analytic and the Nash cases (pointing out the differences in the second case with respect to the first). We keep all the notations introduced in §
3.D
for the description of the set of points on non-coherence of a
C
-analytic set. The proof is conducted in several steps:

Step 1.
Initial preparation. Define

\displaystyle e:=

\displaystyle\max\{\dim(N_{k}(Z_{k},R_{k})):\ k=2,\ldots,d\}=\dim(N(X)),

\displaystyle\ell_{e}:=

\displaystyle\max\{k=2,\ldots,d:\ \dim(N_{k}(Z_{k},R_{k}))=e\}.

As
N(X)\neq\varnothing
, both integers exist. By Theorem
3.9

N_{\ell_{e}}(Z_{\ell_{e}},R_{\ell_{e}})\setminus\bigcup_{k={\ell_{e}}+1}^{d}N_{k}(Z_{k},R_{k})
is the set of points
z\in X
such that
X_{z}
has a non-coherent irreducible component of dimension
{\ell_{e}}
and all the irreducible components of
X_{z}
of higher dimension are coherent. As coherence and non-coherence are properties of local nature,
N(X\cap U)=N(X)\cap U
for each open set
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. We have
\dim(N_{k}(Z_{k},R_{k}))<\dim(N_{\ell_{e}}(Z_{\ell_{e}},R_{\ell_{e}}))
for each
k={\ell_{e}}+1,\ldots,d
, so
S_{e,{\ell_{e}}}:=N_{\ell_{e}}(Z_{\ell_{e}},R_{\ell_{e}})\setminus\bigcup_{k={\ell_{e}}+1}^{d}N_{k}(Z_{k},R_{k})\neq\varnothing
and has dimension
e
. Let
S_{e,{\ell_{e}}}^{*}
be the set of points of dimension
e
of
S_{e,{\ell_{e}}}
, which is a dense subset of the set of points of dimension
e
of
N_{\ell_{e}}(Z_{\ell_{e}},R_{\ell_{e}})
.

Step 2.
Pick a point
x\in S_{e,{\ell_{e}}}^{*}
and observe that the
C
-semianalytic germ
N_{\ell_{e}}(Z_{\ell_{e}},R_{\ell_{e}})_{x}
has dimension
e
. Let
X_{1,x},\ldots,X_{s,x}
be the irreducible components of
X_{x}
. We may assume:

•

X_{1,x},\ldots,X_{p,x}
have dimension
{\ell_{e}}
.

•

X_{p+1,x},\ldots,X_{m,x}
have dimension
<{\ell_{e}}
.

•

X_{m+1,x},\ldots,X_{s,x}
have dimension
>{\ell_{e}}
.

By
[
ABR
, Ch.VIII.Prop.3.1]
there exist: (1)
\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{n}>0
such that
x\in U^{x}:=\prod_{i=1}^{n}(-\varepsilon_{i},\varepsilon_{i})
and (2) irreducible
C
-analytic sets
X_{i}\subset U^{x}
such that
X_{i}
is a representative of
X_{i,x}
and
\dim(X_{i})=\dim(X_{i,x})
for
i=1,\ldots,r
. In case
X
is a Nash set, the
X_{i}
are also semialgebraic, so they are irreducible Nash sets (Lemma
2.6
). As each
N_{k}(Z_{k},R_{k})
is by Remark
3.10
(i) closed, we may assume
N_{k}(Z_{k},R_{k})\cap U^{x}=\varnothing
for
k={\ell_{e}}+1,\ldots,d
. This means that

N(X\cap U^{x})=N(X)\cap U^{x}=\bigcup_{k=2}^{\ell_{e}}N_{k}(Z_{k},R_{k})\cap U^{x}.

We assume in addition
X_{k}
is coherent in
U^{x}
for
k=m+1,\ldots,r
. Let
V_{0}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
be an invariant open (semialgebraic) neighborhood of
U^{x}
such that
g_{1},\ldots,g_{r}
admit invariant holomorphic extensions
G_{1},\ldots,G_{r}\in{\mathcal{O}}(V_{0})
to
V_{0}
.

Consider the Nash diffeomorphism

\varphi:U^{x}=(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\ (y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})\mapsto\Big(\frac{y_{1}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon_{1}^{2}-y_{1}^{2}}},\ldots,\frac{y_{n}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}^{2}-y_{n}^{2}}}\Big).

As
\varphi
is a local Nash diffeomorphism on an invariant open semialgebraic neighborhood of
B_{n}(0,1)
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\equiv{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}
, there exists by
[
BFR
, Lem.9.2]
invariant open semialgebraic neighborhoods
V\subset V_{0}
of
B_{n}(0,1)
and
W\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\equiv{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
such that
\varphi
extends to an invariant analytic (in fact invariant complex Nash) diffeomorphism
\Phi:V\to W
. Using
\Phi
we may assume
U^{x}={\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. After our preliminary reductions
N_{k}(Z_{k},R_{k})=\varnothing
for
k={\ell_{e}}+1,\ldots,d
, so
N(X)=\bigcup_{k=2}^{\ell_{e}}N_{k}(Z_{k},R_{k})
.

If
z\in N_{\ell_{e}}(Z_{\ell_{e}},R_{\ell_{e}})
, then
X_{z}
has a non-coherent irreducible component of dimension
{\ell_{e}}
. As
X_{m+1},\ldots,X_{s}
are coherent and the irreducible
C
-analytic components of
X
of dimension
{\ell_{e}}
are
X_{1},\ldots,X_{p}
, we deduce
N_{\ell_{e}}(Z_{\ell_{e}},R_{\ell_{e}})\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{p}N_{\ell_{e}}(X_{i},\varnothing)
. Consequently,

N_{\ell_{e}}(Z_{\ell_{e}},R_{\ell_{e}})=\bigcup_{i=1}^{p}(N_{\ell_{e}}(Z_{\ell_{e}},R_{\ell_{e}})\cap N_{\ell_{e}}(X_{i},\varnothing))\\
\leadsto\ N_{\ell_{e}}(Z_{\ell_{e}},R_{\ell_{e}})_{x}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{p}(N_{\ell_{e}}(Z_{\ell_{e}},R_{\ell_{e}})_{x}\cap N_{\ell_{e}}(X_{i},\varnothing)_{x}).

We may assume
x\in N_{\ell_{e}}(Z_{\ell_{e}},R_{\ell_{e}})\cap N_{\ell_{e}}(X_{1},\varnothing)
and
\dim(N_{\ell_{e}}(Z_{\ell_{e}},R_{\ell_{e}})_{x}\cap N_{\ell_{e}}(X_{1},\varnothing)_{x})=e
, so
\dim(N_{\ell_{e}}(X_{1},\varnothing)_{x})\geq e
.

Step 3.
Construction of the path
\alpha
. Let us recall (and simplify) how to construct
N_{\ell_{e}}(X_{1},\varnothing)
. Write
Z=X_{1}
. As
Z
is an irreducible
C
-analytic set of dimension
{\ell_{e}}
, we may assume that the complexification
\widetilde{Z}
of
Z
is irreducible. Let
\pi:Y\to\widetilde{Z}
be the normalization of
\widetilde{Z}
endowed with an anti-involution
\sigma:Y\to Y
, which is induced by the usual conjugation on
\widetilde{Z}
. Let
Y^{\sigma}:=\{y\in Y:\ \sigma(y)=y\}
be the fixed part of
Y
, which is a
C
-analytic space (and in addition semialgebraic in some
{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
if
X
is a Nash set). Define

\displaystyle Y^{\sigma}_{({\ell_{e}})}:=\operatorname{Cl}(Y^{\sigma}\setminus\operatorname{Sing}(Y^{\sigma})),

\displaystyle C_{1}:=\pi^{-1}(Z)\setminus Y^{\sigma},\quad C_{2}:=Y^{\sigma}\setminus Y^{\sigma}_{({\ell_{e}})},

\displaystyle A_{i}:=\operatorname{Cl}(C_{i})\cap Y^{\sigma}_{({\ell_{e}})}

It holds
N_{\ell_{e}}(Z,\varnothing)=\pi(A_{1})\cup\pi(A_{2})
. We consider here the real analytic structure of
\pi:Y\to\widetilde{Z}
to give meaning to the structure of
C
-semianalytic set of
\pi^{-1}(X)
and to compute (real) dimensions of the involved objects. As
\pi
is a proper map with finite fibers, we have

0\leq e\leq\dim(N_{\ell_{e}}(Z,\varnothing))=\max\{\dim(\pi(A_{1})),\dim(\pi(A_{2}))\}=\max\{\dim(A_{1}),\dim(A_{2})\}

As
Y^{\sigma}_{({\ell_{e}})}
is a closed subset of
Y^{\sigma}
, we have

\displaystyle\dim(A_{1})

\displaystyle=\dim(\operatorname{Cl}(C_{1})\cap\operatorname{Cl}(Y^{\sigma}_{({\ell_{e}})}))\leq\dim(\operatorname{Cl}(\pi^{-1}(Z)\setminus Y^{\sigma})\cap Y^{\sigma}),

\displaystyle\dim(A_{2})

\displaystyle=\dim(\operatorname{Cl}(C_{2})\cap\operatorname{Cl}(Y^{\sigma}_{({\ell_{e}})}))=\dim(\operatorname{Cl}(Y^{\sigma}\setminus Y^{\sigma}_{({\ell_{e}})})\cap Y^{\sigma}_{({\ell_{e}})}).

By Lemma
4.10
and as
\pi
is an analytic proper map with finite fibers, we have

\displaystyle\dim(\pi(A_{1}))=\dim(A_{1})

\displaystyle\leq\dim(\operatorname{Cl}(\pi^{-1}(Z)\setminus Y^{\sigma})\cap Y^{\sigma})<\dim(\pi^{-1}(Z)\setminus Y^{\sigma}),

(4.2)

\displaystyle\dim(\pi(A_{2}))=\dim(A_{2})

\displaystyle=\dim(\operatorname{Cl}(Y^{\sigma}\setminus Y^{\sigma}_{({\ell_{e}})})\cap Y^{\sigma}_{({\ell_{e}})})<\dim(Y^{\sigma}\setminus Y^{\sigma}_{({\ell_{e}})}).

(4.3)

Thus, either
\pi^{-1}(Z)\setminus Y^{\sigma}
or
Y^{\sigma}\setminus Y^{\sigma}_{({\ell_{e}})}
has dimension
>e
.

As
x\in N_{\ell_{e}}(Z,\varnothing)=\pi(A_{1})\cup\pi(A_{2})
and
\dim(N_{\ell_{e}}(Z,\varnothing)_{x})\geq e
, there exists a point
w\in A_{1}\cup A_{2}
such that
\pi(w)=x
and
\dim(A_{1,w}\cup A_{2,w})=\dim(N_{\ell_{e}}(Z,\varnothing)_{x})\geq e
(recall that
\pi
is an analytic proper map with finite fibers). Using (
4.2
) and (
4.3
) we deduce that either
\dim(\pi^{-1}(Z)_{w}\setminus Y^{\sigma}_{w})>e
or
\dim(Y^{\sigma}_{w}\setminus Y^{\sigma}_{({\ell_{e}}),w})>e
. As
\dim(N(X))=e
and
\pi
is an analytic proper map with finite fibers,
\pi^{-1}(N(X))
is a
C
-semianalytic set of dimension
e
. Thus, either
\dim((\pi^{-1}(Z)_{w}\setminus Y^{\sigma}_{w})\setminus\pi^{-1}(N(X))_{w})>e\geq 0
or
\dim((Y^{\sigma}_{w}\setminus Y^{\sigma}_{({\ell_{e}}),w})\setminus\pi^{-1}(N(X))_{w})>e\geq 0
. By the analytic curve selection lemma
[
ABR
, Rem.VII.4.3(b)]
(in the analytic case) or the Nash curve selection lemma
[
BCR
, Prop.8.1.13]
(in the Nash case) there exists an analytic arc (resp. Nash arc)
\beta:(-1,1)\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
such that
\beta(0)=x
and either
\beta((0,1))\subset(\pi^{-1}(Z)\setminus Y^{\sigma})\setminus\pi^{-1}(N(X))
or
\beta((0,1))\subset(Y^{\sigma}\setminus Y^{\sigma}_{({\ell_{e}})})\setminus\pi^{-1}(N(X))
. Define
\alpha:(-1,1)\to X_{1},\ t\mapsto(\pi\circ\beta)(t)
, which is an analytic arc (resp. Nash arc) such that
\alpha(0)=x
and
\alpha((0,1))\subset T(X_{1})\setminus N(X)
(Lemma
3.7
), so
{\mathcal{I}}_{X_{1},\alpha(t)}\subsetneq{\mathcal{J}}_{X_{1},\alpha(t)}
for each
t\in(0,1)
. We claim:
{\mathcal{I}}_{X,\alpha(t)}\subsetneq{\mathcal{J}}_{X,\alpha(t)}
for each
t\in(0,1)
, so
\alpha((0,1))\subset T(X)\setminus N(X)
.

As
{\mathcal{I}}_{X,\alpha(t)}\subset\bigcap_{i=1}^{s}{\mathcal{I}}_{X_{i},\alpha(t)}
and
\bigcap_{i=1}^{s}{\mathcal{J}}_{X_{i},\alpha(t)}={\mathcal{J}}_{X,\alpha(t)}
, it is enough to check
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,\alpha(t)}\setminus{\mathcal{I}}_{X_{1},\alpha(t)}
for each
t\in(0,1)
. As
{\mathcal{I}}_{X_{1},\alpha(t)}\subsetneq{\mathcal{J}}_{X_{1},\alpha(t)}
, there exists by Lemma
4.9
an irreducible component
T_{t,\alpha(t)}
of
\widetilde{X}_{1,\alpha(t)}
such that
\dim(T_{t,\alpha(t)}\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{\alpha(t)})<\dim(T_{t,\alpha(t)})
for each
t\in(0,1)
. By Lemma
4.7
we have
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,\alpha(t)}\setminus{\mathcal{I}}_{X_{1},\alpha(t)}\neq\varnothing
for each
t\in(0,1)
, as claimed.

We have proved that
S_{e,{\ell_{e}}}^{*}\subset\operatorname{Cl}(T(X)\setminus N(X))
, so
\operatorname{Cl}(S_{e,{\ell_{e}}}^{*})\subset\operatorname{Cl}(T(X)\setminus N(X))
. Thus, for each
x\in\operatorname{Cl}(S_{e,{\ell_{e}}}^{*})
there exists by the analytic curve selection lemma
[
ABR
, Rem.VII.4.3(b)]
(in the analytic case) or the Nash curve selection lemma
[
BCR
, Prop.8.1.13]
(in the Nash case) there exists an analytic arc
\gamma:(-1,1)\to X
such that
\gamma(0)=x
and
\gamma((0,1))\subset T(X)\setminus N(X)
.

Consider the
C
-analytic subset
X^{\prime}:=X\setminus\operatorname{Cl}(S_{e,{\ell_{e}}}^{*})
of
\Omega:={\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus\operatorname{Cl}(S_{e,{\ell_{e}}}^{*})
and observe that
N(X^{\prime})=N(X)\setminus\operatorname{Cl}(S_{e,{\ell_{e}}}^{*})
and
T(X^{\prime})\subset T(X)\setminus\operatorname{Cl}(S_{e,{\ell_{e}}}^{*})
, because
\widetilde{X^{\prime}}\subset\widetilde{X}\setminus\operatorname{Cl}(S_{e,{\ell_{e}}}^{*})
. Denote

\displaystyle e^{\prime}:=

\displaystyle\max\{\dim(N_{k}(Z_{k},R_{k})):\ k=2,\ldots,d\}=\dim(N(X^{\prime})),

\displaystyle\ell_{e^{\prime}}:=

\displaystyle\max\{k=2,\ldots,d:\ \dim(N_{k}(Z_{k},R_{k}))=e^{\prime}\}

and observe that
e^{\prime}\leq e
and
\ell_{e^{\prime}}<\ell_{e}
if
e^{\prime}=e
, because
S_{e,{\ell_{e}}}^{*}
is dense in the set of points of dimension
e
of
N_{\ell_{e}}(Z_{\ell_{e}},R_{\ell_{e}})
. Thus, by induction hypothesis on the pair
(e,\ell_{e})
for each
x\in N(X)\setminus\operatorname{Cl}(S_{e,{\ell_{e}}}^{*})
there exists an analytic arc
\gamma:(-1,1)\to X
such that
\gamma(0)=x
and
\gamma((0,1))\subset T(X^{\prime})\setminus N(X^{\prime})\subset T(X)\setminus N(X)
(because
N(X)=N(X^{\prime})\cup\operatorname{Cl}(S_{e,{\ell_{e}}}^{*})
), as required.
∎

5.
Special global equations outside the set of points of non coherence

The purpose of this section is to find global equations of a
C
-analytic set
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
outside the set
N(X)
of points of non coherence that encodes the differential property of the points of
T(X)\setminus N(X)
.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a
C
-analytic set (resp. Nash set) and let
N(X)
be its set of points of non-coherence. Then there exists
h\in{\mathcal{I}}(X\setminus N(X))
(resp.
h\in{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X\setminus N(X))
) such that
\mathcal{Z}(h)=X\setminus N(X)
and
h_{x}\in{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}\setminus{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}
for each
x\in T(X)\setminus N(X)
.

The previous function
h
is not unique, however we will see below (Theorem
5.3
) that under certain hypothesis (
X
is either a
C
-analytic locally hypersurface or a Nash locally hypersurface) there is an
h
satisfying the conditions of Theorem
5.1
such that all the others are multiples of such
h
. To lighten the proof of Theorem
5.1
we need the following preliminary result, whose proof is inspired by
[
ABF2
, Lem.4.1]
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
be an invariant open Stein set and let
{\mathcal{G}}
be a coherent invariant sheaf of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\Omega}
-ideals on
\Omega
. Let
\{H_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}\subset H^{0}(\Omega,{\mathcal{G}})
be a sequence. There exists positive real numbers
\nu_{j}>0
for each
j\geq 1
such that: if
0<\mu_{j}\leq\nu_{j}
and
p_{j}\geq 1
is a positive integer for each
j\geq 1
, then
H:=\sum_{j\geq 1}\mu_{j}^{2p_{j}}H_{k}^{2p_{j}}\in H^{0}(\Omega,{\mathcal{G}})
.

Proof.

Let
\{L_{\ell}\}_{\ell\geq 1}
be an exhaustion of
\Omega
by compact sets. Define
\lambda_{j}:=\max_{L_{j}}\{|H_{j}|^{2}\}+1
and
\nu_{j}:=1/(\sqrt{2^{j}\lambda_{j}})
. Fix
0<\mu_{j}\leq\nu_{j}
and a positive integer
p_{j}\geq 1
for each
j\geq 1
. Consider the series
H:=\sum_{j\geq 1}\mu_{j}^{2p_{j}}H_{j}^{2p_{j}}
. We claim:
H
converges uniformly on the compact subsets of
\Omega
.

Indeed, let
L\subset\Omega
be a compact set and observe that there exists an index
\ell_{0}\geq 1
such that
L\subset L_{\ell}
for all
\ell\geq\ell_{0}
. Moreover, for each
z\in L
we have
\mu_{\ell}^{2}|H_{\ell}(z)|^{2}\leq\nu_{\ell}^{2}|H_{\ell}(z)|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2^{\ell}}<1
, so
\mu_{\ell}^{2p_{j}}|H_{\ell}(z)|^{2p_{j}}\leq\mu_{\ell}^{2}|H_{\ell}(z)|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2^{\ell}}
if
\ell\geq\ell_{0}
. Thus,

\Big|\sum_{\ell\geq\ell_{0}}\mu_{\ell}^{2p_{j}}H_{\ell}^{2p_{j}}(z)\Big|\leq\sum_{\ell\geq\ell_{0}}\mu_{\ell}^{2p_{j}}|H_{\ell}(z)|^{2p_{j}}\leq\sum_{\ell\geq\ell_{0}}\frac{1}{2^{\ell}}=\frac{1}{2^{\ell_{0}-1}}\leq 1

for each
z\in L
, as claimed.

Denote
S_{m}:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu_{j}^{2p_{j}}H_{j}^{2p_{j}}\in H^{0}(\Omega,{\mathcal{G}})
and observe
H:=\sum_{j\geq 1}\mu_{j}^{2p_{j}}H_{j}^{2p_{j}}=\lim_{m\to\infty}S_{m}
in the Fréchet topology of
H^{0}(\Omega,{\mathcal{G}})
. As
H^{0}(\Omega,{\mathcal{G}})
is by
[
C1
, VIII.Thm.11, pag.62]
a closed ideal of
H^{0}(\Omega,{\mathcal{O}}_{\Omega})
, we conclude
H\in H^{0}(\Omega,{\mathcal{G}})
, as required.
∎

We denote
{\mathbb{C}}\{{\tt t}\}
the ring of convergent series in one variable with coefficients in
{\mathbb{C}}
and let
\omega:{\mathbb{C}}\{{\tt t}\}\to{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{+\infty\},\ f:=\sum_{k\geq 0}a_{k}{\tt t}^{k}\mapsto\omega(f):=\inf\{k\geq 0:\ a_{k}\neq 0\}
the function order in
{\mathbb{C}}\{{\tt t}\}
. We are ready to prove Theorem
5.1
.

Proof of Theorem
5.1
.

We prove both cases separately:

Analytic Case
. Consider the coherent sheaf of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n}}
-ideals
{\mathcal{F}}:={\mathcal{J}}_{X}|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X)}\otimes_{R}{\mathbb{C}}
on the open set
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X)
. By
[
C2
, Prop.2 & 5]
the coherent sheaf of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n}}
-ideals
{\mathcal{F}}
extends to a coherent invariant sheaf of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n}}
-ideals
{\mathcal{G}}
on an invariant open Stein neighborhood
\Omega
of
U
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
. Let
\{L_{\ell}\}_{\ell\geq 1}
be an exhaustion of
\Omega
by compact sets. As
{\mathcal{G}}
is invariant and coherent, Cartan’s Theorem A implies that there exists a countable collection of invariant holomorphic sections
\{H_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}\subset H^{0}(\Omega,{\mathcal{G}})
such that for each
\ell\geq 1
there exists
j(\ell)
so that the analytic germs
H_{1,z},\ldots,H_{j(\ell),z}
generate the ideal
{\mathcal{G}}_{z}
for each
z\in L_{\ell}
.

Let
D:=\{x_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}\subset T(X)
be a countable dense subset of
T(X)
. For each
k\geq 1
we have
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x_{k}}\setminus{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x_{k}}\neq\varnothing
, so
({\mathcal{J}}_{X,x_{k}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}})\setminus{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}_{\widetilde{X},x_{k}}\neq\varnothing
(because
{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x_{k}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}}={\mathcal{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}_{\widetilde{X},x_{k}}
). Thus,
\widetilde{X}_{x_{k}}\setminus\mathcal{Z}(\{H_{j}:\ j\geq 1\})\neq\varnothing
for each
k\geq 1
. By the curve selection lemma
[
ABR
, Rem.VII.4.3(b)]
(using the underlying real structures of all the involved complex analytic sets) there exists an analytic curve
\gamma_{k}:(-1,1)\to\widetilde{X}
such that
\gamma_{k}(0)=x_{k}
and
\gamma_{k}((0,1))\subset\widetilde{X}\setminus\mathcal{Z}(\{H_{j}:\ j\geq 1\})
for each
k\geq 1
.

For each
k\geq 1
let
j_{k}\geq 1
be such that the analytic series
H_{j_{k}}\circ\gamma_{k}\in{\mathbb{C}}\{{\tt t}\}\setminus\{0\}
. Denote
A_{k}:=H_{j_{k}}
for each
k\geq 1
. We claim:
There exists
H_{0}\in H^{0}(\Omega,{\mathcal{G}})
such that
H_{0}\circ\gamma_{k}\in{\mathbb{C}}\{{\tt t}\}\setminus\{0\}
for each
k\geq 1
,
h_{0}:=H_{0}|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X)}\geq 0
and
X\setminus N(X)\subset\mathcal{Z}(h_{0})
.

Denote
p_{1}:=1
and for each
k\geq 2
let
p_{k}\geq 1
be such that
\omega(A_{k}^{2p_{k}}\circ\gamma_{j})>\omega(A_{j}^{2p_{j}}\circ\gamma_{j})
for
1\leq j\leq k-1
. By Lemma
5.2
there exists positive real numbers
\nu_{k}>0
for each
k\geq 1
such that if
0<\mu_{k}\leq\nu_{k}
for each
k\geq 1
, then
H_{0}:=\sum_{k\geq 1}\mu_{k}^{2p_{k}}A_{k}^{2p_{k}}\in H^{0}(\Omega,{\mathcal{G}})
. We choose inductively each
0<\mu_{k}\leq\nu_{k}
such that
\omega(\sum_{k=1}^{m}\mu_{k}^{2p_{k}}(A_{k}^{2p_{k}}\circ\gamma_{j}))\leq\omega(A_{j}^{2p_{j}}\circ\gamma_{j})<+\infty
for each
1\leq j\leq m
and each
m\geq 1
. We conclude
\omega(H_{0}\circ\gamma_{j})\leq\omega(A_{j}^{2p_{j}}\circ\gamma_{j})<+\infty
for each
j\geq 1
. Thus,
H_{0}\in H^{0}(\Omega,{\mathcal{G}})
,
H_{0}\circ\gamma_{k}\in{\mathbb{C}}\{{\tt t}\}\setminus\{0\}
for each
k\geq 1
,
h_{0}:=H_{0}|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X)}\geq 0
(because it is a sum of squares of invariant analytic functions) and
X\setminus N(X)\subset\mathcal{Z}(h_{0})
(because
H_{0}\in H^{0}(\Omega,{\mathcal{G}})
), as claimed.

By Lemma
5.2
there exist positive real numbers
\lambda_{j}>0
such that
H^{\prime}:=\sum_{j\geq 1}\lambda_{j}^{2}H_{j}^{2}\in H^{0}(\Omega,{\mathcal{G}})
. For each
j\geq 1
denote
h_{j}:=\lambda_{j}H_{j}|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X)}
and write
h^{\prime}:=H^{\prime}|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X)}=\sum_{j\geq 1}h_{j}^{2}
. It holds:
{\mathcal{Z}}(h^{\prime})=X\setminus N(X)
.

Indeed,

{\mathcal{Z}}(h^{\prime})=\bigcap_{j\geq 1}{\mathcal{Z}}(h_{j})=\bigcap_{j\geq 1}({\mathcal{Z}}(H_{j})\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n})=\Big(\bigcap_{j\geq 1}{\mathcal{Z}}(H_{j})\Big)\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}=\operatorname{supp}({\mathcal{G}})\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}=X\setminus N(X).

For each
k\geq 1
let
m_{k}\geq 1
be an integer such that the tuple

(H_{0}\circ\gamma_{k},H^{\prime}\circ\gamma_{k})\in\{{\tt t}^{m_{k}}\}{\mathbb{C}}\{{\tt t}\}^{2}\setminus\{{\tt t}^{m_{k}+1}\}{\mathbb{C}}\{{\tt t}\}^{2}

and define
