w_{k}:=(\frac{H_{1}\circ\gamma_{k}}{{\tt t}^{m_{k}}},\frac{H^{\prime}\circ\gamma_{k}}{{\tt t}^{m_{k}}})\in{\mathbb{C}}\{{\tt t}\}^{2}\setminus\{{\tt t}\}{\mathbb{C}}\{{\tt t}\}^{2}
, which satisfies
w_{k}(0):=(w_{1k},w_{2k})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
. By Baire’s Theorem there exists positive real numbers
\lambda_{0},\lambda^{\prime}>0
such that
\lambda_{0}w_{1k}+\lambda^{\prime}w_{2k}\neq 0
for each
k\geq 1
. Consequently,
H:=\lambda_{0}H_{0}+\lambda^{\prime}H^{\prime}
satisfies that

\frac{H\circ\gamma_{k}}{{\tt t}^{m_{k}}}=\lambda_{0}\frac{H_{1}\circ\gamma_{k}}{{\tt t}^{m_{k}}}+\lambda^{\prime}\frac{H^{\prime}\circ\gamma_{k}}{{\tt t}^{m_{k}}}\in{\mathbb{C}}\{{\tt t}\}\setminus\{{\tt t}\}{\mathbb{C}}\{{\tt t}\}

for each
k\geq 1
. Thus,
H\circ\gamma_{k}\in{\mathbb{C}}\{{\tt t}\}\setminus\{0\}
for each
k\geq 1
and we define
h:=H|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X)}
, which is a positive semidefinite analytic function on
X\setminus N(X)
. We conclude
h_{x_{k}}\not\in{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x_{k}}\setminus{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x_{k}}
for each
k\geq 1
and
\mathcal{Z}(h)=\mathcal{Z}(h_{0})\cap\mathcal{Z}(h^{\prime})=X\setminus N(X)
, so
h\in{\mathcal{I}}(X\setminus N(X))
. Suppose there exists
x\in T(X)
such that
h_{x}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x_{k}}
. Then there exists an open neighborhood
U^{x}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
of
x
such that
h|_{U^{x}}\in{\mathcal{I}}(X){\mathcal{O}}(U^{x})
. As
D
is dense in
T(X)
, there exists some
k\geq 1
such that
x_{k}\in U^{x}
, so
h_{x_{k}}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x_{k}}
, which is a contradiction. Consequently,
h_{x}\in{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}\setminus{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}
for each
x\in T(X)
.

Nash Case
. As
X\setminus N(X)
is a coherent Nash subset of
V:={\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X)
, Theorem
2.14
(A) assures that the global sections of the sheaf of
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-ideals
{\mathcal{J}}_{X}^{\bullet}|_{V}
generate
{\mathcal{J}}^{\bullet}_{X,x}
for each
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X)
. As the ring of Nash functions
{\mathcal{N}}(V)
is Noetherian, the ideal
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X\setminus N(X))
is generated by finitely many global sections
h_{1},\ldots,h_{s}
. Thus,
{\mathcal{J}}^{\bullet}_{X,x}=\{h_{1},\ldots,h_{s}\}{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
for each
x\in V
.

Let
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
be an invariant open neighborhood of
V
such that
h_{1},\ldots,h_{s}
have invariant holomorphic extensions
H_{1},\ldots,H_{s}\in{\mathcal{O}}(\Omega)
. Let
D:=\{x_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}\subset T(X)
be a countable dense subset of
T(X)
. For each
k\geq 1
we have
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x_{k}}\setminus{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x_{k}}\neq\varnothing
(because
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x_{k}}^{\bullet}\setminus{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x_{k}}^{\bullet}\neq\varnothing
and the inclusion
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\tt x}_{k}}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\tt x}_{k}}
is by Corollary
2.9
faithfully flat for each
k\geq 1
), so
({\mathcal{J}}_{X,x_{k}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}})\setminus{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}_{\widetilde{X},x_{k}}\neq\varnothing
(because
{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x_{k}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}}={\mathcal{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}_{\widetilde{X},x_{k}}
). Thus,
\widetilde{X}_{x_{k}}\setminus\mathcal{Z}(\{H_{1},\ldots,H_{s}\})\neq\varnothing
for each
k\geq 1
. By the curve selection lemma
[
ABR
, Rem.VII.4.3(b)]
(using the underlying real structures of all the involved complex analytic sets) there exists an analytic curve
\gamma_{k}:(-1,1)\to\widetilde{X}
such that
\gamma_{k}(0)=x_{k}
and
\gamma_{k}((0,1))\subset\widetilde{X}\setminus\mathcal{Z}(\{H_{1},\ldots,H_{s}\})
for each
k\geq 1
.

For each
k\geq 1
let
j_{k}\in\{1,\ldots,s\}
be such that the analytic series
H_{j_{k}}\circ\gamma_{k}\in{\mathbb{C}}\{{\tt t}\}\setminus\{0\}
. For each
k\geq 1
let
m_{k}\geq 1
be an integer such that the tuple
(H_{1}^{2}\circ\gamma_{k},\ldots,H_{s}^{2}\circ\gamma_{k})\in\{{\tt t}^{m_{k}}\}{\mathbb{C}}\{{\tt t}\}^{s}\setminus\{{\tt t}^{m_{k}+1}\}{\mathbb{C}}\{{\tt t}\}^{s}
and define
w_{k}:=(\frac{H_{1}^{2}\circ\gamma_{k}}{{\tt t}^{m_{k}}},\ldots,\frac{H_{s}^{2}\circ\gamma_{k}}{{\tt t}^{m_{k}}})\in{\mathbb{C}}\{{\tt t}\}^{s}\setminus\{{\tt t}\}{\mathbb{C}}\{{\tt t}\}^{s}
, which satisfies
w_{k}(0):=(w_{1k},\ldots,w_{sk})\neq 0
. By Baire’s Theorem there exists positive real numbers
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{s}>0
such that
\lambda_{1}w_{1k}+\cdots+\lambda_{s}w_{sk}\neq 0
for each
k\geq 1
. Consequently,
H:=\lambda_{1}H_{1}^{2}+\cdots+\lambda_{s}H_{s}^{2}
satisfies that

\frac{H\circ\gamma_{k}}{{\tt t}^{2m_{k}}}=\lambda_{1}\frac{H_{1}^{2}\circ\gamma_{k}}{{\tt t}^{2m_{k}}}+\cdots+\lambda_{r}\frac{H_{s}^{2}\circ\gamma_{k}}{{\tt t}^{2m_{k}}}\in{\mathbb{C}}\{{\tt t}\}\setminus\{{\tt t}\}{\mathbb{C}}\{{\tt t}\}

for each
k\geq 1
. Thus,
H\circ\gamma_{k}\in{\mathbb{C}}\{{\tt t}\}\setminus\{0\}
for each
k\geq 1
and we define
h:=H|_{V}
. We conclude
h_{y_{k}}\not\in{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x_{k}}^{\bullet}
for each
k\geq 1
and
\mathcal{Z}(h)=\mathcal{Z}(h_{1},\ldots,h_{s})=X\setminus N(X)
, so
h\in{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X\setminus N(X))
. Suppose there exists
x\in T(X)
such that
h_{x}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x_{k}}^{\bullet}
. Then there exists an open (semialgebraic) neighborhood
U^{x}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
of
x
such that
h|_{U^{x}}\in{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X){\mathcal{N}}(U^{x})
. As
D
is dense in
T(X)
, there exists some
k\geq 1
such that
x_{k}\in U^{x}
, so
h_{x_{k}}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x_{k}}^{\bullet}
, which is a contradiction. Consequently,
h_{x}\in{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}^{\bullet}\setminus{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}^{\bullet}
for each
x\in T(X)
, as required.
∎

5.A.
C
-analytic and Nash locally hypersurfaces

We say that a
C
-analytic set (resp. Nash set)
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a
C
-analytic locally hypersurface (resp. Nash locally hypersurface) if for each
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
the ideal
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
(resp.
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}^{\bullet}
of
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
) is principal. In particular, a
C
-analytic locally hypersurface is pure dimensional. Thus, not every
C
-analytic hypersurface is a
C
-analytic locally hypersurface. For instance, Whitney’s umbrella
W:=\{y^{2}-zx^{2}=0\}
or Cartan’s umbrella
C:=\{(x^{2}+y^{2})z-y^{3}=0\}
are
C
-analytic hypersurfaces that are not
C
-analytic locally hypersurfaces. However, the examples in §
3.E
are
C
-analytic locally hypersurfaces. Let us show the existence of ‘good’ global equations for
C
-analytic locally hypersurfaces (resp. Nash locally hypersurfaces) outside the set of points on non-coherence.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a
C
-analytic locally hypersurface (resp. Nash locally hypersurface). Let
N(X)
be the set of points of non-coherence of
X
. Then there exists
h\in{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X))
(resp.
h\in{\mathcal{N}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X))
) such that
h_{x}
generates the ideal
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}
(resp.
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}^{\bullet}
) of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
(resp.
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
) for each
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X)
.

Proof.

Assume first
X
is a
C
-analytic locally hypersurface. Consider the exact sequence

0\to{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}^{*}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\setminus\{0\}\overset{\rho}{\to}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\setminus\{0\}/{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}^{*}\to 0

where
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}^{*}
is the sheaf of invertible analytic germs, whereas
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\setminus\{0\}/{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}^{*}
is the sheaf of germs of positive divisors and
\rho
is the canonical projection. The locally principal coherent sheaf of ideals
{\mathcal{J}}_{X}
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X)
defines naturally an element
\sigma_{X}\in H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X),{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\setminus\{0\}/{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}^{*})
. Consider the long exact sequence

0\to H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X),{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}^{*})\to H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X),{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\setminus\{0\})\\
\to H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X),{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\setminus\{0\}/{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}^{*})\to H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X),{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}^{*}).

We claim:
H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X),{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}^{*})=0
. To that end, consider the exact sequence of sheaves

0\to{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\overset{\varphi}{\to}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}^{*}\overset{\pi}{\to}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}^{*}/\varphi({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})\to 0

where
\varphi(f_{x})=\exp(f_{x})
and
\pi(g_{x})=g_{x}\varphi({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x})
. Observe that
\varphi({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x})
is the set of all strictly positive analytic germs and
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}^{*}=\varphi({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x})\sqcup-\varphi({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x})
, so
\pi(g_{x})
is determined by the sign of
g_{x}
. Thus,
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}^{*}/\varphi({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})
is isomorphic to the constant sheaf
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
and after identifying both sheaves, we may assume
\pi(g_{x})=\frac{g_{x}}{|g_{x}|}
for each
g_{x}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}^{*}
. Consider the long exact sequence

0\to H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X),{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})\to H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X),{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}^{*})\to H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X),{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})\\
\to H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X),{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})\to H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X),{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}^{*})\to H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X),{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})\to H^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X),{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}).

By Cartan’s Theorem B we have
H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X),{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})=0
and
H^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X),{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})=0
, so

H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X),{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}^{*})\cong H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X),{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}).

To compute the last group we use universal coefficient theorem for cohomology
[
Ha
, Thm.3.2]
and obtain the following exact sequence

0\to{\rm Ext}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{1}(H_{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X),{\mathbb{Z}}),{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})\to H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X),{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})\to{\rm Hom}(H_{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X),{\mathbb{Z}}),{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})\to 0.

As
N(X)\subset X
has codimension
\geq 2
, the
C
-semialnalytic set
N(X)\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
has codimension
\geq 3
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, so
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X)
is connected and
H_{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X),{\mathbb{Z}})={\mathbb{Z}}
. As
{\mathbb{Z}}
is a free group,

{\rm Ext}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{1}(H_{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X),{\mathbb{Z}}),{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})={\rm Ext}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{1}({\mathbb{Z}},{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})=0

(see
[
Ha
, p.195]
), so
H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X),{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
is isomorphic to
{\rm Hom}(H_{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X),{\mathbb{Z}}),{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
. In addition, as
N(X)
has codimension
\geq 3
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, the first homotopy group
\pi_{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X))=0
, so its abelianization
H_{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X),{\mathbb{Z}})=0
. Thus,
{\rm Hom}(H_{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X),{\mathbb{Z}}),{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})=0
, so also
H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X),{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})=0
. This means that the homomorphism

H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X),{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\setminus\{0\})\overset{\rho}{\to}H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X),{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\setminus\{0\}/{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}^{*})\to 0

is surjective. Consequently, there exists
h\in H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X),{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\setminus\{0\})
such that
\rho(h)=\sigma_{X}
. Thus,
h
generates the ideal
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}
for each
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X)
.

Assume next
X
is a Nash subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. By the previous case there exist
h\in{\mathcal{I}}(X\setminus N(X))
that generates the ideal
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}
for each
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X)
. Let
g\in{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X\setminus N(X))\setminus\{0\}
. Then there exists
b\in{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X))
such that
g=bh
. By
[
CS
, Cor.2]
there exists Nash functions
b_{1},h_{1}\in{\mathcal{N}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X))
and analytic functions
u,v\in{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X))
such that
uv=1
,
b=ub_{1}
and
h=vh_{1}
. Thus,
g=b_{1}h_{1}
. As
v
is a unit of
{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X))
, the Nash function
h_{1}
generates the ideal
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}
for each
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X)
. As
(h_{1}){\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}=(h_{1}){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}={\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}={\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}^{\bullet}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
and the inclusion
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
is by Corollary
2.9
faithfully flat, we deduce
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}^{\bullet}=(h_{1}){\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
for each
x\in X\setminus N(X)
, as required.
∎

Remarks 5.4
.

Let
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a
C
-analytic locally hypersurface (resp. Nash locally hypersurface).

(i) The irreducible components of each analytic germ
X_{x}
have dimension
n-1
. Thus, if
X_{x}
is not coherent, it has a non-coherent irreducible component
X_{1,x}
of dimension
n-1
. Following the notation in §
3.D
we deduce by Theorem
3.9
that
N(X)=N_{n-1}(X,\varnothing)
. The proof of Theorem
4.1
in this particular case can be substantially simplified, because
\ell_{e}=n-1
in all cases.

(ii) By Lemma
5.3
there exists
h\in{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X))
(resp.
h\in{\mathcal{N}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X))
) such that
h_{x}
generates the ideal
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}
(resp.
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}^{\bullet}
) for each
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X)
. Thus,
h
satisfies the requirements of the statement of Theorem
5.1
for each
x\in T(X)\setminus N(X)
. In addition, any other function satisfying such requirements is a multiple of
h
, because
J_{X,x}=h_{x}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
for each
x\in X\setminus N(X)
.
\sqbullet

6.
Proofs of the main results

In this section we prove Theorems
1.4
and
1.7
. As an application, we also prove Corollaries
6.10
and
6.11
. We begin with some preliminary results.

6.A.
Obstruction set of a meromorphic function

In order to manage better meromorphic functions on a
C
-analytic set, we will use freely the following result.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
R
be a reduced ring and let
Q(R):=\{\frac{f}{g}:\ a,b\in R,\ \text{$b$ is not a zero divisor}\}
be its total ring of fractions. Two fractions
\frac{f_{1}}{g_{1}},\frac{f_{2}}{g_{2}}\in Q(R)
are equal if and only if
f_{1}g_{2}-f_{2}g_{1}=0
.

Proof.

Let
S\subset R
be the multiplicative set of the non-zero divisor of
R
. Then
Q(R)=S^{-1}R
and
R\setminus S
is the union of the minimal prime ideals of
R
. As
R
is reduced, the zero ideal is the intersection of the minimal prime ideals of
R
. If
\frac{f_{1}}{g_{1}}=\frac{f_{2}}{g_{2}}
, there exists
g\in S
such that
g(f_{1}g_{2}-f_{2}g_{1})=0
. Let
{\mathfrak{p}}
be a minimal prime ideal of
R
. As
g(f_{1}g_{2}-f_{2}g_{1})\in{\mathfrak{p}}
and
g\in R\setminus S\subset R\setminus{\mathfrak{p}}
, we have
f_{1}g_{2}-f_{2}g_{1}\in{\mathfrak{p}}
. As this happens for each minimal prime ideal
{\mathfrak{p}}
of
R
, we deduce that
f_{1}g_{2}-f_{2}g_{1}=0
, because it belongs to the intersection of the minimal prime ideals of
R
, which is the zero ideal. The converse is straightforward.
∎

6.A.1.

Let
X
be a
C
-analytic subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, let
\zeta:X\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a function and let
x\in X
. Suppose there exists analytic germs
f_{x},g_{x}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
(depending on
x
) such that
\zeta_{x}=\frac{f_{x}}{g_{x}}
and
g_{x}
does not belong to any minimal prime of
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}
. Observe that
\zeta
is an analytic function at
x\in X
if there exists
a_{x}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
such that
\frac{f_{x}}{g_{x}}=-a_{x}
on
X_{x}
or, equivalently, if
f_{x}+a_{x}g_{x}\in{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}
. This is equivalent to
f_{x}\in g_{x}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}+{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}
.

6.A.2.

Let
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be the
C
-analytic irreducible components of the
C
-analytic set
X
and let
\zeta:=\frac{f}{g}
be a meromorphic function on
X
, that is,
f,g\in{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
and
g|_{X_{i}}\neq 0
for each
i\in I
. If
\zeta
coincides with the restriction to
X
of an analytic function on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, there exists
a\in{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
such that
\frac{f}{g}=-a
on
X
or, equivalently,
f\in g{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})+{\mathcal{I}}(X)
. We have
f_{x}\in g_{x}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}+{\mathcal{I}}(X){\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}=g_{x}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}+{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}
for each
x\in X
. Consequently, if there exists a point
x\in X
such that
f_{x}\not\in g_{x}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}+{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}
, the meromorphic function
\zeta
admits no analytic extension to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. We define the obstructing set of the meromorphic function
\zeta=\frac{f}{g}
as
{\tt O}(\zeta):=\{x\in X:\ f_{x}\not\in g_{x}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}+{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}\}
. By Lemma
6.1
the obstructing set
{\tt O}(\zeta)
does not depend on the meromorphic representation of
\zeta
. As
{\mathcal{I}}_{X}
is a coherent sheaf of ideals,
{\tt O}(\zeta)
is in addition a closed subset of
X
.

In case
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a Nash set and
\zeta=\frac{f}{g}
, where
f,g
are Nash functions and
g
is not identically zero on any of the irreducible components of
X
, we define
{\tt O}^{\bullet}(\zeta):=\{x\in X:\ f_{x}\not\in g_{x}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}^{\bullet}+{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}^{\bullet}\}
. As the inclusion homomorphism
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
is by Corollary
2.9
faithfully flat,
{\tt O}^{\bullet}(\zeta)={\tt O}(\zeta)
.

Remark 6.2
.

A meromorphic function (resp. meromorphic Nash function)
\zeta
has an analytic (resp. Nash) extension to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
if and only if
{\tt O}(\zeta)=\varnothing
. Consequently,
{\tt O}(\zeta)
determines whether
\zeta
admits or not an analytic (resp. Nash) extension to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and how far is
\zeta
from having such analytic (resp. Nash) extension.

The ‘only if’ implication is clear, so let us proof the ‘if’ implication. If
{\tt O}(\zeta)=\varnothing
, then
\zeta
defines an element of
H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{I}}_{X})
(resp. of
H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{I}}_{X}^{\bullet})
). By Cartan’s Theorem B (resp. Theorem
2.14
(B)) there exists an analytic function
a\in{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
(resp. Nash function
a\in{\mathcal{N}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
) such that
a|_{X}=\zeta
.
\sqbullet

6.B.
Winning family of denominators

Let
Y\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a
C
-analytic subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and let
\widetilde{Y}
be an invariant Stein complexification of
Y
, which is a closed subset of an invariant open Stein neighborhood
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
of
Y
. By
[
C2
, Proof of Prop.15]
there exist finitely many invariant
P_{1},\ldots,P_{m}\in{\mathcal{O}}(\Omega)
such that
\mathcal{Z}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{m})=\widetilde{Y}
. Consider the family of holomorphic functions

P_{\lambda}:=\lambda_{1}P_{1}^{2}+\cdots+\lambda_{m}P_{m}^{2}\in{\mathcal{O}}(\Omega)

(6.1)

where
\lambda:=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
. We are particularly interested in the case when
\lambda
belongs to the open orthant
{\mathcal{Q}}_{m}:=\{\lambda_{1}>0,\ldots,\lambda_{m}>0\}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
. In that case the holomorphic functions
P_{\lambda}
are in particular invariant. By
[
ABF1
, §2.2]
and Oka’s coherence theorems
[
N1
, Ch.IV]
if
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
is a contractible open Stein neighborhood of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, we can find a square-free invariant
P^{*}_{\lambda}\in{\mathcal{O}}(\Omega)
such that
P^{*}_{\lambda}|P_{\lambda}
and
\mathcal{Z}(P^{*}_{\lambda})=\mathcal{Z}(P_{\lambda})
.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
Z_{y}
be a complex analytic germ at
y\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
and let
Z_{1,y},\ldots,Z_{r,y}
be the irreducible components of
Z_{y}
. Suppose
\widetilde{Y}_{y}
does not contain
Z_{i,y}
for
i=1,\ldots,r
. Then there exists a proper algebraic subset
S\subsetneq{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
such that (
{\mathcal{Q}}_{m}\setminus S
is dense in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{m}
and)

\dim(Z_{y}\cap\mathcal{Z}(P_{\lambda,y}))<\dim(Z_{y})
for each
\lambda\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{m}\setminus S
.

Proof.

Let
W\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
be an open neighborhood of
y
and let
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{r}
be irreducible complex analytic subsets of
W
such that the irreducible components of
Z_{y}
are
Z_{1,y},\ldots,Z_{r,y}
. By the curve selection lemma
[
ABR
, Rem.VII.4.3(b)]
(using the underlying real structures of all the involved complex analytic sets) there exists analytic curves
\alpha_{i}:(-1,1)\to Z_{i}
such that
\alpha_{i}(0)=y
and
\alpha_{i}((0,1))\subset Z_{i}\setminus\widetilde{Y}
for
i=1,\ldots,r
. As
\widetilde{Y}=\mathcal{Z}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{m})
there exists
q_{i}\geq 1
such that
(P_{1}^{2}\circ\alpha_{i},\ldots,P_{m}^{2}\circ\alpha_{i})\in(({\tt t}^{q_{i}}){\mathbb{C}}[[{\tt t}]])^{m}\setminus(({\tt t}^{q_{i}+1}){\mathbb{C}}[[{\tt t}]])^{m}
, so
\zeta_{i}:=(\frac{(P_{1}^{2}\circ\alpha_{i})}{{\tt t}^{q_{i}}},\ldots,\frac{(P_{m}^{2}\circ\alpha_{i})}{{\tt t}^{q_{i}}})\in{\mathbb{C}}[[{\tt t}]]^{m}\setminus(({\tt t}){\mathbb{C}}[[{\tt t}]]^{m})
for
i=1,\ldots,r
. Thus, the vectors
w_{i}:=(w_{i1},\ldots,w_{im})=\zeta_{i}(0)
are not identically zero for
i=1,\ldots,r
. Consider
S:=\bigcup_{i=1}^{r}\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m}:\ \lambda_{1}w_{i1}+\cdots+\lambda_{m}w_{im}=0\}
, which is a proper algebraic subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, so
{\mathcal{Q}}_{m}\setminus S
is a dense open subset of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{m}
. If
\lambda\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{m}\setminus S
, then
\prod_{i=1}^{r}P_{\lambda}\circ\alpha_{i}\neq 0
. Consequently,
\dim(Z_{i}\cap\mathcal{Z}(P_{\lambda}))<\dim(Z_{i})
for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
, so
\dim(Z_{y}\cap\mathcal{Z}(P_{\lambda,y}))<\dim(Z_{y})
, as required.
∎

Examples 6.4
.

(i) An example of application of the previous results is
Y:=\{q:=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{n})\}
,
\widetilde{Y}:=\{q\}
and
P_{i}:={\tt x}_{i}-q_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,n
.

(ii) Another example of application of the previous result (in the analytic case) is a discrete subset
Y:=\{y_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
of
T(X)
. In this case
\widetilde{Y}=Y
. In order to provide finitely many equations to describe
\widetilde{Y}
we consideran analytic diffeomorphism
\varphi:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
that transforms
Y
onto the set
{\mathbb{Z}}\times\{(0,\overset{(n-1)}{\ldots},0)\}
, see
[
BKS
, Cor.3]
. Take
P_{1}(x):=\sin(2\pi x_{1})
,
P_{2}(x)=x_{2}
, …,
P_{n}(x)=x_{n}
.
\sqbullet

6.C.
Winning equations around a
C
-analytic subset (almost numerators)

Let
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a
C
-analytic set and let
Y\subset X
be a
C
-analytic subset.

6.C.1.

We have seen in Lemma
5.1
that there exists an analytic function
h\in{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X))
(resp. Nash function
h\in{\mathcal{N}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X))
) such that
\mathcal{Z}(h)=X\setminus N(X)
and
h_{x}\in{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}\setminus{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}
(resp.
h_{x}\in{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}^{\bullet}\setminus{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}^{\bullet}
) for each
x\in T(X)\setminus N(X)
. Thus, if
Y\subset X\setminus N(X)
, then
U_{0}:={\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus N(X)
is an open neighborhood (resp. open semialgebraic neighborhood) of
Y
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and
h\in{\mathcal{O}}(U_{0})
(resp.
h\in{\mathcal{N}}(U_{0})
) satisfies
\mathcal{Z}(h)=X\cap U_{0}
and
h_{y}\in{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}\setminus{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}
(resp.
h_{x}\in{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}^{\bullet}\setminus{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}^{\bullet}
) for each
x\in Y\cap T(X)
. If
X
is non-coherent, then
T(X)\setminus N(X)\neq\varnothing
(Corollaries
4.2
and
4.4
).

6.C.2.

If
Y:=\{y_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
is a discrete set, for each
y_{k}\in Y\cap T(X)
there exists
h_{k,y_{k}}\in{\mathcal{J}}_{X,y_{k}}\setminus{\mathcal{I}}_{X,y_{k}}
such that
\mathcal{Z}(h_{k,y_{k}})=X_{y_{k}}
and
h^{\prime}_{k,y_{k}}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{X,y_{k}}
such that
\mathcal{Z}(h^{\prime}_{k,y_{k}})=X_{y_{k}}
. Substituting
h_{k,y_{k}}
by
h_{k,y_{k}}^{2}+h_{k,y_{k}}^{\prime 2}
we may assume
h_{k,y_{k}}\in{\mathcal{J}}_{X,y_{k}}\setminus{\mathcal{I}}_{X,y_{k}}
and
\mathcal{Z}(h_{k,y_{k}})=X_{y_{k}}
for each
y_{k}\in Y\cap T(X)
. For the points
y_{k}\in Y\setminus T(X)
let
h_{k,y_{k}}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{X,y_{k}}={\mathcal{J}}_{X,y_{k}}
be such that
\mathcal{Z}(h_{k,y_{k}})=X_{y_{k}}
. Let
\{V_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
be pairwise disjoint open neighborhoods in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
of the point
y_{k}
such that
h_{k,y_{k}}
has an analytic representative
h_{k}
in
V_{k}
for each
k\geq 1
. Define
U_{0}:=\bigsqcup_{k\geq 1}V_{k}
and

h:U_{0}\to{\mathbb{R}},\ x\mapsto h_{k}(x)\ \text{if $x\in V_{k}$}.

The function
h\in{\mathcal{O}}(U_{0})
satisfies
\mathcal{Z}(h)=X\cap U_{0}
and
h_{y}\in{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}\setminus{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}
for each
x\in Y\cap T(X)
. In this case we have no restriction with respect to the set
N(X)
. The previous construction holds analogously in the Nash case if we consider a finite set instead of a discrete set.

6.D.
Proof of Theorems
1.4
and
1.7

We recall first the
1
-cocycle and
1
-coboundary relations for a sheaf on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
corresponding to an open covering of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
constituted only by two open sets.

Remark 6.5
.

Let
{\mathfrak{U}}:=\{U_{0},U_{1}\}
be an open covering of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a sheaf on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Pick
i,j,k\in\{0,1\}
and sections
f_{ij}\in{\mathcal{F}}(U_{i}\cap U_{j}),f_{ik}\in{\mathcal{F}}(U_{i}\cap U_{k}),f_{jk}\in{\mathcal{F}}(U_{j}\cap U_{k})
. All possible intersections of three elements of
{\mathfrak{U}}
and the corresponding
1
-cocycle relations are the following:

\displaystyle U_{0}\cap U_{0}\cap U_{0}\ \leadsto\ 0=f_{00}-f_{00}+f_{00}=f_{00},

\displaystyle U_{0}\cap U_{0}\cap U_{1}\ \leadsto\ 0=f_{01}-f_{01}+f_{00}=f_{00},

\displaystyle U_{0}\cap U_{1}\cap U_{0}\ \leadsto\ 0=f_{10}-f_{00}+f_{01}=f_{10}+f_{01},

\displaystyle U_{0}\cap U_{1}\cap U_{1}\ \leadsto\ 0=f_{11}-f_{01}+f_{01}=f_{11},

\displaystyle U_{1}\cap U_{0}\cap U_{0}\ \leadsto\ 0=f_{00}-f_{10}+f_{10}=f_{00},

\displaystyle U_{1}\cap U_{0}\cap U_{1}\ \leadsto\ 0=f_{01}-f_{11}+f_{10}=f_{10}+f_{01},

\displaystyle U_{1}\cap U_{1}\cap U_{0}\ \leadsto\ 0=f_{10}-f_{10}+f_{11}=f_{11},

\displaystyle U_{1}\cap U_{1}\cap U_{1}\ \leadsto\ 0=f_{11}-f_{11}+f_{11}=f_{11}.

Thus, we obtain the following relations
f_{00}=0
on
U_{0}
,
f_{11}=0
on
U_{1}
and
f_{10}=-f_{01}
on
U_{0}\cap U_{1}
. Given
f_{0}\in{\mathcal{F}}(U_{0})
and
f_{1}\in{\mathcal{F}}(U_{1})
, the
1
-coboundary relations are the following:

\displaystyle U_{0}\cap U_{0}\ \leadsto\ f_{00}=f_{0}-f_{0}=0,

\displaystyle U_{0}\cap U_{1}\ \leadsto\ f_{01}=f_{1}-f_{0},

\displaystyle U_{1}\cap U_{0}\ \leadsto\ f_{10}=f_{0}-f_{1},

\displaystyle U_{1}\cap U_{1}\ \leadsto\ f_{11}=f_{1}-f_{1}=0.

In this situation a
1
-cocycle

(f_{00},f_{01},f_{10},f_{11})=(0,f_{01},-f_{01},0)\in{\mathcal{F}}(U_{0}\cap U_{0})\times{\mathcal{F}}(U_{0}\cap U_{1})\times{\mathcal{F}}(U_{1}\cap U_{0})\times{\mathcal{F}}(U_{1}\cap U_{1})

is a
1
-coboundary if and only if there exists
f_{i}\in{\mathcal{F}}(U_{i})
for
i\in\{0,1\}
such that
f_{01}=f_{1}-f_{0}
.
\hfill\sqbullet

As we have pointed out in the Introduction, cohomology will not help in the Nash setting, because even
H^{1}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathbb{R}})\neq 0
, see
[
H
]
. Thus, we will use cohomology to prove Theorem
1.4
and find the mentioned meromorphic function that is local analytic on the
C
-analytic set
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, but does not admit an analytic extension to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Then we will adapt the last part of the latter construction to prove Theorem
1.7
finding a meromorphic Nash function on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
that is local Nash on the Nash set
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, but does not admit an analytic extension to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, skipping however the cohomological procedure. We are ready to prove the main results of this article.

We will prove simultaneously stronger results than Theorems
1.4
and
1.7
and we provide next the precise statements. When reading the following results the reader should have in mind Corollary
4.2
, Theorem
5.1
and §
6.C
, because they guarantee the existence of the
C
-analytic sets (resp. Nash sets)
Y
in the statementss.

Theorem 6.6
.

Let
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a
C
-analytic set with
N(X)\neq\varnothing
. Let
Y\subset X
be a
C
-analytic subset that contains no irreducible component of
X
and meets
T(X)
, let
U_{0}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be an open neighborhood of
Y
and let
h\in H^{0}(U_{0},{\mathcal{J}}_{X})
be such that
h_{y}\in{\mathcal{J}}_{X,y}\setminus{\mathcal{I}}_{X,y}
for each
y\in Y\cap T(X)
. Then there exist a meromorphic function
\zeta:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\dashrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
with set of poles
Y
such that
\zeta|_{X}
is an analytic function on
X
and
Y\cap T(X)\subset{\tt O}(\zeta)\subset Y\cap\operatorname{Cl}(T(X))
.

Theorem 6.7
.

Let
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a Nash set with
N(X)\neq\varnothing
. Let
Y\subset X
be a Nash subset that contains no irreducible component of
X
and meets
T(X)
, let
U_{0}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be an open semialgebraic neighborhood of
Y
and let
h\in H^{0}(U_{0},{\mathcal{J}}_{X}^{\bullet})
be such that
h_{y}\in{\mathcal{J}}_{X,y}^{\bullet}\setminus{\mathcal{I}}_{X,y}^{\bullet}
for each
y\in Y\cap T(X)
. Then there exist a meromorphic Nash function
\zeta:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\dashrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
with set of poles
Y
such that
\zeta|_{X}
is a local Nash function on
X
and
Y\cap T(X)\subset{\tt O}(\zeta)\subset Y\cap\operatorname{Cl}(T(X))
.

Remarks 6.8
.

(i) If
Y\cap N(X)=\varnothing
(as in §
6.C.1
), we have
{\tt O}(\zeta)=Y\cap T(X)
, because
\operatorname{Cl}(T(X))=T(X)\cup N(X)
(Corollary
4.2
).

(ii) As there are many possible intersections
Y\cap T(X)
for
C
-analytic subsets
Y
of
X
, we deduce from Theorem
6.6
(resp. Theorem
6.7
) that there are many different meromorphic functions (resp. Nash meromorphic functions) on a non-coherent
C
-analytic set (resp. Nash set)
X
that are analytic (resp. local Nash) on
X
and have no analytic extensions (resp. Nash extensions) to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, see the statements of Theorems
1.4
and
1.7
.
\sqbullet

Proofs of Theorems
6.6
and
6.7
.

The proof of both results is conducted in several steps:

Step 1.

Initial preparation.
Consider the exact sequence of sheaves

0\to{\mathcal{J}}_{X}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{J}}_{X}\to 0

and the exact long corresponding sequence of global sections

0\to H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{J}}_{X})\to H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})\to H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{J}}_{X})\to H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{J}}_{X})\to H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})\\
\to\cdots\to H^{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})\to H^{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{J}}_{X})\to H^{p+1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{J}}_{X})\to H^{p+1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}).

By Cartan’s Theorem B, we have
H^{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})=0
for each
p\geq 1
. Consequently,

H^{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{J}}_{X})\cong H^{p+1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{J}}_{X})

for
p\geq 1
and we have a (initial) exact sequence

0\to H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{J}}_{X})\to H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})\to H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{J}}_{X})\overset{\delta}{\to}H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{J}}_{X})\to 0.

(6.2)

The homomorphism
H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})\to H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{J}}_{X})
is surjective if and only if
H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{J}}_{X})=0
. If
X
is coherent, the sheaf of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-ideals
{\mathcal{J}}_{X}
is coherent and by Cartan’s Theorem B we have
H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{J}}_{X})=0
, so we will prove:
if
X
is non-coherent, then
H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{J}}_{X})\neq 0
. As
H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{J}}_{X})=\displaystyle\lim_{\longrightarrow}H^{1}({\mathfrak{V}},{\mathcal{J}}_{X})
, where
{\mathfrak{V}}
runs over the open coverings of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and
H^{1}({\mathfrak{V}},{\mathcal{J}}_{X})
is the C̆ech cohomology group of
{\mathcal{J}}_{X}
associated to
{\mathfrak{V}}
, it suffices to show that
H^{1}({\mathfrak{U}},{\mathcal{J}}_{X})\neq 0
for coverings
{\mathfrak{U}}
constituted by two open sets (see Remark
6.5
).

Step 2.
Construction of a
1
-cocycle that it not a
1
-coboundary. Let
U_{0}
be the open set provided in the statement, define
U_{1}:={\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus Y
and observe that
{\mathfrak{U}}:=\{U_{0},U_{1}\}
is an open covering of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. We are going to construct a
1
-cocycle
f_{01}\in H^{0}(U_{0}\cap U_{1},{\mathcal{J}}_{X})
such that for each pair
(f_{0},f_{1})\in H^{0}(U_{0},{\mathcal{J}}_{X})\times H^{0}(U_{1},{\mathcal{J}}_{X})
it holds
f_{01}\neq f_{1}-f_{0}
.

Let
\widetilde{Y}
be a complexification of
Y
that is a closed subset of an invariant contractible open Stein neighborhood
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Let
P_{1},\ldots,P_{m}\in{\mathcal{O}}(\Omega)
be invariant complex analytic functions such that
\widetilde{Y}=\mathcal{Z}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{m})
, use
[
C2
, Proof of Prop.15]
. In the Nash setting we pick a finite system of generators
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}
of the ideal
{\mathcal{I}}(Y)
, consider analytic extensions
P_{1},\ldots,P_{m}
of
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}
to an open semialgebraic neighborhood
\Omega
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
and define
\widetilde{Y}:=\mathcal{Z}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{m})
, which is a complexification of
Y
. As
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
is by Corollary
2.9
faithfully flat, in the Nash case we have
h_{y}\in{\mathcal{J}}_{X,y}\setminus{\mathcal{I}}_{X,y}=({\mathcal{J}}_{X,y}^{\bullet}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x})\setminus({\mathcal{I}}_{X,y}^{\bullet}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x})
for each
y\in Y\cap T(X)
as well.

For each
\lambda\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{m}:=\{\lambda_{1}>0,\ldots,\lambda_{m}>0\}
define

P_{\lambda}({\tt z}):=\lambda_{1}P_{1}^{2}+\cdots+\lambda_{m}P_{m}^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad f_{01,\lambda}({\tt z}):=\frac{h({\tt z})}{P_{\lambda}({\tt z})}\in H^{0}(U_{0}\cap U_{1},{\mathcal{J}}_{X}),

where
U_{0}\cap U_{1}=U_{0}\setminus Y
. Substituting
P_{\lambda}
by a suitable divisor, we may assume
P_{\lambda}
has no multiple factors (see §
6.B
).

Pick
f_{0}\in H^{0}(U_{0},{\mathcal{J}}_{X})
and
f_{1}\in H^{0}(U_{1},{\mathcal{J}}_{X})
and assume that
f_{01,\lambda}=f_{1}-f_{0}
on
U_{0}\cap U_{1}
. Thus,
h=f_{1}P_{\lambda}-f_{0}P_{\lambda}
and define
g_{\lambda}:=f_{1}P_{\lambda}=h+f_{0}P_{\lambda}
. The latter function is analytic on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}=U_{0}\cup U_{1}
, because
P_{\lambda}
is analytic on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
f_{1}
is analytic on
U_{1}={\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus Y
and both
h,f_{0}
are analytic on
U_{0}
(which contains
Y
). As
f_{1}|_{X\setminus Y}=0
,
h|_{X\cap U_{0}}=0
,
f_{0}|_{X\cap U_{0}}=0
,
P_{\lambda}|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\in{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
and
Y\subset X\cap U_{0}
, we deduce that
g_{\lambda}|_{X}=0
. Let
G_{\lambda}
be an invariant analytic extension of
g_{\lambda}
to an invariant open neighborhood
\Omega^{\prime}\subset\Omega
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. As
g_{\lambda}|_{X}=0
, we may assume by Lemma
3.1
that
G_{\lambda}|_{\widetilde{X}\cap\Omega^{\prime}}=0
. Let
V_{0}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
be an invariant open neighborhood of
U_{0}
such that
h,f_{0}
extend to invariant holomorphic functions
H,F_{0}
on
V_{0}
.

Let
D:=\{y_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
be a dense subset of
Y\cap T(X)
. For each
j\geq 1
we have
h_{y_{j}}\in{\mathcal{J}}_{X,y_{j}}\setminus{\mathcal{I}}_{X,y_{j}}
, so
H_{y_{j}}\not\in{\mathcal{I}}_{X,y_{j}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}}={\mathcal{J}}_{\widetilde{X},y_{j}}^{\mathbb{C}}
. Thus, for each
j\geq 1
there exists an irreducible component
Z_{j,y_{j}}
of
\widetilde{X}_{y_{j}}
such that
Z_{j,y_{j}}\not\subset\mathcal{Z}(H_{y_{j}})
. If
X_{1}
is a
C
-irreducible component of
X
of dimension
1
, then
{\mathcal{J}}_{X_{1}}={\mathcal{I}}_{X_{1}}
, so
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(Z_{j,y_{j}})\geq 2
for each
j\geq 1
. By
[
E
, Cor.10.9]
we deduce
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(Z_{j,y_{j}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(H_{y_{j}}))=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(Z_{j,y_{j}})-1\geq 1
. By Lemma
6.3
,
[
E
, Cor.10.9]
and Baire’s Theorem there exists
\lambda_{0}\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{n}
such that
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(Z_{j,y_{j}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(H_{y_{j}})\cap\mathcal{Z}(P_{\lambda_{0},y_{j}}))=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(Z_{j,y_{j}})-2\geq 0
and
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(Z_{j,y_{j}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(P_{\lambda_{0},y_{j}}))=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(Z_{j,y_{j}})-1\geq 1
for each
j\geq 1
. Thus,
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}((\widetilde{X}_{y_{j}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(P_{\lambda_{0},y_{j}}))\setminus\mathcal{Z}(H_{y_{j}}))\geq 1
for each
j\geq 1
. The set of possible values of
\lambda_{0}
is a countable intersection of open dense subsets of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{m}
(and it is itself a dense subset of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{m}
), but we fix one
\lambda_{0}
for the following.

Pick a point
y\in Y\cap T(X)
and suppose
\widetilde{X}_{y}\cap\mathcal{Z}(P_{\lambda_{0},y})\subset\mathcal{Z}(H_{y})
. There exists an open neighborhood
W^{y}
of
y
such that
\widetilde{X}\cap\mathcal{Z}(P_{\lambda_{0}})\cap W^{y}\subset\mathcal{Z}(H)\cap W^{y}
. As
D
is dense in
Y
, we find
y_{j}\in D\cap W^{y}
such that
\widetilde{X}_{y_{j}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(P_{\lambda_{0},y_{j}})\subset\mathcal{Z}(H_{y_{j}})
, whereas
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}((\widetilde{X}_{y_{j}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(H_{y_{j}}))\setminus\mathcal{Z}(H_{y_{j}}))\geq 1
for each
j\geq 1
, which is a contradiction. Thus,
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}((\widetilde{X}_{y}\cap\mathcal{Z}(P_{\lambda_{0},y}))\setminus\mathcal{Z}(H_{y}))\geq 1
for each
y\in Y\cap T(X)
.

Fix a point
y\in Y\cap T(X)
. By the curve selection lemma
[
ABR
, Rem.VII.4.3(b)]
(using the underlying real structures of all the involved complex analytic sets) there exists an analytic curve
\beta^{y}:(-1,1)\to\widetilde{X}
such that
\beta^{y}(0)=y
and
\beta^{y}((0,1))\subset(\widetilde{X}\cap\mathcal{Z}(P_{\lambda_{0}}))\setminus\mathcal{Z}(H)
. We have
G_{\lambda_{0}}\circ\beta^{y}=0
,
P_{\lambda_{0}}\circ\beta^{y}=0
, whereas
H\circ\beta^{y}\neq 0
. Consequently,

0=G_{\lambda_{0}}\circ\beta^{y}=H\circ\beta^{y}+(F_{0}\circ\beta^{y})(P_{\lambda_{0}}\circ\beta^{y})=H\circ\beta^{y},

which is a contradiction. Thus,
f_{01,\lambda_{0}}\in H^{0}(U_{0}\cap U_{1},{\mathcal{J}}_{X})
provides a
1
-cocycle that it is not a
1
-coboundary and the obstruction to be a
1
-coboundary concentrates at the points of
Y\cap T(X)
.

Step 3.
Construction of an analytic function (resp. semialgebraic local Nash function) on
X
that is not the restriction of a analytic function (resp. Nash function) on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
Let us use the previous
1
-cocycle to find an element
\xi\in H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{J}}_{X})
that is not the image of an element of
H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})
. This provides a local analytic function on
X
that is not the restriction of a analytic function on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. As in the Nash case the involved data is Nash, we obtain a semialgebraic local Nash function on
X
that is not the restriction of a analytic function on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, so it is not the restriction of a Nash function on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

Consider the diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{0}(U_{0},{\mathcal{J}}_{X})\times H^{0}(U_{1},{\mathcal{J}}_{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}(U_{0})\times{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}(U_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{0}(U_{0},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{J}}_{X})\times H^{0}(U_{1},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{J}}_{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{0}(U_{0}\cap U_{1},{\mathcal{J}}_{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}(U_{0}\cap U_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{0}(U_{0}\cap U_{1},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{J}}_{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{(f_{0},f_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(f_{0},f_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{f_{1}-f_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{f_{1}-f_{0}}
\textstyle{(q_{0},q_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{([q_{0}],[q_{1}])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{q_{1}-q_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[q_{1}-q_{0}]}

For
f_{01,\lambda_{0}}
we have to find
(q_{0},q_{1})\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}(U_{0})\times{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}(U_{1})
such that

\frac{h}{P_{\lambda_{0}}}=f_{01,\lambda_{0}}=q_{1}-q_{0}=\delta((q_{0},q_{1}))\ \text{ on $U_{0}\cap U_{1}$}.

Consequently,
h=q_{1}P_{\lambda_{0}}-q_{0}P_{\lambda_{0}}
and
h_{\lambda_{0}}:=q_{1}P_{\lambda_{0}}=h+q_{0}P_{\lambda_{0}}
. The latter function is analytic on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, because
P_{\lambda_{0}}
is analytic in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
q_{1}
is analytic on
U_{1}={\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus Y
and both
h,q_{0}
are analytic on
U_{0}
(which contains
Y
). Let us show how one constructs
h_{\lambda_{0}}\in{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
such that

q_{0}:=\frac{h_{\lambda_{0}}-h}{P_{\lambda_{0}}}\in{\mathcal{O}}(U_{0}).

We distinguish both cases:

Analytic Case.
We consider the coherent sheaf of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-ideals
{\mathcal{F}}:=P_{\lambda_{0}}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and the exact sequence of coherent

0\to{\mathcal{F}}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{F}}\to 0.

As the support of the sheaf
{\mathcal{F}}
is
Y
and
Y\subset U_{0}
, the analytic function
h
defines an element of
H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{F}})
. By Cartan’s Theorem B the sequence

0\to H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{F}})\to H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})\to H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{F}})\to 0

is exact, so there exist an analytic function
h_{\lambda_{0}}\in{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
such that
h_{\lambda_{0}}-h
is a multiple of
P_{\lambda_{0}}
in an open neighborhood of
Y
.

Nash Case.
We consider the finite sheaf of
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-ideals
{\mathcal{F}}:=P_{\lambda_{0}}{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
. As the support of
{\mathcal{F}}
is
Y
and
Y\subset U_{0}
, the Nash function
h
defines an element of
H^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{F}})
. By Proposition
2.7
(B) there exist a Nash function
h_{\lambda_{0}}\in{\mathcal{N}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
such that
h_{\lambda_{0}}-h
is a multiple of
P_{\lambda_{0}}
in an open (semialgebraic) neighborhood of
Y
.

Thus, we have constructed
h_{\lambda_{0}}
both in the analytic and in the Nash case. Observe in both cases that
q_{0}:=\frac{h_{\lambda_{0}}-h}{P_{\lambda_{0}}}\in{\mathcal{O}}(U_{0})
, because
h_{\lambda_{0}}-h\in{\mathcal{O}}(U_{0})
,
\mathcal{Z}(P_{\lambda_{0}})\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}=Y
and
P_{\lambda_{0}}
divides
h_{\lambda_{0}}-h
in some neighborhood of
Y
.

As
Y\subset U_{0}
and
\mathcal{Z}(h)=X\cap U_{0}
, we deduce
h_{\lambda_{0}}|_{Y}=0
. As
\mathcal{Z}(P_{\lambda_{0}})=Y
, we have

\begin{cases}q_{0}:=\frac{h_{\lambda_{0}}-h}{P_{\lambda_{0}}}\in{\mathcal{O}}(U_{0}),\\
q_{1}:=\frac{h_{\lambda_{0}}}{P_{\lambda_{0}}}\in{\mathcal{O}}(U_{1}).\end{cases}

Observe that
\delta((q_{0},q_{1}))=q_{1}-q_{0}=\frac{h}{P_{\lambda_{0}}}=f_{01,\lambda_{0}}
(see (
6.2
)).

Sought function.

Consider the analytic (resp. semialgebraic local Nash) function on
X
defined by

q_{\lambda_{0}}:X\to{\mathbb{R}},\ z\mapsto\begin{cases}q_{0}(z)&\text{if $z\in X\cap U_{0}=X\setminus N(X)$},\\
q_{1}(z)&\text{if $z\in X\cap U_{1}=X\setminus Y$}.\end{cases}

The previous analytic (resp. semialgebraic local Nash) function is well defined on
X
, because
h
is an analytic equation of
X\cap U_{0}
inside
U_{0}
. In fact,
\xi_{\lambda_{0}}:=\frac{h_{\lambda_{0}}}{P_{\lambda_{0}}}
is a meromorphic (resp. meromorphic Nash function) function on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
such that
Y=\mathcal{Z}(P_{\lambda_{0}})\subset\mathcal{Z}(h_{\lambda_{0}})
,
\xi_{\lambda_{0}}|_{X}=q
(observe that
h|_{X\cap U_{0}}=0
) is an analytic (resp. semialgebraic local Nash) function on
X
and it has no analytic extension to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
(and consequently in the Nash case it has no Nash extension to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
), because it defines a
1
-cocyle that is not a
1
-coboundary. However, let us conclude alternatively:
\xi_{\lambda_{0}}=\frac{h_{\lambda_{0}}}{P_{\lambda_{0}}}
has no analytic extension to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

Suppose there exists an analytic function
a\in{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
such that
a|_{X}=\frac{h_{\lambda_{0}}}{P_{\lambda_{0}}}|_{X}
. Let
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
be an open neighborhood of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
to which both
h_{\lambda_{0}}
and
a
admit holomorphic extensions that we denote
H_{\lambda_{0}}
and
A
. As
(aP_{\lambda_{0}}-h_{\lambda_{0}})|_{X}=0
, we have by Lemma
3.1
that
(AP_{\lambda_{0}}-H_{\lambda_{0}})|_{\widetilde{X}}=0
. Pick a point
y\in Y\cap T(X)
and let
\beta^{y}:(-1,1)\to\widetilde{X}
be the analytic curve described in
Step 2
such that
\beta^{y}(0)=y
and
\beta^{y}((0,1))\subset(\widetilde{X}\cap\mathcal{Z}(P_{\lambda_{0}}))\setminus\mathcal{Z}(H)
. Let
V\subset V_{0}
be an invariant open neighborhood of
Y
and an invariant analytic function
B\in{\mathcal{O}}(V)
such that
H|_{V}-H_{\lambda_{0}}|_{V}=BP_{\lambda_{0}}|_{V}
. We have

\displaystyle(A\circ\beta^{y})(P_{\lambda_{0}}\circ\beta^{y})-(H_{\lambda_{0}}\circ\beta^{y})=0,

\displaystyle(H\circ\beta^{y})-(H_{\lambda_{0}}\circ\beta^{y})-(B\circ\beta^{y})(P_{\lambda_{0}}\circ\beta^{y})=0.

As
P_{\lambda_{0}}\circ\beta^{y}=0
, we deduce
H_{\lambda_{0}}\circ\beta^{y}=0
and
(H\circ\beta^{y})-(H_{\lambda_{0}}\circ\beta^{y})=0
, which is a contradiction, because
H\circ\beta^{y}\neq 0
.

Step 4.
Determining the obstructing set.
We finally prove:
Y\cap T(X)\subset{\tt O}(\xi_{\lambda_{0}})\subset Y\cap\operatorname{Cl}(T(X))
. As
\xi_{\lambda_{0}}
is analytic on
X
,
{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}={\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}
for each
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus\operatorname{Cl}(T(X))
and the set of poles of
\xi_{\lambda_{0}}
is contained in
Y
, we deduce that
{\tt O}(\xi_{\lambda_{0}})\subset Y\cap\operatorname{Cl}(T(X))
.

Pick a point
y\in Y\cap T(X)
and suppose there exists
a_{y}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},y}
such that
h_{\lambda_{0},y}-a_{y}^{\prime}P_{{\lambda_{0}},y}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{X,y}
. We keep the notations introduced above. Let
A_{y}^{\prime}
be the invariant analytic extension of
a_{y}
to
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}_{y}
and let
A^{\prime}
be an analytic representative of
A_{y}^{\prime}
defined on an open neighborhood of
y
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
. We may assume (after shrinking the domain of
\beta^{y}
and reparameterizing) that
\operatorname{im}(\beta^{y})
is contained in such open neighborhood. We have

\displaystyle(A^{\prime}\circ\beta^{y})(P_{\lambda_{0}}\circ\beta^{y})-(H_{\lambda_{0}}\circ\beta^{y})=0,

\displaystyle(H\circ\beta^{y})-(H_{\lambda_{0}}\circ\beta^{y})-(B\circ\beta^{y})(P_{\lambda_{0}}\circ\beta^{y})=0.

As
P_{\lambda_{0}}\circ\beta^{y}=0
, we deduce
H_{\lambda_{0}}\circ\beta^{y}=0
and
(H\circ\beta^{y})-(H_{\lambda_{0}}\circ\beta^{y})=0
, which is a contradiction, because
H\circ\beta^{y}\neq 0
. Consequently,
y\in{\tt O}(\xi_{\lambda_{0}})
and we deduce
Y\cap T(X)\subset{\tt O}(\xi_{\lambda_{0}})
.

This finishes the proof of both Theorem
1.4
and Theorem
1.7
.
∎

Remark 6.9
.

If
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a non-coherent analytic set, we have proceeded as follows to find
q\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\omega}(X)\setminus{\mathcal{O}}(X)
. We choose a
C
-analytic subset
Y\subset X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
that meets
T(X)
. We choose an analytic function
h
on a neighborhood
U_{0}
of
Y
such that
h_{y}\in{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}\setminus{\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}
for each
x\in Y
. We can guarantee the existence of such an analytic function if
Y
is either a discrete set (resp. finite in the Nash case) or if
Y\cap N(X)=\varnothing
(see §
6.C
). We choose a finite family of analytic functions (resp. Nash functions)
P_{1},\ldots,P_{m}
on an open neighborhood (resp. open semialgebraic neighborhood)
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
such that
\widetilde{Y}:=\mathcal{Z}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{m})
is a complexification of
Y
. We consider the family of invariant analytic functions (resp. Nash functions)
P_{\lambda}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}H_{i}^{2}
for
\lambda\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{m}
. We choose suitable
\lambda_{0}\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{m}
such that
f_{01,\lambda_{0}}:=\frac{h}{P_{\lambda_{0}}}
defines a
1
-cocycle of
H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathcal{J}}_{X})
that is not a
1
-coboundary. From this
1
-cocycle we construct
q
finding
h_{\lambda_{0}}\in{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
such that
h_{\lambda_{0}}-h
is divisible by
P_{\lambda_{0}}
in a neighborhood of
Y
. The chosen
q\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\omega}(X)\setminus{\mathcal{O}}(X)
is the restriction to
X
of the meromorphic function
\xi_{\lambda_{0}}:=\frac{h_{\lambda_{0}}}{P_{\lambda_{0}}}
. In case
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a Nash set we may choose
h\in{\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}(X\setminus N(X))
and
h_{\lambda_{0}}\in{\mathcal{N}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
. Thus,
\xi_{\lambda_{0}}:=\frac{h_{\lambda_{0}}}{P_{\lambda_{0}}}
defines an element
q\in{\mathcal{C}}^{{{\mathcal{N}}}}(X)\setminus{\mathcal{N}}(X)
.

\sqbullet

6.E.
An application: ‘non-global’ models for non-coherent objects

From the previous results let us deduce the existence of ‘non-global’ models for each non-coherent subsets of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
both in the analytic and in the Nash settings.

Corollary 6.10
.

Let
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a non-coherent
C
-analytic set. There exist many real analytic sets
X^{*}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
that are not a
C
-analytic sets, but
X^{*}
is analytically diffeomorphic to
X
.

Corollary 6.11
.

Let
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a non-coherent Nash set. There exist many semialgebraic sets
X^{*}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
that are locally Nash sets, but not Nash subsets of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
, such that
X^{*}
is local Nash diffeomorphic to
X
.

Remark 6.12
.

As coherence of real analytic sets is a local property, it is preserved by (local) analytic diffeomorphims. Thus, a real analytic set that is analytically diffeomorphic to a coherent real analytic set, it is a
C
-analytic set. Analogously, as coherence of Nash sets is a local property, it is preserved by local Nash diffeomorphims. Consequently, a semialgebraic set that is locally a Nash set and local Nash diffeomorphic to a coherent Nash set, it is a Nash set.

\sqbullet

We proceed with the proof of Corollaries
6.10
and
6.11
keeping the notation already introduced along the proof of Theorems
6.6
and
6.7
.

Proof of Corollaries
6.10
and
6.11
.

We continue from the end of
Step 3
and keep all the notations already introduced in the proof of Theorems
1.4
and
1.7
.

Step 4.

Construction of an analytic (resp. semialgebraic local Nash) set
X^{*}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
that is analytically diffeomorphic (Nash diffeomorphic) to
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

Define
X^{*}:={\rm graph}(\xi_{\lambda_{0}}|_{X})
, which is an analytic (resp. semialgebraic local Nash) set analytically diffeomorphic (resp. local Nash diffeomorphic) to
X
via the mutually inverse analytic maps (resp. local Nash maps):

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\lambda_{0}}:

\displaystyle X\to X^{*},\ x\mapsto(x,\xi_{\lambda_{0}}(x)),

\displaystyle\pi:

\displaystyle X^{*}\to X,\ (x,y)\mapsto x.

By Lemma
2.6
the proof of both Corollaries
6.10
and
6.11
reduces to check:
X^{*}
is not a
C
-analytic set
.

Recall that
Y=\mathcal{Z}(P_{\lambda_{0}})
and consider the
C
-analytic set (resp. Nash set)

Z:=(X\times{\mathbb{R}})\cap\mathcal{Z}(P_{\lambda_{0}}{\tt x}_{n+1}-h_{\lambda_{0}})

of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
. If
L:=Y\times{\mathbb{R}}
, we claim:
Z=X^{*}\cup L
.

The inclusion right to left is clear, because
X^{*}={\rm graph}(\xi_{\lambda_{0}}|_{X})
and
Y=\mathcal{Z}(P_{\lambda_{0}})\subset\mathcal{Z}(h_{\lambda_{0}})
. Pick a point
(x,t)\in Z
. If
x\not\in Y
, then
P_{\lambda_{0}}(x)\neq 0
and
(x,t)\in X^{*}
. If
x\in Y
, then
(x,t)\in L
.

Step 5.

The analytic set
X^{*}
is not a
C
-analytic subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
and the obstruction concentrates at the points of
((Y\cap T(X))\times{\mathbb{R}})\cap X^{*}
.
Assume that
X^{*}
is a
C
-analytic set, let
\widetilde{X^{*}}
be an invariant complexification of
X^{*}
and let
\widetilde{X}
be an invariant complexification of
X
. Let
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n+1}
be an invariant Stein open neighborhood of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n+1}
such that
\widetilde{X^{*}}
is a complex analytic subset of
\Omega
(maybe after shrinking
\widetilde{X^{*}}
). Let
\Theta\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
be the image of
\Omega
under the projection
\Pi:{\mathbb{C}}^{n+1}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{n},\ (z,z_{n+1})\mapsto z
, which is an open subset of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
. Shrinking
\Omega
and
\Theta
if necessary, we may assume
\widetilde{X}
is a complex analytic subset of
\Theta
(maybe after shrinking
\widetilde{X}
). We may assume
h_{\lambda_{0}},P_{\lambda_{0}}
have invariant holomorphic extensions
H_{\lambda_{0}},P_{\lambda_{0}}
to
\Theta
. Denote by
\Xi_{\lambda_{0}}:=\frac{H_{\lambda_{0}}}{P_{\lambda_{0}}}
the meromorphic extension of
\xi_{\lambda_{0}}
to
\Theta
. By the identity principle

\widetilde{X^{*}}\setminus\mathcal{Z}(P_{\lambda_{0}}\circ\Pi)=\{(z,\Xi_{\lambda_{0}}(z))\in\Omega:\ z\in\widetilde{X}\setminus\mathcal{Z}(P_{\lambda_{0}})\}.

As
h-h_{\lambda_{0}}=P_{\lambda_{0}}b
on an open neighborhood of
Y
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
(where
b
is an analytic function on such open neighborhood of
Y
), we may assume that there exists an invariant holomorphic extension
B
of
b
to
V_{0}
such that
H-H_{\lambda_{0}}=P_{\lambda_{0}}B
. This means that
H
and
H_{\lambda_{0}}
take the same values on
\mathcal{Z}(P_{\lambda_{0}})\cap V_{0}
. Recall that
D:=\{y_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
is a dense countable subset of
Y\cap T(X)
. Write
\rho_{j}:=-B(y_{j})
and observe that if
\mu\neq\rho_{j},0
for each
j\geq 1
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mu P_{\lambda_{0}}-H_{\lambda_{0}},P_{\lambda_{0}})_{y_{j}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}(P_{\lambda_{0}},H_{\lambda_{0}})_{y_{j}}=\mathcal{Z}(P_{\lambda_{0}},H-P_{\lambda_{0}}B)_{y_{j}}=\mathcal{Z}(P_{\lambda_{0}},H)_{y_{j}},

(6.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mu P_{\lambda_{0}}-H_{\lambda_{0}},H)_{y_{j}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}(\mu P_{\lambda_{0}}-H_{\lambda_{0}}+H,H)_{y_{j}}=\mathcal{Z}(\mu P_{\lambda_{0}}-P_{\lambda_{0}}B,H)_{y_{j}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}((\mu+B)P_{\lambda_{0}},H)_{y_{j}}=\mathcal{Z}(P_{\lambda_{0}},H)_{y_{j}}.

(6.4)

In addition,
y_{j}\in\mathcal{Z}(P_{\lambda_{0}},H_{\lambda_{0}})
, so
y_{j}\in\mathcal{Z}(\mu P_{\lambda_{0}}-H_{\lambda_{0}})
for each
\mu\in{\mathbb{R}}
. As
\widetilde{X}_{y_{j}}\setminus\mathcal{Z}(H_{y_{j}})\neq\varnothing
, there exists for each
j\geq 1
an irreducible component
T_{j,y_{j}}
of
\widetilde{X}_{y_{j}}
such that
T_{j,y_{j}}\not\subset\mathcal{Z}(H_{y_{j}})
, so by
[
E
, Cor.10.9]

\dim(T_{j,y_{j}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(H_{y_{j}}))=\dim(T_{j,y_{j}})-1.

(6.5)

By Lemma
6.3
and Baire’s Theorem we may choose
{\lambda_{0}}\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{m}
(from the beginning) such that

\dim(T_{j,y_{j}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(H,P_{\lambda_{0}})_{y_{j}})<\dim(T_{j,y_{j}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(H)_{y_{j}})

(6.6)

for each
j\geq 1
. We claim:
\widetilde{X}_{y_{j}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(\mu P_{\lambda_{0}}-H_{\lambda_{0}})_{y_{j}}\not\subset\mathcal{Z}(HP_{\lambda_{0}})_{y_{j}}
for each
j\geq 1
and each
\mu\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus(\{\rho_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}\cup\{0\})
.

Otherwise, there exist
j_{0}\geq 1
and
\mu_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus(\{\rho_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}\cup\{0\})
such that
\widetilde{X}_{y_{j_{0}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(\mu_{0}P_{\lambda_{0}}-H_{\lambda_{0}})_{y_{j_{0}}}\subset\mathcal{Z}(HP_{\lambda_{0}})_{y_{j_{0}}}
. By (
6.3
) and (
6.4
)

\begin{split}\widetilde{X}_{y_{j_{0}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(&\mu_{0}P_{\lambda_{0}}-H_{\lambda_{0}})_{y_{j_{0}}}\\
&=\widetilde{X}_{y_{j_{0}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(\mu_{0}P_{\lambda_{0}}-H_{\lambda_{0}})_{y_{j_{0}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(HP_{\lambda_{0}})_{y_{j_{0}}}=\widetilde{X}_{y_{j_{0}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(\mu_{0}P_{\lambda_{0}}-H_{\lambda_{0}},HP_{\lambda_{0}})_{y_{j_{0}}}\\
&=(\widetilde{X}_{y_{j_{0}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(\mu_{0}P_{\lambda_{0}}-H_{\lambda_{0}},P_{\lambda_{0}})_{y_{j_{0}}})\cup(\widetilde{X}_{y_{j_{0}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(\mu_{0}P_{\lambda_{0}}-H_{\lambda_{0}},H)_{y_{j_{0}}})\\
&=\widetilde{X}_{y_{j_{0}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(P_{\lambda_{0}},H)_{y_{j_{0}}}.\end{split}

On the other hand, for each irreducible component
T_{y_{j_{0}}}
of
\widetilde{X}_{y_{j_{0}}}
, we have by
[
E
, Cor.10.9]

\dim(T_{y_{j_{0}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(\mu_{0}P_{\lambda_{0}}-H_{\lambda_{0}})_{y_{j_{0}}})\geq\dim(T_{y_{j_{0}}})-1.

(6.7)

In addition,

T_{y_{j_{0}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(\mu_{0}P_{\lambda_{0}}-H_{\lambda_{0}})_{y_{j_{0}}}=T_{y_{j_{0}}}\cap\widetilde{X}_{y_{j_{0}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(\mu_{0}P_{\lambda_{0}}-H_{\lambda_{0}})_{y_{j_{0}}}\\
=T_{y_{j_{0}}}\cap\widetilde{X}_{y_{j_{0}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(P_{\lambda_{0}},H)_{y_{j_{0}}}=T_{y_{j_{0}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(P_{\lambda_{0}},H)_{y_{j_{0}}}

for each irreducible component
T_{y_{j_{0}}}
of
\widetilde{X}_{y_{j_{0}}}
. By (
6.5
), (
6.6
) and (
6.7
) there exists an irreducible component
T_{j_{0},y_{j_{0}}}
of
\widetilde{X}_{y_{j_{0}}}
such that

\dim(T_{j_{0},y_{j_{0}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(H,P_{\lambda_{0}})_{y_{j_{0}}})<\dim(T_{j_{0},y_{j_{0}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(H)_{y_{j_{0}}})\\
=\dim(T_{j_{0},y_{j_{0}}})-1\leq\dim(T_{j_{0},y_{j_{0}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(\mu_{0}P_{\lambda_{0}}-H_{\lambda_{0}})_{y_{j_{0}}}),

which is a contradiction. Thus,
\widetilde{X}_{y_{j}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(\mu P_{\lambda_{0}}-H_{\lambda_{0}})_{y_{j}}\not\subset\mathcal{Z}(HP_{\lambda_{0}})_{y_{j}}
for each
j\geq 1
and each
\mu\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus(\{\rho_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}\cup\{0\})
, as claimed.

Pick
y\in Y\cap T(X)
and let us check:
\widetilde{X}_{y}\cap\mathcal{Z}(\mu P_{\lambda_{0}}-H_{\lambda_{0}})_{y}\not\subset\mathcal{Z}(HP_{\lambda_{0}})_{y}
.

Otherwise,
\widetilde{X}_{y}\cap\mathcal{Z}(\mu P_{\lambda_{0}}-H_{\lambda_{0}})_{y}\subset\mathcal{Z}(HP_{\lambda_{0}})_{y}
, so there exists an open neighborhood
W^{y}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
such that
\widetilde{X}\cap\mathcal{Z}(\mu P_{\lambda_{0}}-H_{\lambda_{0}})\cap W^{y}\subset\mathcal{Z}(HP_{\lambda_{0}})\cap W^{y}
. As
D
is dense in
Y\cap T(X)
, there exists
y_{j}\in W^{y}
, so
\widetilde{X}_{y_{j}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(\mu P_{\lambda_{0}}-H_{\lambda_{0}})_{y_{j}}\subset\mathcal{Z}(HP_{\lambda_{0}})_{y_{j}}
, which is a contradiction, because
\widetilde{X}_{y_{j}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(\mu P_{\lambda_{0}}-H_{\lambda_{0}})_{y_{j}}\not\subset\mathcal{Z}(HP_{\lambda_{0}})_{y_{j}}
for each
j\geq 1
.

Fix
y\in Y\cap T(X)
. By the curve selection lemma
[
ABR
, Rem.VII.4.3(b)]
(using the underlying real structures of all the involved complex analytic sets) there exists an analytic arc

\alpha^{y}:(-1,1)\to((\widetilde{X}\cap\mathcal{Z}(\mu P_{\lambda_{0}}-H_{\lambda_{0}}))\setminus\mathcal{Z}(HP_{\lambda_{0}}))\cup\{y\}

such that
(\alpha^{y})^{-1}(y)=\{0\}
. Thus,
\Xi_{\lambda_{0}}\circ\alpha_{\mu}=\frac{H_{\lambda_{0}}\circ\alpha_{\mu}}{P_{\lambda_{0}}\circ\alpha_{\mu}}=\mu
for each
\mu\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0,\rho_{j}:\ j\geq 1\}
, so
L\setminus\{(y,0),(y,\rho_{j}):\ j\geq 1\}\subset\widetilde{X^{*}}\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}=X^{*}
. As
{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0,\rho_{j}:\ j\geq 1\}
is (by Baire’s Theorem) dense in
{\mathbb{R}}
, we conclude
L\subset X^{*}
, so
Z=X^{*}\cup L=X^{*}
, which is a contradiction. We conclude
X^{*}
is not a
C
-analytic subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
and the obstruction to be a
C
-analytic set concentrates at the points of
((Y\cap T(X))\times{\mathbb{R}})\cap X^{*}
, as required.
∎

7.
Smooth semialgebraic functions versus Nash functions

The intrinsic definition of semialgebraic differentiable function is a classical problem that goes back to Whitney’s extension theorem §
1.A
. Inspired by the latter and based on jets of semialgebraic functions, we recall a notion of differentiable semialgebraic function of intrinsic nature proposed in
[
ATh
]
, which is motivated by the proofs of Whitney’s extension theorem collected in
[
M
, §1]
and
[
KP2
,
Th
]
.

7.A.
Differentiable semialgebraic functions

Recall that we denote
{\mathbb{N}}:=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
the set of natural numbers including
0
.

Definitions 7.1
.

Let
S\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a semialgebraic set and denote
{\tt x}:=({\tt x}_{1},\ldots,{\tt x}_{n})
. A
semialgebraic jet on
S
of order
p\geq 0
is a collection of semialgebraic functions
F:=(f_{\alpha})_{|\alpha|\leq p}
on
S
. Here we denote
\alpha:=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}
and
|\alpha|:=\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}
. For each
a\in S
write

T_{a}^{p}F:=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq p}\frac{f_{\alpha}(a)}{\alpha!}({\tt x}-a)^{\alpha}\quad\text{and}\quad R_{a}^{p}F:=f_{0}-T_{a}^{p}F.

Denote the set of all semialgebraic jets on
S
of order
p
with
{\mathcal{J}}^{p}(S)
, which has a natural structure of
{\mathbb{R}}
-vector space. For every
\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}
such that
|\beta|\leq p
we denote

D^{\beta}:{\mathcal{J}}^{p}(S)\to{\mathcal{J}}^{p-|\beta|}(S),\ F:=((f_{\alpha})_{|\alpha|\leq p})\mapsto F_{\beta}:=(f_{\gamma+\beta})_{|\gamma|\leq p-|\beta|},

that is a linear map.

Definition 7.2

(Def.1)
.

A continuous semialgebraic function
f:S\to{\mathbb{R}}
is a
{\mathcal{C}}^{p}
semialgebraic function if there exists a semialgebraic jet
F:=(f_{\alpha})_{|\alpha|\leq p}
on
S
of order
p
such that:

(C.1)

f_{0}=f
and for each
\beta
with
|\beta|\leq p
and every point
a\in S
it holds
|R_{x}^{p-|\beta|}F_{\beta}(y)|=o(\|x-y\|^{p-|\beta|})
for
x,y\in S
when
x,y\to a
,

that is, the function
\tau:(0,+\infty)\to{\mathbb{R}}
defined by

\tau(t):=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in S,\ x\neq y\\
\|x-a\|\leq t,\ \|y-a\|\leq t\end{subarray}}\frac{|R_{x}^{p-|\beta|}F_{\beta}(y)|}{\|x-y\|^{p-|\beta|}}

is an increasing function that can be continuously extended to
t=0
by
\tau(0)=0
.

We denote by
{\mathcal{S}}^{p}(S)
the collection of all the
{\mathcal{C}}^{p}
semialgebraic functions on
S
(shortly, denoted as
{\mathcal{S}}^{p}
-functions).

Remark 7.3
.

Condition
(C.1)
is equivalent by
[
M
, I.Thm.2.2]
to the following one:

(C.2)

f_{0}=f
and for each point
a\in S
there exists an increasing, continuous and concave function
\alpha_{a}:[0,+\infty)\to[0,+\infty)
such that
\alpha_{a}(0)=0
and for each
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
it holds

|T_{x}^{p}F(z)-T_{y}^{p}F(z)|\leq\alpha_{a}(\|x-y\|)\cdot(\|z-x\|^{p}+\|z-y\|^{p})

for
x,y\in S
when
x,y\to a
.
\sqbullet

There are other alternatives to define
{\mathcal{S}}^{p}
-functions on a general semialgebraic set
S\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. We recall some of them:

(Def.2)

There exist an open semialgebraic neighborhood
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
of
S
and a
{\mathcal{S}}^{p}
-function
F:U\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that
F|_{S}=f
.

(Def.3)

For each point
x\in S
there exist an open semialgebraic neighborhood
U^{x}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
of
x
and an
{\mathcal{S}}^{p}
-function
F_{x}:U^{x}\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that
F_{x}|_{U^{x}\cap S}=f|_{U^{x}\cap S}
.

(Def.4)

For each point
x\in S
there exist an open neighborhood
U^{x}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
of
x
and an
{\mathcal{C}}^{p}
differentiable function
F_{x}:U^{x}\to{\mathbb{R}}
(non-necessarily semialgebraic) such that
F_{x}|_{U^{x}\cap S}=f|_{U^{x}\cap S}
.

(Def.5)

There exist an open neighborhood
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
of
S
and a
{\mathcal{C}}^{p}
differentiable function
F:U\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that
F|_{S}=f
.

Observe that (Def.2)
\Longrightarrow
(Def.3)
\Longrightarrow
(Def.4)
\iff
(Def.5) and (Def.2)
\Longrightarrow
Definition
7.2
. The implication (Def.4)
\Longrightarrow
(Def.5) follows considering a
{\mathcal{C}}^{p}
-partition of unity. Analogously, if
S\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a compact semialgebraic set, (Def.3)
\Longrightarrow
(Def.2) using an
{\mathcal{S}}^{p}
-partition of unity. If
S\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a closed semialgebraic set, Definition
7.2
implies (Def.2), see
[
KP2
,
Th
]
. If in addition
n=2
, the authors prove in
[
FL
]
that (Def.5) implies (Def.2), whereas in
[
ATh
]
the authors show that (Def.5) implies (Def.2) if
p=1
and
n\geq 1
. In
[
BCM
, Cor.1.7]
the authors present a weak version of ‘(Def.5) implies (Def.2)’ via an intermediate function
t:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{N}}
, which encodes a certain loss of differentiability. Namely, if there exist an open semialgebraic neighborhood
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
of a closed semialgebraic set
S\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and a
{\mathcal{C}}^{t(p)}
differentiable function
G:U\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that
G|_{S}=f
, then there exists an
{\mathcal{S}}^{p}
-function
F:U\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that
F|_{S}=f
. In the general case (that is,
S\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a general semialgebraic set), Definition
7.2
implies the existence of a
{\mathcal{S}}^{p-1}(U)
function
F
on an open semialgebraic neighborhood
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
of
S
, see
[
BFG
, Lem.2.6]
.

7.B.
Smooth semialgebraic functions and Nash functions

A classical result in real analytic geometry
[
BCR
, Prop.8.1.8]
states that a function on a Nash manifold
S\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is Nash (smooth and semialgebraic) if and only if it is an analytic algebraic function on
S
. Thus, one can wonder if a similar result holds for general semialgebraic subsets of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. We begin by defining
{\mathcal{S}}^{(\infty)}(S)
. Denote

{\mathcal{S}}^{(\infty)}(S):=\bigcap_{p\geq 0}{\mathcal{S}}^{p}(S)

(7.1)

the ring of smooth semialgebraic functions on
S
, where
{\mathcal{S}}^{p}(S)
is the ring of
{\mathcal{S}}^{p}
functions on
S
following Definition
7.2
. We analyze first the local case and for the sake of completeness we borrow the following result from
[
BFG
]
.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
S\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a semialgebraic set and let
f\in{\mathcal{S}}^{(\infty)}(S)
. For each
x\in S
there exists a Nash function germ
F_{x}\in{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
such that
F_{x}|_{S_{x}}=f_{x}
.

Proof.

Let
f\in{\mathcal{S}}^{(\infty)}(S)
and assume
x=0\in S
. For each
p\geq 0
let
F_{p}:=(f^{p}_{\alpha})_{|\alpha|\leq p}
be a semialgebraic jet of order
p
associated to
f
. We may assume:
f^{p}_{\alpha}(0)=f_{\alpha}^{k}(0)
for each pair of integers
p\leq k
and each
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}
with
|\alpha|\leq p
. Thus, the definition
f_{\alpha}(0):=f_{\alpha}^{p}(0)
if
|\alpha|=p
is consistent.

To that end, recall first that
f_{0}^{p}=f
for each
p\geq 0
. Next, replace recursively the semialgebraic jet
F_{p+1}
by
\widetilde{F}_{p+1}:=(\widetilde{f}^{p+1}_{\alpha})
where
\widetilde{f}_{\alpha}^{p+1}(x):=f_{\alpha}^{p+1}(x)-f^{p+1}_{\alpha}(0)+\tilde{f}^{p}_{\alpha}(0)
whenever
|\alpha|\leq p
. Using property
(C.2)
one shows that
\widetilde{F}_{p}
is a semialgebraic jet associated to
f
as a
{\mathcal{S}}^{p}
-function and
\tilde{f}_{\alpha}^{k}(0)=\tilde{f}_{\alpha}^{p}(0)
if
k\geq p
and
|\alpha|\leq p
.

Consider the formal series

h_{0}:=\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}}\frac{1}{\alpha!}f_{\alpha}(0){\tt x}^{\alpha}\in{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]]

(7.2)

and let
S_{0}
be the germ at the origin of
S
. For simplicity we identify
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},0}
with
{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]]_{\rm alg}
. Let us prove:
f|_{S_{0}}
is the germ of a Nash function on
S_{0}
.

Let
\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{s}
be a Nash stratification of
S_{0}
(apply
[
BCR
, §9.1]
to a suitable representative of
S_{0}
). Let
Z_{i}
be the Nash closure of the germ
\Delta_{i}
, which is an irreducible Nash set germ (because
\Delta_{i}
is a connected germ at point of the closure of a Nash stratum), and let
X_{i}
be the Nash closure of the graph
\Gamma_{i}:=\Gamma(f|_{\Delta_{i}})
. It holds
\dim(X_{i})=\dim(\Gamma_{i})=\dim(\Delta_{i})=\dim(Z_{i})
and
X_{i}
is an irreducible Nash germ (because it is the Nash closure of the graph of a Nash function germ
f|_{\Delta_{i}}
on a connected germ
\Delta_{i}
at point of the closure of a Nash manifold).

To ease notation write for a while
\Delta:=\Delta_{i}
,
Z:=Z_{i}
,
\Gamma:=\Gamma_{i}
and
X:=X_{i}
. Let
g({\tt x},{\tt y})\in{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x},{\tt y}]]_{\rm alg}
be such that
X=\mathcal{Z}(g)
. We claim:
g({\tt x},h_{0}({\tt x}))\in{\mathcal{I}}(Z){\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]]
.

Let
{\mathfrak{G}}
be the collection of the germs
\gamma_{0}
at the origin of (continuous) semialgebraic curves
\gamma:[0,1)\to\Delta\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
such that
\gamma(0)=0
. We identify each germ
\gamma_{0}
with a Puiseux tuple
{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt t}^{*}]]^{n}
. For each
\gamma\in{\mathfrak{G}}
define the homomorphism

\gamma^{*}:{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]]\to{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt t}^{*}]],\ \zeta\mapsto\zeta\circ\gamma.

By the curve selection lemma
[
BCR
, Thm.2.5.5]
we have
{\mathcal{I}}(Z)=\bigcap_{\gamma\in{\mathfrak{G}}}\ker(\gamma^{*})\cap{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]]_{\rm alg}
. The completion of the local noetherian ring
{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]]_{\rm alg}
is
{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]]
. We have

{\mathcal{I}}(Z){\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]]=\Big(\bigcap_{\gamma\in{\mathfrak{G}}}\ker(\gamma^{*})\cap{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]]_{\rm alg}\Big){\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]]=\bigcap_{\gamma\in{\mathfrak{G}}}(\ker(\gamma^{*})\cap{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]]_{\rm alg}){\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]]=\bigcap_{\gamma\in{\mathfrak{G}}}\ker(\gamma^{*}).

Assume
\xi:=g({\tt x},h_{0}({\tt x}))\not\in{\mathcal{I}}(Z){\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]]
. Then there exists
\gamma\in{\mathfrak{G}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt t}^{*}]]^{n}_{\rm alg}
such that
\xi\notin\ker(\gamma^{*})
. After reparameterizing the variable
{\tt t}
we may assume
\gamma\in{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt t}]]^{n}_{\rm alg}
. Let
k\geq 1
be the order of the series
\xi(\gamma)
. Write
h_{0}=a+b
where
a:=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}\frac{1}{\alpha!}f_{\alpha}(0){\tt x}^{\alpha}\in{\mathbb{R}}[{\tt x}]
has degree
\leq k
and
b
has order
>k
. There exists a power series
s\in{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x},{\tt z}_{1},{\tt z}_{2}]]
such that

g({\tt x},{\tt z}_{1}+{\tt z}_{2})=g({\tt x},{\tt z}_{1})+{\tt z}_{2}s({\tt x},{\tt z}_{1},{\tt z}_{2}).

(7.3)

Consequently,

g({\tt x},h_{0})=g({\tt x},a+b)=g({\tt x},a)+bs({\tt x},a,b)

for some
s\in{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x},{\tt z}_{1},{\tt z}_{2}]]
. Thus,

\xi(\gamma)=g(\gamma,a(\gamma))+bs(\gamma,a(\gamma),b(\gamma)).

As
\omega(\xi(\gamma))=k
and
\omega(bs({\tt x},a,b))\geq k+1
, we deduce
\omega(g({\tt x},a)(\gamma))=k
. Let us analyze next the Puiseux series
f(\gamma)\in{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt t}^{*}]]
. We have

|f(y)-a(y)|=\Big|f(y)-\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}\frac{1}{\alpha!}f_{\alpha}(0)y^{\alpha}\Big|=o(\|y\|^{k})

(7.4)

for
y\in S
when
y\to 0
. Thus,

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\Big(\frac{f(\gamma(t))-a(\gamma(t))}{t^{k}}\Big)=0,

so
f(\gamma)-a(\gamma)\in{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt t}^{*}]]
has order strictly greater than
k
. By (
7.3
) we deduce

\omega(g(\gamma,f(\gamma))-g(\gamma,a(\gamma)))>k,

so
\omega(g((\gamma),f(\gamma)))=k
, which is a contradiction, because
g(\gamma(t),f(\gamma(t)))=0
for each
t\in[0,1)
(recall that
\gamma\in{\mathfrak{G}}
and
X=\mathcal{Z}(g)
is the Zariski closure of
\Gamma(f|_{\Delta})
). Consequently,
g({\tt x},h_{0}({\tt x}))\in{\mathcal{I}}(Z){\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]]
, as claimed.

Let
f_{i1},\ldots,f_{im}\in{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]]_{\rm alg}
be a system of generators of
I(Z_{i})
and let
g_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x},{\tt y}]]_{\rm alg}
be such that
\mathcal{Z}(g_{i})=X_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,s
. We know that
g_{i}({\tt x},h_{0}({\tt x}))\in I(Z_{i}){\mathbb{R}}[[x]]
. Thus, there exist
a_{i1},\ldots,a_{im}\in{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]]
such that
g_{i}({\tt x},h_{0})=a_{i1}f_{i1}+\cdots+a_{im}f_{im}
for
i=1,\ldots,s
. By Artin’s approximation theorem
[
BCR
, Thm.8.3.1]
there exist Nash functions germs
F,\widetilde{a}_{i1},\ldots,\widetilde{a}_{im}\in{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]]_{\rm alg}
such that
g_{i}({\tt x},F)=\widetilde{a}_{i1}f_{i1}+\cdots+\widetilde{a}_{im}f_{im}
for each
i=1,\ldots,s
. Consequently,
\Gamma(F|_{Z_{i}})\subset X_{i}
for each
i=1,\ldots,s
. As
Z_{i}
is an irreducible Nash set and
F
is a Nash function germ,
\Gamma(F|_{Z_{i}})
is an irreducible Nash set of dimension
\dim(Z_{i})=\dim(X_{i})
. As
X_{i}
is irreducible,
X_{i}=\Gamma(F|_{Z_{i}})
for
i=1,\ldots,s
. Thus,
F|_{\Delta_{i}}=X_{i}\cap(\Delta_{i}\times{\mathbb{R}})=\Gamma(f|_{\Delta_{i}})
for
i=1,\ldots,s
, because each
X_{i}
is the graph of
F
over
Z_{i}
and simultaneously it is the Nash closure of
\Gamma(f|_{\Delta_{i}})
for
i=1,\ldots,s
. We conclude
F|_{\Delta_{i}}=f|_{\Delta_{i}}
for
i=1,\ldots,s
, so
F|_{S_{0}}=f_{0}
, as required.
∎

Remark 7.5
.

All the definitions above of
{\mathcal{S}}^{p}
-function on a semialgebraic point
S
provide the same definition of
{\mathcal{S}}^{(\infty)}
function germ at each point
x\in S
.

In the proof of Lemma
7.4
we have used that
f\in{\mathcal{S}}^{(\infty)}(S)
only to show that there exists a formal power series
h:=\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}}b_{\alpha}{\tt x}^{\alpha}\in{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]]
such that for each
k\geq 1

\Big|f(y)-\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}b_{\alpha}y^{\alpha}\Big|=o(\|y\|^{k})

for
y\in S
when
y\to 0
.

It is enough show:
Each alternative definition of
{\mathcal{S}}^{p}
function provides a formal power series
h
satisfying the previous condition.

As (Def.2)
\Longrightarrow
(Def.3)
\Longrightarrow
(Def.4)
\iff
(Def.5), it is enough to prove the existence of
h
for (Def.4). Then, for each
p\geq 0
there exists an open neighborhood
U^{x}_{p}
and a
{\mathcal{C}}^{p}
differentiable function
F_{p,x}
on
U^{x}_{p}
such that
F_{p,x}|_{S\cap U^{x}_{p}}=f|_{S\cap U^{x}_{p}}
. We change recursively
F_{p+1,x}
by

\widetilde{F}_{p+1,x}:=F_{p,x}-\sum_{|\alpha|\leq p-1}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|}\widetilde{F}_{p,x}}{\partial{\tt x}^{\alpha}}(x)({\tt x}-x)^{\alpha}

and
\widetilde{F}_{0,x}=F_{0,x}
. Then

h:=\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\frac{\widetilde{F}_{|\alpha|,x}}{\partial{\tt x}^{\alpha}}(0){\tt x}^{\alpha}\in{\mathbb{R}}[[{\tt x}]]

satisfies the required conditions.
\sqbullet

Definition 7.6
.

We define the ring
{\mathcal{N}}(S)
of
Nash functions on the semialgebraic set
S\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
as the restrictions to
S
of the elements of the ring
H^{0}(S,({\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})|_{S})
of global cross-sections on
S
of the coherent sheaf
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
.

Remark 7.7
.

By
[
FGR
, Thm.1.3]
we have
H^{0}(S,({\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})|_{S})=\displaystyle\lim_{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{N}}(V)
where
V\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
covers the open semialgebraic neighborhoods of
S
.
\sqbullet

It is clear that
{\mathcal{N}}(S)\subset{\mathcal{S}}^{(\infty)}(S)
and at this point we wonder:

Problem 7.8
.

Under which conditions the inclusion of rings
{\mathcal{N}}(S)\subset{\mathcal{S}}^{(\infty)}(S)
is an equality?

7.C.
Nash sets

We begin considering the case when
S=X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a Nash set. Let us prove the equality between the rings
{\mathcal{S}}^{(\infty)}(X)
with
{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathcal{N}}}(X)
.

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a Nash set. Then
{\mathcal{S}}^{(\infty)}(X)={\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathcal{N}}}(X)
.

Proof.

The inclusion
{\mathcal{S}}^{(\infty)}(X)\subset{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathcal{N}}}(X)
follows from Lemma
7.4
. To prove the converse inclusion pick
f\in{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathcal{N}}}(X)=H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{J}}_{X}^{\bullet})
and let us check:
f
is a semialgebraic function
.

Observe that
f|_{X\setminus N(X)}\in H^{0}(X\setminus N(X),{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{J}}_{X}^{\bullet})={\mathcal{N}}(X\setminus N(X))
(see Remark
3.11
). By Remark
3.10
(ii) we know that
X\setminus N(X)
is dense in
X
. As
f:X\to{\mathbb{R}}
is a continuous function and
f|_{X\setminus N(X)}
is semialgebraic, we conclude that
f
itself is semialgebraic (because the graph of
f
is the closure of the graph of
f|_{X\setminus N(X)}
, so it is a semialgebraic set).

As
f\in{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathcal{N}}}(X)
, we deduce that
f
is the restriction to
X
of a
{\mathcal{C}}^{p}
differentiable function on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
for each
p\geq 1
. By
[
BCM
, Cor.1.7]
we conclude that
f
is the restriction to
X
of a
{\mathcal{C}}^{p}
semialgebraic function on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
for each
p\geq 1
, so
f\in{\mathcal{S}}^{(\infty)}(X)
, as required.
∎

We have already proven in Corollary
2.15
that
{\mathcal{N}}(X)=H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{I}}_{X}^{\bullet})
for each Nash set
X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. By Theorem
1.7
we have:
{\mathcal{S}}^{(\infty)}(X)={\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathcal{N}}}(X)={\mathcal{N}}(X)
if and only if
X
is coherent (that is, if
{\mathcal{J}}_{X,x}^{\bullet}={\mathcal{I}}_{X,x}^{\bullet}
for each
x\in X
).

7.D.
Semialgebraic sets

Let
S\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a semialgebraic set and let
{\mathcal{J}}_{S}^{\bullet}
be the sheaf of
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
-ideals given by

{\mathcal{J}}_{S,x}^{\bullet}:=\{f_{x}\in{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}:\ S_{x}\subset\mathcal{Z}(f_{x})\},

(7.5)

the germs of Nash functions on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{x}
vanishing identically on
S_{x}
for each
x\in U
.

7.D.1.
Ring of smooth semialgebraic functions

We prove next (taking again advantage of
[
BCM
, Cor.1.7]
) the following characterization of the ring
{\mathcal{S}}^{(\infty)}(S)
when
S
is a locally compact semialgebraic set:

Theorem 7.10
.

Let
S\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a locally compact semialgebraic set and let
f:S\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a function. The following conditions are equivalent:

(i)

f\in{\mathcal{S}}^{(\infty)}(S)
.

(ii)

f\in{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathcal{N}}}(S):=H^{0}(S,{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}/{\mathcal{J}}_{S}^{\bullet})
.

(iii)

f
is semialgebraic and for each
x\in S
there exists a Nash function germ
F_{x}\in{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n},x}
such that
F_{x}|_{S_{x}}=f_{x}
.

Proof.

(i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii) The inclusion
{\mathcal{S}}^{(\infty)}(S)\subset{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathcal{N}}}(S)
follows from Lemma
7.4
.

(ii)
\Longrightarrow
(iii) Let us prove that if
f\in{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathcal{N}}}(S)
, then
f
is semialgebraic. The other condition follows from the definition of
{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathcal{N}}}(S)
. Let
U
be the open semialgebraic set and
X\subset U
the Nash set described above. Let
T
be the (semialgebraic) set of points of
S\setminus\operatorname{Sing}(X)
of dimension
d:=\dim(S)
and
M_{d}
the interior of
T
in
X\setminus\operatorname{Sing}(X)
. Thus,
M_{d}\subset X\setminus N(X)
is a Nash manifold of dimension
d
and it is dense in the set
S_{(d)}
of points of
S
of dimension
d
. Observe that
{\mathcal{J}}^{\bullet}_{S}|_{M_{d}}={\mathcal{J}}^{\bullet}_{M_{d}}={\mathcal{I}}^{\bullet}_{M_{d}}
. Thus,
f|_{M_{d}}
is a Nash function on
M_{d}
. As the graph of
f|_{S_{(d)}}
is the closure in
